On the Bloch–Kato conjecture for GSp ⁡ ( 4 ) \GSp(4)
David Loeffler, Sarah Livia Zerbes

Abstract




Abstract
.
We prove an explicit reciprocity law for the Euler system attached to the spin motive of a genus
2
Siegel modular form. As consequences, we obtain one inclusion of the Iwasawa Main Conjecture for such motives, and the Bloch–Kato conjecture in analytic rank 0 for their critical twists.

Todas las artes tienen en común el esfuerzo de dominar la materia, de reordenar el caos.

—
P. Maurensieg
, Teoría de las sombras

Introduction

0.1.
Aims of this paper

Euler systems are one of the most powerful tools for controlling the cohomology groups of global Galois representations, and hence for proving cases of the two interrelated conjectures linking these groups to values of
L
-functions: the Bloch–Kato conjecture and the Main Conjecture of Iwasawa Theory. More precisely, it follows from work of Kolyvagin, Kato and Rubin that if there exists an Euler system for some Galois representation
V
, and if the bottom class of this Euler system is non-zero, then we obtain a bound on the Selmer group of
V
. So, in order to make progress on the above conjectures, we need to first construct an Euler system for
V
, and then to prove a formula (an
explicit reciprocity law
) relating the localisation of this Euler system at
p
to the values of
L
-functions.

The goal of this paper is to carry out this program for the 4-dimensional spin Galois representations arising from Siegel modular forms for the group
\operatorname{Sp}_{4}(\mathbf{Z})
(of sufficiently regular weights). This builds on earlier work carried out in the paper
[
LSZ17
]
together with Chris Skinner, where we constructed an Euler system for these spin Galois representations. At the time that paper was written, we faced severe conceptual difficulties in proving an explicit reciprocity law for the Euler system; so we could not rule out the possibility that the entire Euler system was zero, and the arithmetic applications given in
op.cit.
were conditional on assuming that the Euler system satisfied an explicit reciprocity law of the expected form.

The main result of this paper is a proof of the missing explicit reciprocity law. This paper can be seen as a sequel to the paper
[
LPSZ19
]
with Skinner and Vincent Pilloni, in which we constructed a
p
-adic
L
-function interpolating critical values of the spin
L
-functions of an automorphic representation of
\GSp_{4}
, using Piatetski–Shapiro’s integral formula for the spin
L
-function
[
PS97
]
, Harris’ interpretation of this integral in terms of coherent cohomology
[
Har04
]
, and Pilloni’s results on
p
-adic interpolation of coherent cohomology (“higher Hida theory”)
[
Pil17
]
.

As a consequence of the explicit reciprocity law, we obtain one inclusion of the Iwasawa Main Conjecture for the spin Galois representation, and the Bloch–Kato conjecture for the analytic rank 0 twists of this Galois representation.

0.2.
Main results of the paper

In order to state the results a little more precisely, we need to introduce some notation. Let
\Pi
be a non-endoscopic, non-CAP, globally generic automorphic representation of
G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
, of weights
(k_{1},k_{2})=(r_{1}+3,r_{2}+3)
with
r_{1},r_{2}\geqslant 0
, and write
W_{\Pi}
for the associated spin Galois representation. We suppose
\Pi
is unramified and Borel-ordinary at
p
. Let
(q,r)
be integers with
0\leqslant q\leqslant r_{2}
and
0\leqslant r\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
.

In
[
LSZ17
]
we defined cohomology classes

z^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{M,m}\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{m}}),W_{\Pi}^{*}(-q)),

for
m\geqslant 0
and
M\geqslant 1
not divisible by
p
or the primes of ramification of
\Pi
, satisfying Euler-system norm compatibility conditions as
M
and
m
vary. These classes depend on choices of local data
(w,\underline{\Phi})
at the bad places. The first main result of this paper is to evaluate the image of the
M=1,m=0
class under the Bloch–Kato logarithm map

\log_{\mathrm{BK}}:H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},W_{\Pi}^{*}(-q))\longrightarrow\left(\Fil^{1}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi})\right)^{*},

relating these to non-critical
p
-adic
L
-values:

Theorem A
.

Assume that
r_{2}\geqslant 1
. Then, for a suitable choice of element
\nu\in\Fil^{1}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi})
, we have

\left\langle\nu,\,\log_{\mathrm{BK}}z^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{1,0}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi})}=(\star)\times\mathcal{L}_{p}(\Pi,-1-r_{2}+q,r)\cdot\tilde{Z}(w,\underline{\Phi})

for an explicit non-zero factor
(\star)
. Here,
\mathcal{L}_{p}(\Pi,\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})
denotes the 2-variable spin
p
-adic
L
-function constructed in
[
LPSZ19
]
, and
\tilde{Z}(w,\underline{\Phi})
is a product of local zeta integrals (which is non-zero for a suitable choice of
(w,\underline{\Phi})
).

The proof of this theorem occupies the majority of the paper. Note that we do not require
\Pi
to have level 1 here. (The assumption that
\Pi
be Borel-ordinary can also be relaxed to the weaker condition of Klingen-ordinarity; the stronger ordinarity condition is needed to construct classes
z^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{M,m}
for all
m
without introducing unbounded denominators, but only the
m=0
class appears in the theorem. Klingen-ordinarity is vital, however, since without it we cannot even define the
p
-adic
L
-function.)

Remark 1
.

The proof of Theorem A relies on an assertion concerning the rigid cohomology of strata in the special fibre of a
\GSp_{4}
Shimura variety, formulated as
0.10.2.3
below. A proof of this statement has been found by Lan and Skinner and will appear in a forthcoming paper.

Our second main result is a considerable strengthening of Theorem A, under far more restrictive hypotheses. We now assume that
\Pi
satisfies the conditions of Theorem A, and also the following extra conditions:

•

\Pi
has level one (i.e.
\Pi_{\ell}
is unramified for all finite primes
\ell
);

•

r_{1}-r_{2}\geqslant 6
;

•

for some (and hence every)
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
-stable lattice
T
in
W_{\Pi}^{\star}
, and every Dirichlet character
\chi
of prime-to-
p
conductor, Rubin’s “big image” condition
\operatorname{Hyp}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{p^{\infty}}),T(\chi))
holds (cf.
[
LSZ17
, Assumption 11.1.2]
).

The condition
r_{1}-r_{2}\geqslant 6
implies that the
p
-adic
L
-function factors as the product of two copies of a single-variable
p
-adic
L
-function
\mathcal{L}_{p}(\Pi,\mathbf{j})
.

Theorem B
.

There exists an Euler system for
W_{\Pi}^{*}(-1-r_{2})
, whose image under the Perrin-Riou cyclotomic regulator map is the
p
-adic
L
-function
\mathcal{L}_{p}(\Pi,\mathbf{j})
.

Note that this result relies on Theorem A not only for
\Pi
itself, but also for all the classical specialisations of a
p
-adic family passing through
\Pi
. From Theorem B we readily obtain the following two arithmetic applications. The first gives one inclusion in the Iwasawa main conjecture for
W_{\Pi}^{*}
, up to inverting
p
:

Theorem C
.

Let
V=W_{\Pi}^{*}(-1-r_{2})
, and denote by
\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{\infty},V)
the Nekovář   Selmer complex, with the unramified local conditions at
\ell\neq p
and the Greenberg-type local condition at
p
determined by the Klingen-ordinarity of
\Pi
. Assume that the above conditions are satisfied. Then the module
\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{p^{\infty}}),V)
is torsion over the Iwasawa algebra, and its characteristic ideal divides the
p
-adic
L
-function
\mathcal{L}_{p}(\Pi,\mathbf{j})
.

Note that this is a divisibility of ideals in
\Lambda_{L}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
where
\Gamma\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and
L
is a finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
. The module
\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\Iw}
can also be interpreted more classically as the base-extension to
L
of the Pontryagin dual of a Selmer group attached to a representation of cofinite type over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, linking up with more classical formulations of an Iwasawa main conjecture; see
Proposition

0.18.1.2
below.

Our second application is to the Bloch–Kato conjecture:

Theorem D
.

Assume that the above conditions are satisfied. Let
0\leqslant j\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
, and let
\rho
be a finite-order character of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. If
L\left(\Pi\otimes\rho,\frac{1-r_{1}+r_{2}}{2}+j\right)\neq 0
, then
H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{Q},V(-j-\rho))=0
.

Future plans

In future work, we hope to relax the conditions on the weight and tame level of
\Pi
, and to consider deformation towards singular (non-cohomological) weights. This will have applications to the Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture for modular abelian surfaces.

More generally, the strategy that we developed for the proof of the explicit reciprocity law should be applicable to many other cases where an Euler system has been constructed, but where the relevant
L
-values cannot be expressed purely in terms of degree zero coherent cohomology, as they can in previously-studied cases such as
\GL_{2}\times\GL_{2}
. The case of the Euler system attached to the Asai representation of quadratic Hilbert modular forms is currently being studied by one of our PhD students.

0.3.
Strategy

We outline the overall strategy used in the proofs of Theorems A and B.

0.3.1.
Strategy for Theorem A

(1)

Using equivariance properties of the Lemma–Flach classes as the test data
(w,\underline{\Phi})
vary, we show that it suffices to prove the theorem for
(w,\underline{\Phi})
which have a certain specific type at
p
(“Klingen-type test data”). For these Klingen test data at
p
, the left-hand side of
A
can be expressed as a pairing (
0.6.2
) between a de Rham cohomology class
\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}
of Klingen parahoric level which is an ordinary eigenvector for the Hecke operator
U_{2,\Kl}^{\prime}
, and the logarithm of an étale class which is the pushforward of a pair of
\GL_{2}
Eisenstein classes along a certain “twisted” embedding
\iota_{\Delta}
of Shimura varieties
Y_{H,\Delta}\hookrightarrow Y_{G,\Kl}
. (This embedding is also used in the definition of the
p
-adic
L
-function
\mathcal{L}_{p}(\Pi)
in
[
LPSZ19
]
.)

(2)

We express the pairing (
0.6.2
) using the “Nekovář–Nizioł finite-polynomial cohomology” of
[
BLZ16
]
(a variant of the syntomic cohomology introduced in
[
NN16
]
). This gives a formalism of Abel–Jacobi maps, allowing us to write (
0.6.2
) as a cup-product between the pushforward of the syntomic
\GL_{2}\times\GL_{2}
Eisenstein class and a class
\eta_{\NNfp,q,-D}
which is a lifting of
\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}
to Nekovář–Nizioł fp-cohomology; see (
0.7.8
). By a new comparison result due to Ertl–Yamada
[
EY19
]
, this is equivalent to a pairing in log–rigid finite-polynomial cohomology (c.f. Proposition
0.10.4.2
).

(3)

Using the Eigenspace Vanishing Conjecture
0.10.2.3
, we show that the pairing factors through a pairing in the rigid fp-cohomology of the p-rank
\geqslant 1
locus
Y_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
, which only “sees” the restriction of the Eisenstein class to the ordinary locus
Y_{H,\Delta}^{\mathrm{ord}}\subseteq Y_{H,\Delta}
(Theorem
0.10.4.6
). This allows us to use the explicit description, due to Bannai–Kings, of the syntomic Eisenstein classes for
\GL_{2}
over the ordinary locus, in terms of non-classical
p
-adic Eisenstein series.

(4)

To actually compute the pairing of Theorem
0.10.4.6
and relate it to
p
-adic
L
-functions, we need an explicit description of the lifting
\eta_{\NNfp,q,-D}
in terms of classes in the coherent cohomology groups studied in Pilloni’s higher Hida theory. This is the most novel part of the construction, and relies on two new ingredients:

•

A theory of rigid and coherent cohomology with
partial compact support
(see Section
0.8
), i.e. with compact support towards some of the closed strata of the special fibre but not towards others. This allows us to bypass the lack of a Frobenius lifting over
Y_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
, by instead working in the cohomology of the ordinary locus
Y_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}
with an appropriate partial support condition.

•

A new spectral sequence, the
Poznań spectral sequence
(Proposition
0.12.2.4
), relating syntomic (or finite-polynomial) cohomology to the mapping fibre of a polynomial in Frobenius over coherent cohomology. This spectral sequence can be seen as a syntomic analogue of the Frölicher spectral sequence relating de Rham and coherent cohomology.

(5)

We now use an identity relating Hecke operators on
G
and on
H
(Proposition
0.12.5.1
) to simplify the coherent cohomology pairing until we are left with only two terms. Both can be recognised as special values at
\mathbf{j}=0
of
p
-adic measures
\mathscr{L}_{1}(\mathbf{j})
and
\mathscr{L}_{2}(\mathbf{j})
, which are very similar, but
a priori
not quite identical, to the
p
-adic
L
-function of
[
LPSZ19
]
– the difference lies in the choice of local data at
p
. By a local zeta-integral computation, we show that at critical values the measure
\mathscr{L}_{1}
has the same interpolating property as the
p
-adic
L
-function, while the measure
\mathscr{L}_{2}
is identically 0. So the regulator is given by the value of
\mathscr{L}_{1}
at
\mathbf{j}=0
, and this corresponds to a non-critical value of the
p
-adic
L
-function. This completes the proof of Theorem A.

Remark 2
.

•

The first glimpse of the Poznań spectral sequence is
[
BK10
, Proposition A.16]
, which represents elements of the first syntomic cohomology group of a smooth pair in terms of classes in coherent cohomology.

•

The Hecke operator identity of
Proposition

0.12.5.1
is an analogue in the present setting of an identity of Hecke operators for
\GL_{2}\times\GL_{2}
which occurs in the proofs of regulator formulae for Rankin–Selberg convolutions; see the proof of
[
KLZ20
, Lemma 6.4.6]
.

•

The idea of (coherent) cohomology with partial compact support was discovered independently by Pilloni (
[
Pil20
]
).

0.3.2.
Strategy for Theorem B

In order to deduce Theorem B from Theorem A, we use variation in a
p
-adic family. We use
p
-adic families of “Siegel type” – one-parameter families in which we vary
(r_{1},r_{2})

p
-adically while keeping the difference
r_{1}-r_{2}
fixed.

If we knew that the
p
-adic
L
-function of
[
LPSZ19
]
extended to Siegel-type families, and that there existed a
p
-adic Eichler–Shimura isomorphism for such families, interpolating the period isomorphisms for the middle steps of the Hodge filtration at each classical specialisation (analogous to the results of Ohta
[
Oht95
]
and Andreatta–Iovita–Stevens for
\GL_{2}

[
AIS15
]
), then Theorem B would be a virtually immediate consequence of Theorem A (we sketch the argument in
Section

0.17.4
). However, these ingredients do not seem to be available yet for
\GSp_{4}
; both statements seem to be accessible for Klingen-type families (with
r_{1}
varying but
r_{2}
fixed), but the case of Siegel-type families is less clear.

Instead, we use an alternative argument, relying on the existence of a
p
-adic
L
-function for functorial liftings to
\GL_{4}
of Siegel-type families, a refinement of the results of
[
DJR18
]
. (Details of this will appear in forthcoming work.) A careful analysis of the relation between this new “Betti”
p
-adic
L
-function for the family, and the “coherent”
p
-adic
L
-function of
[
LPSZ19
]
for its classical specialisations, leads to the conclusion that the image of the Euler system for
\Pi
under the Perrin-Riou regulator must be a scalar multiple of the
p
-adic
L
-function.

What remains to be proven is that this scalar factor is not zero. We show that if the ratio of periods giving this scalar factor degenerates to 0, then this happens not only for the Euler system class over the cyclotomic extension
\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})
, but simultaneously for the classes over
\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}})
for all auxiliary conductors
M
. This gives an Euler system satisfying a stronger-than-expected local condition at
p
, and an result due to Mazur and Rubin shows that in fact no such Euler system can exist, contradicting our assumption. This completes the proof of Theorem B.

0.4.
Acknowledgements

We are also very grateful to Fabrizio Andreatta, Antonio Cauchi, Veronika Ertl, Elmar Große-Klönne, Kiran Kedlaya, Kai-Wen Lan, Chris Lazda, Bernhard Le Stum, Vincent Pilloni, Joaquin Rodrigues, Christopher Skinner, Chris Williams and Kazuki Yamada for many helpful discussions.

One of the key ingredients for proving the main results of this paper is Pilloni’s Higher Hida Theory, which we learnt about during the workshop
Motives, Galois Representations and Cohomology Around the Langlands Program
at the IAS in November 2017. We would like to express our gratitude to the organizers for the invitation.

We discovered a new spectral sequence which is crucial for the proof of the explicit reciprocity law while attending Gregorz Banaszak’s birthday conference in Poznań in September 2018. We are very grateful to the organizers for the invitation to such an inspiring event.

Part of the work on this paper was carried out while we were visiting the Bernoulli Centre, Princeton University, the Morningside Centre and the Isaac Newton Institute. We would like to thank these institutions for their hospitality.

0.5.
Conventions

In this paper,
p
is a prime. As in
[
LSZ17
, §2]
,
G
denotes the symplectic group
\GSp_{4}
,
P_{\Sieg}
and
P_{\Kl}
denote its standard Siegel and Klingen parabolic subgroups, and
H
denotes the subgroup
\GL_{2}\times_{\GL_{1}}\GL_{2}
.

Step 1: The problem, and a first reduction

0.6.
Euler systems for Siegel automorphic representations

Here we briefly recall the Galois cohomology classes constructed in
[
LSZ17
]
and formulate the problem we are trying to solve, which is to evaluate the images of these classes under the Bloch–Kato logarithm at
p
. We then explain a reduction step (the first of many), expressing these quantities as cup-products in the variant of finite-polynomial cohomology for
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
-varieties introduced in
[
NN16
]
and
[
BLZ16
]
.

0.6.1.
Automorphic representations

Definition 0.6.1.1
.

Let
(\Pi^{H},\Pi^{W})
be a pair of non-endoscopic, non-CAP automorphic representations of
G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Q}})
with the same finite part
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}
, as in
[
LSZ17
]
, with
\Pi^{W}
globally generic, discrete series at
\infty
of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})=(r_{1}+3,r_{2}+3)
for some integers
r_{1}\geqslant r_{2}\geqslant 0
.

Note 0.6.1.2
.

Recall that
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}
denotes the “arithmetically normalised” twist
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}=\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}\otimes\|\cdot\|^{-(r_{1}+r_{2})/2}
, which is definable over a number field
E
.

0.6.2.
Hecke parameters at
p

Let
p
be a prime such that
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}
is unramified at
p
.

Definition 0.6.2.1
.

We write
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
for the Hecke parameters of
\Pi_{p}^{\prime}
, and
P_{p}(X)
for the polynomial
(1-\alpha X)\dots(1-\delta X)
.

The Hecke parameters are algebraic integers in
\bar{E}
, and are well-defined up to the action of the Weyl group. Extending
E
if necessary, we may assume that they lie in
E
itself. They all have complex absolute value
p^{w/2}
, where
w\coloneqq r_{1}+r_{2}+3
, and they satisfy
\alpha\delta=\beta\gamma=p^{w}\chi_{\Pi}(p)
, where
\chi_{\Pi}(p)
is a root of unity.

Note 0.6.2.2
.

The polynomial
P_{p}(X)
is consistent with the notation of Theorem 10.1.3 of
[
LSZ17
]
, and in particular the local
L
-factor is given by

L(\Pi_{p},s-\tfrac{w}{2})=P_{p}\left(p^{-s}\right)^{-1}=\left[\left(1-\tfrac{\alpha}{p^{s}}\right)\dots\right]^{-1}.

Note, however, that the Hecke parameters here are
not
quite the same as the
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
in
[
LPSZ19
]
Proposition 3.2, which are the Hecke parameters of a different twist of
\Pi_{p}
.

We shall fix an embedding
E\hookrightarrow L\subset\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
, where
L
is a finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, and let
v_{p}
be the valuation on
L
such that
v_{p}(p)=1
. If we order
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
in such a way that
v_{p}(\alpha)\leqslant\dots\leqslant v_{p}(\delta)
(which is always possible using the action of the Weyl group), then we have the valuation estimates

v_{p}(\alpha)\geqslant 0,\qquad v_{p}(\alpha\beta)\geqslant r_{2}+1.

Remark 0.6.2.3
.

These inequalities correspond to the fact that the Newton polygon of the
p
-adic Galois representation associated to
\Pi
lies on or above the Hodge polygon; see
Proposition

0.6.7.1
below.

Definition 0.6.2.4
.

We say
\Pi
is
Siegel ordinary
at
p
if
v_{p}(\alpha)=0
, and
Klingen ordinary
at
p
if
v_{p}(\alpha\beta)=r_{2}+1
(and
Borel ordinary
if it is both Siegel and Klingen ordinary).

Lemma 0.6.2.5
.

If
\Pi
is Klingen-ordinary at
p
, then none of
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
has the form
p^{n}\zeta
with
n\in\mathbf{Z}
and
\zeta
a root of unity. (In other words, Assumption 11.1.1 of
[
LSZ17
]
is satisfied.)

Proof.

Since all of the Hecke parameters are Weil numbers of weight
w
, it follows that if one of the parameters has this form, then
w
must be even and
n=w/2
. In particular, this parameter has
p
-adic valuation
w/2
. However, if
\Pi
is Klingen-ordinary then
\alpha,\beta
have valuations at most
r_{2}+1\leqslant\tfrac{w-1}{2}
, and
\gamma,\delta
have valuations at least
r_{1}+2\geqslant\tfrac{w+1}{2}
, so none can have valuation
(r_{1}+r_{2}+3)/2
.
∎

0.6.3.
Shimura varieties

Definition 0.6.3.1
.

For
U\subset G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
a sufficiently small level, and
K
a field of characteristic 0, let
Y_{G}(U)_{K}
denote the base-extension to
K
of the canonical
\mathbf{Q}
-model of the level
U
Shimura variety for
G
. We denote by
Y_{G,K}
the pro-variety
\varprojlim_{U}Y_{G}(U)_{K}
.

Definition 0.6.3.2
.

For each algebraic representation
V
of
G
, let
\mathcal{V}
denote the

G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-equivariant relative Chow motive over
Y_{G,\mathbf{Q}}
associated to
V
via Ancona’s functor, as in
[
LSZ17
, §6.2]
.

Any relative Chow motive over
Y_{G}(U)_{\mathbf{Q}}
gives rise to an object of Voevodsky’s triangulated category of geometrical motives over
\mathbf{Q}
(via pushforward along the structure map
Y_{G}(U)_{\mathbf{Q}}\to\Spec\mathbf{Q}
). Hence we can make sense of motivic cohomology
H^{*}_{\mot}(Y_{G}(U)_{\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{V})
. We use the same symbol
\mathcal{V}
for the
p
-adic étale realisation of this motive, which is a locally constant étale sheaf of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
-vector spaces on
Y_{G}(U)_{\mathbf{Q}}
(with a natural extension to the canonical integral model
Y_{G}(U)_{\mathbf{Z}[1/N]}
if
U
is unramified outside
N
).

Remark 0.6.3.3

(“Liebermann’s trick”)
.

Explicitly, suppose that
V
is a direct factor of
W^{\otimes n}(m)
, where
W
is the defining representation of
G
. Then
H^{i}_{\mot}(Y_{G}(U)_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}},\mathcal{V})
is a direct summand of
H^{i+n}(A^{n},\mathbf{Q}(m))
, where
A
is the universal abelian scheme over
Y_{\mathscr{G}}
. We have

H^{\star}_{\mot}(Y_{\mathscr{G}},\mathcal{V}_{\mot})=e_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot H_{\mot}^{\star+n}(A^{n},\mathbf{Q}(m))

for some projector
e_{\mathcal{V}}
.

0.6.4.
Galois representations and modular parametrisations

Taking
V=V(r_{1},r_{2};r_{1}+r_{2})
in the notation of
[
LPSZ19
]
, the
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}
-isotypical part of
H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},c}(Y_{G}(U)_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}},\mathcal{V})\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}L
is isomorphic to the sum of
\dim\left(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{U}\right)
copies of a 4-dimensional
L
-linear Galois representation
W_{\Pi}
(uniquely determined up to isomorphism), whose semisimplification is the representation
\rho_{\Pi,p}
associated to
\Pi

[
LSZ17
, §§10.1-10.2]
. This is characterised by the relation

\det\left(1-X\rho_{\Pi,p}(\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1})\right)=P_{\ell}(X)

for good primes
\ell
, where
\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}
is an arithmetic Frobenius at
\ell
.

We shall
fix
a choice of representation
W_{\Pi}
in this isomorphism class, as follows. We have assumed that
\Pi^{W}
is globally generic. So
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}
is
a fortiori
everywhere locally generic; that is, for any nontrivial additive character
\psi
of
\mathbf{A}/\mathbf{Q}
, there exists a unique space of
\mathbf{C}
-valued functions on
G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
which transforms via
\psi
under left-translation by
N(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
and which is isomorphic to
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}
as a
G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-representation. We denote this space by
\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})
, and
\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})_{E}
the subspace of Whittaker functions which are
defined over
E
in the sense of
[
LPSZ19
, Definition 10.2]
. This gives a canonical model of
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}
as an
E
-linear representation, so we can define
\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})_{F}
for any extension
F
of
E
by base-extension.

Definition 0.6.4.1
.

With the above notations, we set

W_{\Pi}=\Hom_{L[G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})]}\Big(\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})_{L},H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},c}(Y_{G,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}},\mathcal{V})_{L}\Big).

This is a 4-dimensional
L
-linear representation of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}/\mathbf{Q})
which is a canonical representative of the isomorphism class
\rho_{\Pi,p}
. We therefore obtain a canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})_{L}=\Hom_{\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}/\mathbf{Q})}\Big(W_{\Pi},H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},c}(Y_{G,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}},\mathcal{V})_{L}[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}]\Big).

Remark 0.6.4.2
.

One can give a slightly more down-to-earth definition of
W_{\Pi}
using the newvector theory of
[
RS07
]
and
[
Oka19
]
. This allows us to choose a level
U(\Pi)
such that
\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})^{U(\Pi)}
is one-dimensional and has a canonical basis vector
w^{0}
normalised such that
w^{0}(1)=1
. Then evaluating at
w^{0}
identifies
W_{\Pi}
with the
U(\Pi)
-invariants in
H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},c}(Y_{G,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}},\mathcal{V})_{L}[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}]
. In particular, if
\Pi
has level 1, then we can take
U(\Pi)=G(\widehat{\mathbf{Z}})
; this special case will be needed when we consider variation in
p
-adic families in §
0.17
below.

There is a canonical duality of
G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})\times\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}/\mathbf{Q})
-representations

\big\langle\!\!\big\langle-,-\big\rangle\!\!\big\rangle_{G}:\Big(H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},c}(Y_{G,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}},\mathcal{V})\Big)\times\Big(H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(Y_{G,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}(3))\Big)

given at level
U
by
\operatorname{vol}(U)\cdot\langle-,-\rangle_{Y_{G}(U)}
, where
\langle-,-\rangle_{Y_{G}(U)}
is the Poincaré duality pairing on the cohomology of
Y_{G}(U)
, and “vol” denotes volume with respect to some fixed choice of Haar measure on
G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
. Via this pairing, we can interpret elements of
\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})
as homomorphisms of Galois representations
H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(Y_{G,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}(3))\to W_{\Pi}^{*}
, i.e. as
modular parametrisations
of
W_{\Pi}^{*}
in the sense of
[
LSZ17
, §10.4]
.

0.6.5.
The Lemma–Eisenstein map

We recall the following branching law for representations of
G
restricted to
H=\GL_{2}\times_{\GL_{1}}\GL_{2}
:

Proposition 0.6.5.1
.

Let
q,r
be integers with
0\leqslant q\leqslant r_{2}
and
0\leqslant r\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
. We set

(t_{1},t_{2})=(r_{1}-q-r,r_{2}-q+r),

so that
t_{1},t_{2}\geqslant 0
. Then we have

V|_{H}=\bigoplus_{q,r}\left(\Sym^{t_{1}}\boxtimes\Sym^{t_{2}}\right)\otimes\det{}^{q}.

Remark 0.6.5.2
.

As
(q,r)
vary, the pair
(t_{1},t_{2})
takes all values satisfying
r_{1}-r_{2}\leqslant t_{1}+t_{2}\leqslant r_{1}+r_{2}
,
|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
and
t_{1}+t_{2}=r_{1}+r_{2}\bmod 2
(cf.
[
LPSZ19
, Proposition 6.4]
). The advantage of the
(q,r)
parametrisation is that the bounds are easier to write down.

Notation 0.6.5.3
.

We let
\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2})
denote the space of
\mathbf{Q}
-valued Schwartz functions on
\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}
, with
\GL_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})\times\GL_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
acting by right-translation, satisfying the following vanishing property: if
c=0
, then
\Phi((0,0)\times-)
vanishes identically, and if
d=0
, then
\Phi(-\times(0,0))
vanishes identically.

In
[
LSZ17
, §8.3]
, we defined a map

\mathcal{LE}^{[q,r]}:\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2})\otimes\mathcal{H}(G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}))\to H^{4}_{\mot}(Y_{G,\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}(3-q))

satisfying a certain equivariance property, where
\mathcal{H}(-)
denotes the Hecke algebra with
\mathbf{Q}
-coefficients. Denote by
\mathcal{LE}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}^{[q,r]}
the composite of this map with the étale realisation.

Note 0.6.5.4
.

Since the
L
-packet of
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}
does not contribute to cohomology in degrees
\neq 3
, we can project this into the
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime\vee}
isotypical component and apply the étale Abel-Jacobi map (cf.
[
LSZ17
, §10.3]
) to obtain a homomorphism

\mathcal{LE}^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}:\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2})\otimes\mathcal{H}(G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}))\to H^{1}\left(\mathbf{Q},H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(Y_{G,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}(3-q))[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime\vee}]\right).

Choosing a vector
w\in\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})
and applying the pairing
\big\langle\!\!\big\langle-,-\big\rangle\!\!\big\rangle_{G}
, we obtain a homomorphism

\big\langle\!\!\big\langle w,\mathcal{LE}^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(-)\big\rangle\!\!\big\rangle_{G}:\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2})\otimes\mathcal{H}(G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}))\to H^{1}(\mathbf{Q},W_{\Pi}^{*}(-q)).

For our purposes, it is simpler to work with the bilinear form corresponding to
\LE_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}^{[q,r]}
under Frobenius reciprocity as in
[
LSZ17
, §3.9]
:

Definition 0.6.5.5
.

Let

z^{[\Pi,q,r]}:\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})_{L}\times\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2})\to H^{1}(\mathbf{Q},W^{*}_{\Pi}(-q))\otimes\|\det\|^{-q}

be the unique
H(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-equivariant map such that

\big\langle\!\!\big\langle w,\mathcal{LE}^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(\underline{\Phi}\otimes\xi)\big\rangle\!\!\big\rangle_{G}=z^{[\Pi,q,r]}(\xi\cdot w,\underline{\Phi})

for all
\mathcal{W}\in\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})
,
\underline{\Phi}\in\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2})
, and
\xi\in\mathcal{H}(G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}))
.

Unravelling the notation we have the following:

Proposition 0.6.5.6
.

Suppose that
w\in\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})
. Then for any open compact
U\subset G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
such that
U
fixes
w
and
V=U\cap H(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
fixes
\underline{\Phi}
, we have

z^{[\Pi,q,r]}(w,\underline{\Phi})=\operatorname{vol}(V)\cdot\Big\langle w,\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{U,\star}\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},\underline{\Phi}})\Big\rangle_{Y_{G}(U)}

Here
\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{U,*}
denotes pushforward along
Y_{H}(V)\to Y_{G}(U)
, and
\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},\underline{\Phi}}
denotes the étale realisation of the motivic Eisenstein class
\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Mot,\underline{\Phi}}
(c.f.
[
KLZ20
, §4.1]
). ∎

Note 0.6.5.7
.

Note that this shows that
z^{[\Pi,q,r]}
is independent of the choice of Haar measure on
G
(although not on
H
).

0.6.6.
Zeta-integrals

We shall now make the dependence on
w
and
\underline{\Phi}
a little more precise, as follows. We choose a pair of
E
-valued finite-order characters
\underline{\chi}=(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})
of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\times}/\mathbf{Q}_{>0}^{\times}
, with
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}=\chi_{\Pi}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2};\underline{\chi})
denote the
(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})
-eigenspace for the action of
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}^{\times}\times\hat{\mathbf{Z}}^{\times}
on
\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2})
, and similarly without
{(0)}
.

Note 0.6.6.1
.

If
\underline{\Phi}\in\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\underline{\chi})
, then
z^{[\Pi,q,r]}(w,\underline{\Phi})
vanishes for trivial reasons unless
(-1)^{q+r}
is equal to the common value
(-1)^{r_{1}}\chi_{1}(-1)=(-1)^{r_{2}}\chi_{2}(-1)
.

In the Appendix (see Equation (
0.19.1
)), we use the leading terms of local zeta-integrals to define, for each prime
\ell
, a non-zero bilinear map

\widetilde{Z}_{\ell}:\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\ell}^{\prime})_{E}\otimes\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}^{2}\times\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}^{2};\underline{\chi}_{\ell}^{-1}\right)_{E}\to E,

which is
H(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
-equivariant up to a twist by
|\det|^{-q}
. If
\ell
is unramified and
(w_{\ell},\underline{\Phi}_{\ell})
are the spherical vectors, then
\widetilde{Z}_{\ell}(w_{\ell},\underline{\Phi}_{\ell})=1
, so we may define a global bilinear map

\widetilde{Z}:\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})_{E}\otimes\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2};\underline{\chi}_{\ell}^{-1}\right)_{E}\to E

as the tensor product of the
\widetilde{Z}_{\ell}
over all (finite) primes
\ell
.

Theorem 0.6.6.2
.

Suppose that the following condition is satisfied:

•

If
r_{1}=r_{2}=q
, then for every ramified prime
\ell
such that
L(\Pi_{\ell},s)
has a pole at
s=-\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\Pi_{\ell}
is not of Sally–Tadic type IIIa or IVa, the local characters
\chi_{1,\ell}
and
\chi_{2,\ell}
are both non-trivial.

Then there exists a class

z^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\mathrm{can}}(\underline{\chi})\in H^{1}(\mathbf{Q},W_{\Pi}^{*}(-q))

such that for all
(w,\underline{\Phi})\in\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})_{L}\times\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\underline{\chi}^{-1})_{L}
, we have

z^{[\Pi,q,r]}\left(w,\underline{\Phi}\right)=z^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\mathrm{can}}(\underline{\chi})\cdot\widetilde{Z}(w,\underline{\Phi}).

Note that we do not require the
\chi_{i}
to be ramified at
\ell
, only that they are not identically 1 on
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}^{\times}
. We are most interested in the case where
(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})=(\chi_{\Pi},\mathrm{id})
, and in this case we omit
\underline{\chi}
from the notation and write simply
z^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\mathrm{can}}
.

As the proof of Theorem
0.6.6.2
requires methods rather far removed from the main body of this paper, it will be postponed until the appendix; see
Section

0.19
below. Note also that the proof relies on forthcoming work in preparation by Emory and Takeda if the character
\chi_{\Pi}
is non-trivial.

Remark 0.6.6.3
.

The theorem shows that although the Euler system classes of
[
LSZ17
]
depend on choices of local data at the bad primes, these choices are essentially unimportant, since different choices of local data give the same cohomology class up to scaling factors (modulo some minor issues in the case
r_{1}=r_{2}=q
). However, the dependence on the choice of
r
and
\underline{\chi}
is much less clear. We expect, of course, that the classes for different choices of these parameters are still proportional to each other, and we shall prove this in many cases later in this paper as a consequence of our global results on Selmer groups; but this proportionality seems to be a rather deep result, and does not admit a purely local, representation-theoretic proof.

0.6.7.
Exponential maps and regulators

Recall the following result:

Proposition 0.6.7.1
.

The representation
W_{\Pi}|_{G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}}
is crystalline. The eigenvalues of
\varphi
on
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi})
are the Hecke parameters
\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\}
of
Section

0.6.2
, and the jumps in its Hodge fitration are at
\{0,r_{2}+1,r_{1}+2,r_{1}+r_{2}+3\}
.∎

Lemma 0.6.7.2
.

For all
0\leqslant q\leqslant r_{2}
, we have the following:

(a)

The operators
1-\varphi
and
1-p\varphi
are bijective on
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi}^{*}(-q))
.

(b)

The Bloch–Kato
H^{1}_{\mathrm{e}}
,
H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}
and
H^{1}_{\mathrm{g}}
subspaces of
H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},W_{\Pi}^{*}(-q))
coincide.

(c)

The Bloch–Kato exponential map

\exp:\frac{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi}^{*})}{\Fil^{-q}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi}^{*})}\to H^{1}_{\mathrm{e}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},W_{\Pi}^{*}(-q))

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

It is well known that (a) implies (b) and (c). The assertion (a) amounts to claiming that

\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\}\cap\{1,p,\dots,p^{r_{2}+1}\}=\varnothing.

However, all elements in the first set have Archimedean absolute value
p^{(r_{1}+r_{2}+3)/2}
, and since
r_{1}\geqslant r_{2}
, we have
(r_{1}+r_{2}+3)/2>r_{2}+1
.
∎

Since the localisation at
p
of the class
z^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\mathrm{can}}
is in
H^{1}_{\mathrm{g}}
(by
[
NN16
, Theorem B]
), it is also in
H^{1}_{\mathrm{e}}
. Letting
\log
denote the inverse of the Bloch–Kato exponential, we may define

\log\left(z^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\mathrm{can}}\right)\in\frac{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi}^{*})}{\Fil^{-q}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi}^{*})}=\left(\Fil^{1}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi})\right)^{*}.

Note that the target of this map is 3-dimensional (and independent of
q
in this range).

Assumption 0.6.7.3
.

We assume henceforth that
\Pi
is Klingen-ordinary at
p
.

It follows that there is a distinguished pair of Hecke parameters
(\alpha,\beta)
of minimal valuation, corresponding to a 2-dimensional subspace

\mathcal{N}=\ker\left[(1-\tfrac{\varphi}{\alpha})(1-\tfrac{\varphi}{\beta})\right]\subset\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi}).

Note 0.6.7.4
.

From weak admissibility, we see that
\mathcal{N}\cap\Fil^{1}
must have dimension exactly 1, and that it surjects onto the 1-dimensional graded piece
\Fil^{1}/\Fil^{r_{2}+2}
.

Definition 0.6.7.5
.

Let
\nu
be a basis of the 1-dimensional
L
-vector space
\frac{\Fil^{1}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi})}{\Fil^{r_{2}+2}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi})}
, and let
\nu_{\mathrm{dR}}
denote its unique lifting to
\mathcal{N}\cap\Fil^{1}
.

We can now formulate the key problem treated in this paper:

Problem
: For
(w,\underline{\Phi})\in\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime})_{L}\times\mathcal{S}_{(0)}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\underline{\chi}^{-1})_{L}
, compute the quantity

\operatorname{Reg}_{\nu}^{[\Pi,q,r]}(w,\underline{\Phi})\coloneqq\left\langle\nu_{\mathrm{dR}},\log\left(z^{[\Pi,q,r]}(w,\underline{\Phi})\right)\right\rangle_{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi})}\in L.

Remark 0.6.7.6
.

If the hypotheses of Theorem
0.6.6.2
hold (for
\underline{\chi}=(\chi_{\Pi},1)
), we can formulate this independently of
(w,\underline{\Phi})
; if we define
\operatorname{Reg}_{\nu,\mathrm{can}}^{[\Pi,q,r]}\coloneqq\left\langle\nu_{\mathrm{dR}},\log\left(z^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\mathrm{can}}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi})}
, then we have

\operatorname{Reg}_{\nu}^{[\Pi,q,r]}(w,\underline{\Phi})=\widetilde{Z}(w,\underline{\Phi})\cdot\operatorname{Reg}_{\nu,\mathrm{can}}^{[\Pi,q,r]}\quad\forall\ (w,\underline{\Phi}),

so it suffices to determine the single constant
\operatorname{Reg}_{\nu,\mathrm{can}}^{[\Pi,q,r]}
.

0.6.8.
Periods and
p
-adic
L
-functions

We shall relate the regulator
\operatorname{Reg}_{\nu}^{[\Pi,q,r]}
to the
p
-adic
L
-functions of
[
LPSZ19
]
; so let us briefly recall the construction of
op.cit.
(and slightly refine it by paying closer attention to the periods involved).

Recall that we have chosen a basis vector
\nu
of the 1-dimensional
L
-vector space
\frac{\Fil^{1}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi})}{\Fil^{r_{2}+2}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi})}
. This space is canonically the base-extension to
L
of an
E
-vector space, namely
\Hom_{E[G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})]}\Big(\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}),H^{2}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}})\Big)
, where
H^{2}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}})
denotes the unique copy of
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}
inside a coherent
H^{2}
of
Y_{G,E}
, as in
[
LPSZ19
, §5.2]
.

Definition 0.6.8.1
.

Let
\Omega_{p}(\Pi,\nu)
be any element of
L^{\times}
such that
\nu^{\mathrm{alg}}\coloneqq\Omega_{p}(\Pi,\nu)^{-1}\cdot\nu
is
E
-rational.

We can, of course, choose
\nu
such that
\Omega_{p}(\Pi,\nu)
is 1, but it is convenient to allow more general
\nu
in order to allow variation in
p
-adic families later in this paper.

Proposition 0.6.8.2
.

Let
\tau
be the minimal
K_{\infty}
-type of the discrete series representation
\Pi_{\infty}^{W}
. Then there is a constant
\Omega_{\infty}(\Pi,\nu)\in\mathbf{C}^{\times}
such that the composite map

\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}})_{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{\ \nu^{\alg}\ }H^{2}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}})_{\mathbf{C}}\longrightarrow\Hom_{K_{\infty}}(\tau,\Pi^{W})\longrightarrow\Hom_{K_{\infty}}(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{W}(\Pi)_{\mathbf{C}})

maps
w
to
\Omega^{W}(\Pi,\nu)^{-1}\cdot w\otimes w_{\infty}
, where
w_{\infty}\in\Hom_{K_{\infty}}(\tau,\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\infty}^{W}))
is the vector of standard Whittaker functions at
\infty
(cf.
[
LPSZ19
, §10.2]
).

Here the map
H^{2}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}})_{\mathbf{C}}\longrightarrow\Hom_{K_{\infty}}(\tau,\Pi^{W})
is the comparison isomorphism of Harris and Su relating coherent cohomology to automorphic forms, and the final arrow is given by the global Whittaker transform
\Pi^{W}\to\mathcal{W}(\Pi^{W})
. Since all of these maps are
G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-equivariant bijections, and
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}
is irreducible, it is clear that the composite is multiplication by a nonzero scalar.

Remark 0.6.8.3
.

The quantities
\Omega_{p}(\Pi,\nu)
and
\Omega_{\infty}(\Pi,\nu)
are each only determined modulo
E^{\times}
, but the ratio
\Omega_{p}(\Pi,\nu)^{-1}\otimes\Omega_{\infty}(\Pi,\nu)\in L\otimes_{E}\mathbf{C}
is well-defined (once
\nu
is chosen).

Theorem 0.6.8.4
.

Suppose
r_{2}\geqslant 1
. Then there exists a
p
-adic measure
\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi)\in\Lambda_{L}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
whose evaluation at
(a_{1}+\rho_{1},a_{2}+\rho_{2})
, for
a_{i}
integers with
0\leqslant a_{1},a_{2}\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
and
\rho_{i}
finite-order characters such that

(-1)^{a_{1}+a_{2}}\rho_{1}(-1)\rho_{2}(-1)=-1,

satisfies

\frac{\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi,a_{1}+\rho_{1},a_{2}+\rho_{2})}{\Omega_{p}(\Pi,\nu)}=R_{p}(\Pi,\rho_{1},a_{1})R_{p}(\Pi,\rho_{2},a_{2})\cdot\frac{\Lambda(\Pi\otimes\rho_{1},\tfrac{1-r_{1}+r_{2}}{2}+a_{1})\Lambda(\Pi\otimes\rho_{2},\tfrac{1-r_{1}+r_{2}}{2}+a_{2})}{\Omega_{\infty}(\Pi,\nu)}.

See
[
LPSZ19
, Proposition 10.3]
for further details, including the definition of the factors
R_{p}(\Pi,\rho,a)
. Here
\Lambda(\Pi\otimes\rho,s)
denotes the completed
L
-function (including its Archimedean
\Gamma
-factors). Note that the interpolating property was only proved in
op.cit.
under the assumption that
a_{1}\geqslant a_{2}
, but by comparing interpolating properties at points with
a_{1}=a_{2}
, one sees easily that
\mathcal{L}_{p}(\Pi,\mathbf{j}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{1})=\mathcal{L}_{p}(\Pi,\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})
, so this condition can be removed.

We can now give a precise statement of the theorem we shall prove:

Theorem 0.6.8.5

(
A
)
.

For any
q,r
with
0\leqslant q\leqslant r_{2}
,
0\leqslant r\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
, and
q+r=r_{2}\bmod 2
, we have

(0.6.1)

\operatorname{Reg}^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\nu}(w,\underline{\Phi})=\frac{(-2)^{q}(-1)^{r_{2}-q+1}(r_{2}-q)!}{\mathcal{E}_{p}(\Pi,q)\mathcal{E}_{p}(\Pi,r_{2}+1+r)}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi,-1-r_{2}+q,r)\cdot\widetilde{Z}(w,\underline{\Phi}),

where
\mathcal{E}_{p}(\Pi,n)\coloneqq\left(1-\tfrac{p^{n}}{\alpha}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{p^{n}}{\beta}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\gamma}{p^{n+1}}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\delta}{p^{n+1}}\right)
(which is nonzero for all
n\in\mathbf{Z}
by Lemma
0.6.2.5
).

Note 0.6.8.6
.

(a)

The factor
\mathcal{E}_{p}(\Pi,n)
agrees (up to a sign) with
R_{p}(\Pi,\mathrm{id},-1-r_{2}+n)
; that is, the Euler factors relating
\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}
to the regulator
\mathrm{Reg}_{\nu}^{[\Pi,q,r]}
in the “geometric” range are formally the same as those relating it to complex
L
-values in the “critical” range.

(b)

If the hypotheses of Theorem
0.6.6.2
hold (for
\underline{\chi}=(\chi_{\Pi},1)
), the dependence on
(w,\underline{\Phi})
cancels out and we can write the conclusion simply as

\operatorname{Reg}^{[\Pi,q,r]}_{\nu,\mathrm{can}}=\frac{(-2)^{q}(-1)^{r_{2}-q+1}(r_{2}-q)!}{\mathcal{E}_{p}(\Pi,q)\mathcal{E}_{p}(\Pi,r_{2}+1+r)}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi,-1-r_{2}+q,r).

(c)

If
r_{1}-r_{2}>0
or Hypothesis 10.5 of
[
LPSZ19
]
holds, then
\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi,\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})
factors as a product of a function of
j_{1}
and a function of
j_{2}
. However, our proof of the theorem will not directly “see” this finer decomposition.

0.6.9.
Test data at
p

Proposition 0.6.9.1
.

Let
w_{p,0}\in\mathcal{W}(\Pi^{\prime}_{p})
and
\underline{\Phi}_{p,0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{2}\times\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{2})
be a choice of test data at
p
such that
\widetilde{Z}_{p}(w_{p,0},\underline{\Phi}_{p,0})\neq 0
. Then
Eq.

0.6.1
holds for all test data
(w,\underline{\Phi})
if and only if it holds for
(w^{p}w_{p,0},\underline{\Phi}^{p}\underline{\Phi}_{p,0})
for all prime-to-
p
test data
(w^{p},\underline{\Phi}^{p})
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\Pi_{p}
is unramified and tempered, so all poles of
L(\Pi_{p},s)
have real part 0, and in particular there is no pole at
s=-\tfrac{1}{2}
; so the local analogue of
Theorem

0.6.6.2
at
p
holds unconditionally (see
[
LSZ17
, §3.8]
).
∎

Definition 0.6.9.2
.

•

Let
\gamma_{p}\in G(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
be any matrix whose first column is
(1,1,0,0)^{T}
.

•

Let
w_{p,\Kl}\in\mathcal{W}(\Pi^{\prime}_{p})
denote the normalised
U_{2,\Kl}^{\prime}
-eigenvector at level
\Kl(p)
defined in (
0.20.1
) below.

•

Let
\Phi_{\crit}
be the Schwartz function on
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{2}
defined in
Section

0.15.2
, and
\underline{\Phi}_{p,\Kl}=\Phi_{\crit}\times\Phi_{\crit}
.

We refer to the pair
(\gamma_{p}\cdot w_{p,\Kl},\underline{\Phi}_{p,\Kl})
as
Klingen test data
.

We shall evaluate
\widetilde{Z}_{p}(\gamma_{p}\cdot w_{p,\Kl},\underline{\Phi}_{p,\Kl})
in
Section

0.20.2
below; the result is

\widetilde{Z}_{p}(\gamma_{p}\cdot w_{p,\Kl},\underline{\Phi}_{p,\Kl})=\frac{\mathcal{E}(\Pi,q)\mathcal{E}(\Pi,r_{2}+1+r)}{\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p^{1+q}}\right)}.

In particular, it is nonzero; so it suffices to prove Theorem
0.6.8.5
for test data which is of Klingen type at
p
. For data of this form, the right-hand side of (
0.6.1
) can be written as

\frac{(-2)^{q}(-1)^{r_{2}-q+1}(r_{2}-q)!}{\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p^{1+q}}\right)}\cdot\widetilde{Z}^{p}(w^{p},\underline{\Phi}^{p})\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi,-1-r_{2}+q,r).

The left-hand side of
Eq.

0.6.1
for data of this form can be written explicitly using
Proposition

0.6.5.6
. Let us choose an open compact
U^{p}
such
U^{p}
fixes
w^{p}
and
U^{p}\cap H(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})=V^{p}
fixes
\underline{\Phi}^{p}
.

Notation 0.6.9.3
.

Write
Y_{G,\Kl,\mathbf{Q}}
for the
G
-Shimura variety of level
U^{p}\Kl(p)
, and
Y_{H,\Delta,\mathbf{Q}}
for the
H
-Shimura variety of level
V^{p}K_{p,\Delta}
, where

K_{p,\Delta}=\left\{h\in H(\mathbf{Z}_{p}):\,h=\left(\begin{pmatrix}x&\star\\
&\star\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}x&\star\\
&\star\end{pmatrix}\right)\pmod{p}\quad\text{for some $x$}\right\}.

Write
X_{G,\Kl,\mathbf{Q}}
and
X_{H,\Delta,\mathbf{Q}}
for toroidal compactifications, where the rational polyhedral cone decompositions are chosen as in
[
LPSZ19
, §2.2.4]
.

Remark 0.6.9.4
.

We will define integral models over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
of these Shimura in Section
0.10
.

We have
\gamma_{p}^{-1}K_{p,\Delta}\gamma_{p}\subset\Kl(p)
, so as in
[
LPSZ19
, §4.1]
,
\gamma_{p}
gives a finite morphism of compactified Shiura varieties

\iota_{\Delta}:X_{H,\Delta,\mathbf{Q}}\to X_{G,\Kl,\mathbf{Q}},

which restricts to a closed embedding
Y_{H,\Delta,\mathbf{Q}}\rightarrow Y_{G,\Kl,\mathbf{Q}}
of the uncompactified Shimura varieties. Hence there is a pushforward map
\iota_{\Delta,\star}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}
on étale cohomology; and we have

\left\langle\log z^{[\Pi,q,r]}(w,\underline{\Phi}),\nu_{\mathrm{dR}}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi})}=\operatorname{vol}(V)\left\langle\left(\log\circ\pr_{\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime\vee}}\circ\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta,*}\right)(\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},\Phi}),\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}\right\rangle_{Y_{G}(U)},

where
\eta
denotes the
U_{2,\Kl}^{\prime}
-eigenvector
\nu(w_{p,\Kl}w^{p})
, and
\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}=\nu_{\mathrm{dR}}(w_{p,\Kl}w^{p})
its lifting to
\Fil^{1}\cap\ker[(1-\varphi/\alpha)(1-\varphi/\beta)]
as in
Definition

0.6.7.5
.

We now derive a corresponding formula for the
p
-adic
L
-function. Given
\underline{\Phi}^{p}
, the construction of
[
LPSZ19
]
§7.4 gives a 2-parameter
p
-adic family of Eisenstein series on
H
, which we denote simply by
\mathcal{E}(\underline{\Phi}^{p})
. Then the
p
-adic interpolation theory of
op.cit.
allows us to make sense of
\iota_{\Delta,\star}\left(\mathcal{E}(\underline{\Phi}^{p})\right)
as a measure taking values in
H^{1}
of the
p
-rank
\geqslant 1
locus, and hence to define a measure

\left\langle\eta,\iota_{\Delta,\star}\left(\mathcal{E}(\underline{\Phi}^{p})\right)\right\rangle_{X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}}\in\Lambda_{L}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

This cup product depends on the choice of the level group
U
, but this can be eliminated by renormalising by
\operatorname{vol}V
. From the construction of the
p
-adic
L
-function, we have the following explicit formula:

Proposition 0.6.9.5
.

For
(q,r)
as above, the value of the measure

\vol(V)\cdot\Big\langle\iota_{\Delta,\star}\left(\mathcal{E}(\underline{\Phi}^{p})\right),\eta\Big\rangle_{X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}}

at
(-1-r_{2}+q,r)
is
\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi,-1-r_{2}+q,r)\cdot\widetilde{Z}^{p}(w^{p},\underline{\Phi}^{p})
.∎

Summarising the above discussion, we have the following:

Proposition 0.6.9.6
.

The formula of (
0.6.1
) is equivalent to the following assertion:

For all prime-to-
p
levels
U^{p}
, all
\underline{\Phi}^{p}
stable under
U^{p}\cap H
, and all
\eta\in H^{2}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}})^{U^{p}\Kl(p)}[U_{2,\Kl}=\frac{\alpha\beta}{p^{r_{2}+1}}]
, we have

(0.6.2)

\left\langle\left(\log\circ\pr_{\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime\vee}}\circ\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta,\star}\right)(\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},\underline{\Phi}^{p}\underline{\Phi}_{\Kl}}),\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}\right\rangle_{Y_{G,\Kl}}=\frac{(-2)^{q}(-1)^{r_{2}-q+1}(r_{2}-q)!}{\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p^{1+q}}\right)}\cdot\Big\langle\iota_{\Delta,\star}\left(\mathcal{E}(\underline{\Phi}^{p})\right),\eta\Big\rangle_{X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}}.

It is this formula we shall actually prove.

0.7.
Finite-polynomial cohomology and Abel–Jacobi maps

We briefly recall some geometric formalism from
[
NN16
]
and
[
BLZ16
]
, which we shall use to give formulae for the Abel–Jacobi map of étale cohomology. In this section we shall only consider varieties over
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
; integral models (over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
) will enter the picture later, when we start to make computations.

0.7.1.
P-adic Hodge theory

We recall some constructions from
p
-adic Hodge theory and Galois cohomology; see
[
NN16
, §2D]
and
[
BLZ16
, §1]
for further details.

Filtered modules

Let
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}
denote the maximal unramified extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
.

Definition 0.7.1.1
.

A
filtered
(\varphi,N,G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}})
-module
is a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}
-vector space
D
equipped with the following structures:

•

an
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}
-semilinear Frobenius
\varphi
;

•

an
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}
-linear monodromy operator
N
satisfying
N\varphi=p\varphi N
;

•

an
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}
-semilinear action of
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
commuting with
\varphi
and
N
, such that every
v\in D
is fixed by some open subgroup;

•

a decreasing
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
-linear filtration
\Fil^{\bullet}
on

D_{\mathrm{dR}}\coloneqq\left(D\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}}\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}\right)^{G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}}.

We write
D_{\st}\coloneqq D^{G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}}
and
D_{\mathrm{cris}}\coloneqq D^{(G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}},N=0)}
.

Fontaine’s functor
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}
gives an equivalence of categories between potentially semistable
p
-adic representations of
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
and the subcategory of
weakly admissible
filtered
(\varphi,N,G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}})
-modules. If
D=\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)
, then we have
D_{\st}=\mathbf{D}_{\st}(V)
,
D_{\mathrm{cris}}=\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)
, and
D_{\mathrm{dR}}=\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)
(hence the notation).

Notation 0.7.1.2
.

For
n\in\mathbf{Z}
, let
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}(n)
denote the filtered
(\varphi,N,G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}})
-module whose underlying vector space is
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}
, with
N=0
and the
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
-action being the obvious one, but taking
\varphi=p^{-n}\sigma
where
\sigma
is the native arithmetic Frobenius of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}
, and the filtration concentrated in degree
-n
.

Clearly we have
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}(n)=\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n))
, by identifying
1\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}
with the basis vector
t^{-n}\otimes e_{n}\in\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{cris}}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)
.

The semistable
P
-complex

Definition 0.7.1.3
.

Let
P\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}[t]
be a polynomial with constant term 1, and
D
a filtered
(\varphi,N,G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}})
-module. Define
H^{i}_{\mathrm{st},P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},D)
to be the
i
-th cohomology group of the complex

C_{\st,P}(D)\coloneqq\left[D_{\st}\rTo D_{\st}\oplus D_{\st}\oplus\tfrac{D_{\mathrm{dR}}}{\Fil^{0}}\rTo D_{\st}\right],

where the maps are given by

x\mapsto\left(P(\varphi)x,\,Nx,\,x\bmod\Fil^{0}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(u,v,w)\mapsto Nu-P(p\varphi)v.

If
P(t)=1-t
, then we omit it and write simply
H^{i}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},D)
etc.

Note 0.7.1.4
.

More generally, it will sometimes be convenient to extend the definitions to the case when
P
is a polynomial in
\mathcal{R}[t]
, where
\mathcal{R}
is a commutative
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
-subalgebra of the
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
-endomorphism algebra of
V
.

If
P\mid Q
then we have a natural map of complexes
C_{\st,P}(D)\to C_{\st,Q}(D)
which is the identity in degree 0. There are also products

C^{i}_{\st,P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},D)\otimes C^{j}_{\st,Q}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},E)\to C^{i+j}_{\st,P\star Q}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},D\otimes E),

well-defined up to homotopy, where
P\star Q
is the convolution product (the polynomial whose roots are the pairwise products of those of
P
and
Q
).

Galois cohomology

If
V
is a potentially semistable
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
-representation, then
C_{\st}(\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V))
is the
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
-invariants of a complex of
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
-modules that is quasi-isomorphic to
V
. This gives rise to boundary maps

(0.7.1)

H^{i}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V))\to H^{i}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V),

which are isomorphisms for
i=0
and injective for
i=1
.

Definition 0.7.1.5
.

The
semistable Bloch–Kato exponential
is the map
\exp_{\st,V}:H^{1}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V))\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)
given by (
0.7.1
) for
i=1
. Its image is the Bloch–Kato subspace
H^{1}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)
.

This terminology is justified by the fact that the composition

\frac{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)}{\Fil^{0}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)}\rTo H^{1}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V))\rTo_{\cong}^{\exp_{\st,V}}H^{1}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)

is the usual Bloch–Kato exponential map
\exp_{V}
, with image
H^{1}_{\mathrm{e}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)\subseteq H^{1}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)
.

Notation 0.7.1.6
.

We say a filtered
(\varphi,N,G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}})
-module
D
is
convenient
if it is crystalline (i.e.
D_{\mathrm{cris}}=D_{\mathrm{dR}}
) and
1-\varphi
and
1-p\varphi
are bijective on
D_{\mathrm{cris}}
. We say a crystalline
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
-representation
V
is convenient if
D=\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)
is convenient.

Note 0.7.1.7
.

A filtered
(\varphi,N,G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}})
-module
D
is convenient if and only if so is
D^{*}(1)
.

If
D
is convenient, then
H^{i}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},D)=0
for
i\neq 1
, and the natural map
D_{\mathrm{dR}}/\Fil^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}\to H^{1}_{\mathrm{st}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},D)
is an isomorphism. In particular, for a convenient Galois representation
V
we have
H^{1}_{\mathrm{e}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)=H^{1}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)
and
\exp_{\st,V}
is identified with
\exp_{V}
.

Traces and duality

Lemma 0.7.1.8
.

If
P(1/p)\neq 0
then there is a canonical map

\tr_{\st,P}:H^{1}_{\st,P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}(1))\to\mathbf{Q}_{p}

given by mapping
(x,y,z)\in Z^{1}(C_{\st,P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}(1)))
to
z-P(\tfrac{\sigma}{p})^{-1}x
. If
P(1)\neq 0
this map is an isomorphism.

If
P\mid Q
are two polynomials with
Q(1/p)\neq 0
, then the trace maps for
P
and
Q
are compatible with the change-of-polynomial maps.

Proof.

Immediate from the definitions.
∎

Corollary 0.7.1.9
.

Suppose
D
is convenient. Then, for any
P
satisfying the conditions of the lemma, the pairing given by

H^{0}_{\st,P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},D)\times H^{1}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},D^{*}(1))\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\st,P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}(1))\xrightarrow{\tr_{\st,P}}\mathbf{Q}_{p}

is the restriction to
H^{0}_{\st,P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},D)=\left(D_{\mathrm{cris}}^{P(\varphi)=0}\cap\Fil^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}\right)
of the natural duality pairing
\left(\Fil^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}\right)\times\left(\frac{D_{\mathrm{dR}}^{*}(1)}{\Fil^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}^{*}(1)}\right)\to\mathbf{Q}_{p}
.

0.7.2.
Nekovář–Nizioł cohomology

Let
X
be any
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
-variety, and let
n\in\mathbf{Z}
. Then Nekovář–Nizioł
[
NN16
]
define
R\Gamma_{\NNsyn}(X,n)
and
R\Gamma_{\NNsyn,c}(X,n)
.
This cohomology theory is a Bloch–Ogus theory (Appendix B in
op. cit.
), so it has all of the good functorial properties one expects, such as cup-products, pullbacks, pushforward maps, etc.

More generally, we can define groups
R\Gamma_{\NNfp}(X,n,P)
and
R\Gamma_{\NNfp,c}(X,n,P)
for any polynomial
P
as above, with the case
P(t)=1-t
recovering the theory of
[
NN16
]
; see
[
BLZ16
]
for this generalisation.

By construction, these cohomology theories satisfy the following
descent spectral sequence
:

Proposition 0.7.2.1
.

There exists a spectral sequence

(0.7.2)

{}^{\mathrm{NN}}E_{2}^{ij}=H^{i}_{\st,P}\!\Bigg(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}\Big(H^{j}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}\big(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)\big)\Big)\Bigg)\Rightarrow H^{i+j}_{\NNfp}(X,n,P),

compatible with cup-products and change-of-polynomial maps (and similarly for the compactly-supported variant).

If
X
is smooth of pure dimension
d
, then the étale cohomology of
X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}
vanishes in degrees
>2d
, and there is a
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
-equivariant trace map
H^{2d}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},c}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(d+1))\to\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1)
; so the edge map of this spectral sequence, combined with
Lemma

0.7.1.8
, gives a canonical trace map

(0.7.3)

\tr_{\NNfp,X,P}:H^{2d+1}_{\NNfp,c}(X,d+1,P)\to H^{1}_{\st,P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{nr}}(1))\to\mathbf{Q}_{p},\

for any polynomial
P
such that
P(1/p)\neq 0
.

Theorem 0.7.2.2
.

For all
r\geqslant 0
, there is a natural map

\mathrm{comp}:H^{i}_{\NNsyn}(X,n)\rTo H^{i}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(X,\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n))

which is functorial in
X
and fits into the commutative diagram

\begin{diagram}

and there is a morphism of spectral sequences
{}^{\mathrm{NN}}E_{r}^{ij}\rightarrow{}^{\text{{\'{e}t}}}E_{r}^{ij}
, compatible with
\mathrm{comp}
on the abutment, which is given on the
E_{2}
page by the maps (
0.7.1
). Here,
{}^{\text{{\'{e}t}}}E_{r}^{ij}
denotes the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence

H^{i}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},H^{j}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)))\Rightarrow H^{i+j}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(X,\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)).

Proof.

This is Theorem A of
[
NN16
]
.
∎

0.7.3.
Formalism of Abel–Jacobi maps

Let
X
be a smooth equidimensional
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
-variety of dimension
d
, as before. Recall the following definition:

Definition 0.7.3.1
.

A class in
H^{i}_{\mot}(X,n)
is said to be
homologically trivial
if it is in the kernel of the edge map

H^{i}_{\mot}(X,n)\to H^{0}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},H^{i-1}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)))

induced by the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence. We denote this kernel by
H^{i}_{\mot}(X,n)_{0}
.

Since
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
has cohomological dimension 2, the spectral sequence gives a natural map, the
étale Abel–Jacobi map
,

\operatorname{AJ}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}:H^{i}_{\mot}(X,n)_{0}\to H^{1}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{p},H^{i-1}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n))\right).

Note 0.7.3.2
.

From Theorem 3.2.2, we have

\operatorname{AJ}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}=\exp_{\st}\circ\operatorname{AJ}_{\syn},

where

\operatorname{AJ}_{\syn}:H^{i}_{\mot}(X,n)_{0}\to H^{1}_{\st}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}\left(H^{i-1}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)\right)\right)

is the map given by the spectral sequence
{}^{\mathrm{NN}}E_{r}^{ij}
.

In particular, the map
\operatorname{AJ}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}
takes values in
H^{1}_{\mathrm{g}}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{p},H^{i-1}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n))\right)
(c.f.
[
NN16
, Theorem B]
).

Let
W
be a subspace of
\left[H^{i-1}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n))\right]^{*}(1)=H^{2d+1-i}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},c}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(d+1-n))
as a
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
-representation, and suppose
W
is convenient. Then
W^{*}(1)
is naturally a quotient of
H^{i-1}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n))
, so we have a projection map
\pr_{W^{*}(1)}
.
Moreover, the natural map
\frac{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W^{*}(1))}{\Fil^{0}}\to H^{1}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},W^{*}(1))
is an isomorphism; we write
\log_{W^{*}(1)}
for its inverse.

Notation 0.7.3.3
.

Write
\AJ_{W^{*}(1)}
for the morphism

\operatorname{AJ}_{W^{*}(1)}\coloneqq\log_{W^{*}(1)}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{W^{*}(1)}\circ\operatorname{AJ}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}:H^{i}_{\mot}(X,n)_{0}\to\frac{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}({W^{*}(1)})}{\Fil^{0}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}({W^{*}(1)})}=\left[\Fil^{0}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W)\right]^{*}.

The canonical pairing

\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathrm{dR},W}:\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W)\times\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W^{*}(1))\to\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1))\cong\mathbf{Q}_{p}

identifies the target of
\AJ_{W^{*}(1)}
with the dual of
\Fil^{0}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W)
.

Proposition 0.7.3.4
.

Let
\eta\in\Fil^{0}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W)
, and let
P
be a polynomial with constant term 1 such that
P(\varphi)(\eta)=0
and
P(1/p)\neq 0
.

Then, for any
x\in H^{i}_{\mot}(X,\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n))_{0}
, we have

\langle\operatorname{AJ}_{W}(x),\eta\rangle_{\mathrm{dR},W}=\langle r_{\syn}(x),\,\tilde{\eta}\rangle_{\NNfp,X,P}

where
\tilde{\eta}
is any class in
H^{2d+1-i}_{\NNfp,c}(X,d+1-n,P)
whose image in
H^{0}_{\st,P}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{p},H^{2d+1-i}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},c}(X_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(d+1-n))\right)
is
\eta
.

Proof.

Since the syntomic descent spectral sequence is compatible with products, we have

\langle r_{\syn}(x),\,\tilde{\eta}\rangle_{\NNfp,X,P}=\tr_{\st,P}\left(\AJ_{\syn}(x)\cup\eta\right).

Since
\eta\in H^{0}_{\st,P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},W)
, this pairing factors through the projection of
\AJ_{\syn}(x)
to
H^{1}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(W^{*}(1)))
, which is by construction
\AJ_{W}(x)
. By Corollary
0.7.1.9
, the pairing between
H^{1}_{\st}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(W^{*}(1)))
and
H^{0}_{\st,P}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(W))
is simply the de Rham duality pairing.
∎

0.7.4.
Pushforward and pullback

We can now use the functorial properties of
\NNfp
cohomology to compute the right-hand side. More precisely, let
\iota:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a finite morphism of smooth
K
-varieties, of codimension
c
. Then there are pushforward maps (c.f.
[
Dég08
]
)

H^{i-2c}_{\mot}(Z,r-c)\to H^{i}_{\mot}(X,r)

and similarly for
H^{*}_{\NNsyn}
and
H^{*}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}
; and these are compatible with the maps
r_{\syn}
,
r_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}
, and
\operatorname{comp}
appearing in the diagram of Theorem
0.7.2.2
.

Proposition 0.7.4.1
.

For
z\in H^{i-2c}_{\mot}(Z,r-c)
, we have

\operatorname{tr}_{\NNfp,X,P}\left(\iota_{*}(z)\cup\tilde{w}\right)=\operatorname{tr}_{\NNfp,Z,P}\left(z\cup\iota^{*}(\tilde{w})\right).

Proof.

This follows from the adjunction formula relating pushforward and pullback.
∎

0.7.5.
Coefficients

If
X=Y_{G}(U)
as in Section
0.6.3
, then we can use Liebermann’s trick
0.6.3.3
to define cohomology with coefficients in algebraic representations
V
and to obtain versions of the spectral sequence (
0.7.2
) and of Theorem
0.7.2.2
with coefficients. In particular, the composition of the cup product and (
0.7.3
) defines a pairing

\langle\quad,\quad\rangle_{\NNfp,X,P}:H^{i}_{\NNsyn}(X,\mathcal{V},r)\times H^{2d+1-i}_{\NNfp,c}(X,\mathcal{V}^{\vee},d+1-r,P)\rTo\mathbf{Q}_{p}

for any
P
with
P(1/p)\neq 0
.

For cohomology with coefficients, the formalism of pushforward and pullback maps works as follows: suppose that we have a closed immersion of PEL Shimura varieties
\iota:Y_{H}(U^{\prime})\hookrightarrow Y_{G}(U)
of codimension
d
, for some reductive group
H
and
U^{\prime}=U\cap H(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
. Assume that the closed immersion extends to the toroidal compactifications. Let
W
be a direct summand of
V|_{H}
. We then obtain

(0.7.4)

\displaystyle(\iota_{U}^{\mathcal{W}})_{*}:\,H^{\star}_{\NNsyn}(Y_{H}(U^{\prime}),\mathcal{W},r)

\displaystyle\rTo H^{\star+2d}_{\NNsyn}(Y_{G}(U),\mathcal{V},r+c),

(0.7.5)

\displaystyle(\iota_{U}^{\mathcal{W}})^{*}:\,H^{\star}_{\NNsyn,c}(Y_{G}(U),\mathcal{V},r)

\displaystyle\rTo H^{\star}_{\NNsyn,c}(Y_{H}(U^{\prime}),\mathcal{W},r)

for all
r\in\mathbf{Z}
.

Moreover, these maps are adjoint with respect to this pairing: suppose that
W
is a direct summand of
V|_{H}
, so
W^{\vee}
is a direct summand of
(V^{\vee})|_{H}
. Then for all
x\in H^{i-2c}_{\NNsyn}(Y_{H}(U^{\prime}),\mathcal{W},r-c)
and
y\in H^{2d+1-i}_{\NNfp,c}(X,\mathcal{V}^{\vee},d+1-r,P)
, we have

\left\langle(\iota_{U}^{\mathcal{W}})_{*}(x),\,y\right\rangle_{\NNfp,Y_{G}(U),P}=\left\langle x,\,(\iota_{U}^{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}})^{*}(y)\right\rangle_{\NNfp,Y_{H}(U^{\prime}),P}.

0.7.6.
Reduction: Step 1

Write
D
for the boundary divisor
X_{\Kl,\mathbf{Q}}-Y_{\Kl,\mathbf{Q}}
.

Definition 0.7.6.1
.

Denote by
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{Q}}
the
\GL_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-equivariant relative Chow motive over
Y_{H}
associated to the standard representatioof
\GL_{2}
.

Lemma 0.7.6.2
.

Let
t_{1},\,t_{2}
be as in Proposition
0.6.5.1
, and let
d\geqslant 0
. Then we have pullback and pushforward maps

(0.7.6)

\displaystyle(\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta})_{*}:

\displaystyle\,H^{\star}_{\NNsyn}(Y_{H,\Delta},\Sym^{t_{1}}\mathscr{H}^{\vee}\boxtimes\Sym^{t_{2}}\mathscr{H}^{\vee},d)\rTo H^{\star+2}_{\NNsyn}(Y_{G,\Kl},\mathcal{V}^{\vee},d+1-q),

(0.7.7)

\displaystyle(\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta})^{*}:

\displaystyle\,H^{\star}_{\NNsyn,c}(Y_{G,\Kl},\mathcal{V},d+q)\rTo H^{\star}_{\NNsyn,c}(Y_{H,\Delta},\Sym^{t_{1}}\mathscr{H}\boxtimes\Sym^{t_{2}}\mathscr{H},d).

Proof.

This is an instance of (
0.7.4
) and (
0.7.5
).
∎

Notation 0.7.6.3
.

Write
\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\syn,\underline{\Phi}}
for the image of
\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\mot,\underline{\Phi}}
under
r_{\NNsyn}
.

As in Section
0.6.8
, let
(w,\Phi)
be the product of some arbitrary test data
(w^{p},\Phi^{p})
away from
p
and the Klingen test data at
p
. Shrinking
K^{p}
if necessary, we may assume that
K^{p}
fixes
w^{p}
, and
K_{H}^{p}
fixes
\Phi^{p}
.

Notation 0.7.6.4
.

Write
\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}
for
\eta^{\mathrm{ur}}\in\Fil^{1}H^{3}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{G,\Kl}\langle-D\rangle,\mathcal{V})[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}]
.

Note 0.7.6.5
.

Observe that we can consider
\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}
as an element
\eta_{\mathrm{dR},q}\in\Fil^{1+q}H^{3}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\dots)
, for any
0\leqslant q\leqslant r_{2}
.

Lemma 0.7.6.6
.

Let
0\leqslant n\leqslant r_{2}
. Then there exists a unique lift
\eta_{\NNfp,q,-D}
of
\eta_{\mathrm{dR},q}
to the group
H^{3}_{\NNfp}(X_{G,\Kl},\mathcal{V}(-D),1+q;P_{1+q})[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}]
. Here,
P_{1+q}(T)=\left(1-\frac{p^{1+q}T}{\alpha}\right)\left(1-\frac{p^{1+q}T}{\beta}\right)
.

Proof.

By definition, we have
P(\varphi)\,\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}=0
, so it follows from the definition of fp-cohomology that it will lift to a class

\eta_{\NNfp,q,-D}\in H^{3}_{\NNfp}(X_{\Kl}\langle-D\rangle,\mathcal{V},1+q;P)[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}].

The lift is unique since
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}
does not contribute to cohomology in degrees
\neq 3
.
∎

Notation 0.7.6.7
.

Write
\eta_{\mathrm{dR},q,-D}
for the image of
\eta_{\NNfp,q,-D}
in
H^{3}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{\Kl}\langle-D\rangle,\mathcal{V},1+q)[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}]

\eta_{\mathrm{dR},q,-D}
maps to
\eta_{\mathrm{dR},q}
under the extension-by-0.

We can now make the first reduction of (

1st reduction
: We have

(0.7.8)

\left\langle\left(\log\circ\pr_{\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime\vee}}\circ\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta,\star}\right)(\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},\underline{\Phi}^{p}\underline{\Phi}_{\Kl}}),\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}\right\rangle_{Y_{G,\Kl}}=\left\langle(\iota_{\Delta}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]})_{*}\left(\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\syn,\underline{\Phi}},\,\eta_{\NNfp,q,-D}\right)\right\rangle_{\NNfp,Y_{\Kl}}.
