Y_{H,\Delta}^{\mathrm{ord}}

0.8.
Cohomology with partial support

We recall some basic formalism regarding cohomology of sheaves on rigid spaces, following
[
LS07
]
and
[
GK00
]
, and define variants with “partial compact support”. Let
K
be a finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, with residue field
k
and ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
.

0.8.1.
Frames and tubes

Recall that a
frame
denotes the data of a triple
(X\hookrightarrow Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P})
, where
X
and
Y
are
k
-varieties,
\mathfrak{P}
is a formal
\mathcal{O}
-scheme,
X\hookrightarrow Y
is an open immersion, and
Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
is a closed immersion of
Y
into
\mathfrak{P}
, necessarily factoring through the special fibre
\mathfrak{P}_{0}

[
LS07
, Definition 3.1.5]
.

Note 0.8.1.1
.

We shall always assume
\mathfrak{P}
is an admissible formal scheme, and thus in particular quasi-compact (this is automatically satisfied if
\mathfrak{P}
is the
p
-adic completion of a finite-type flat
\mathcal{O}
-scheme).

Definition 0.8.1.2
.

The frame
(X\hookrightarrow Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P})
is said to be
smooth
if
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
in a neighbourhood of
X
(Definition 3.3.5 of
op.cit.
); it is said to be
proper
if
Y
is proper over
k
(Definition 3.3.10).

The theory is typically only well-behaved for smooth proper frames; note that this does not imply that
X
itself is either smooth or proper.

If
(X\hookrightarrow Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P})
is a frame, then the tube
{\,]}X{[\,}_{\mathfrak{P}}
is an open rigid-analytic subvariety of the analytic generic fibre
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
. We shall henceforth omit the subscript
\mathfrak{P}
if it is clear from context. If
X
is affine and open in
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
, then
{\,]}X{[\,}
is affinoid; it follows that if
X
is any open subvariety of
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
, then
{\,]}X{[\,}
is quasi-compact.

If
X
is not assumed to be open in
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
, then
{\,]}X{[\,}
is no longer quasi-compact. However, it can be written as an increasing union of quasicompacta, the closed tubes
[X]_{\lambda}
of radius
\lambda<1
(which are well-defined if
\lambda>|\varpi_{K}|
).

0.8.2.
Sections with support

Let
V
be any rigid analytic space over
K
, and
T
an admissible open subset of
V
. Then we have a short exact sequence of exact functors on the category of abelian sheaves on
V
,

0\to\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{T}\to\mathrm{id}\to j^{\dagger}_{V-T}\to 0,

and a left-exact sequence of left-exact functors

0\to\underline{\Gamma}_{V-T}\to\mathrm{id}\to h_{*}h^{-1},

where
h
is the inclusion
T\hookrightarrow V
. The second sequence is also exact on the right on injective sheaves, and thus gives an exact triangle of right-derived functors.

Recall that if
X\hookrightarrow Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
is a proper smooth frame, then the rigid cohomology of
X
(with and without compact supports) is defined by

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X)\coloneqq R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}X{[\,}}\,\Omega^{\bullet}_{{\,]}Y{[\,}}\right),\qquad R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},c}(X)\coloneqq R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}X{[\,}}\,\Omega^{\bullet}_{{\,]}Y{[\,}}\right).

See e.g.
[
Ber97
]
or
[
LS07
, Chapter 5]
for further details.

Proposition 0.8.2.1
.

We have
\underline{\Gamma}_{V-T}\circ j^{\dagger}_{V-T}=j^{\dagger}_{V-T}
and
j^{\dagger}_{V-T}\circ\underline{\Gamma}_{V-T}=\underline{\Gamma}_{V-T}
.

Proof.

By definition of
j^{\dagger}_{V-T}
, we have
h^{-1}\circ j^{\dagger}_{V-T}=0
and hence
h_{*}h^{-1}\circ j^{\dagger}_{V-T}=0
. Similarly,
h^{-1}\circ\underline{\Gamma}_{V-T}=0
and hence
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{T}\circ\underline{\Gamma}_{V-T}=h_{!}h^{-1}\circ\underline{\Gamma}_{V-T}=0
. The results now follow from the above exact sequences.
∎

The functors
\underline{\Gamma}
and
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}
are related by the following formula. Let us say that
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
is a
interior subset
if
\{T,V-T^{\prime}\}
is an admissible covering of
V
(i.e.
V-T^{\prime}
is a strict neighbourhood of
V-T
). Then we have

\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{T}(\mathcal{F})=\varinjlim_{T^{\prime}}\ \underline{\Gamma}_{T^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}),

where the limit is over interior subsets
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
. In particular, if
T
and
U
are both admissible open, then there is a natural inclusion
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{T}(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\underline{\Gamma}_{T}(\mathcal{F})
as subsheaves of
\mathcal{F}
, but this is not an equality (except in the trivial case when
\{T,V-T\}
is an admissible covering that disconnects
V
). It seems reasonable to describe
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{T}(\mathcal{F})
as the sections
strictly
supported in
T
.

It is important to note that if
Z\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
is a formal embedding with
\mathfrak{P}
proper, and we take
V=\mathfrak{P}_{K}
and
T={\,]}Z{[\,}
, then the closed tubes
[Z]_{\lambda}
of radius
\lambda<1
are cofinal among interior subsets of
T
, and also among quasi-compact subsets of
T
. So
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Z{[\,}}(\mathcal{F})
is precisely the sections of
\mathcal{F}
supported in a quasicompact subset of
T
.

V

W

U

Figure 1

0.8.3.
Partial compact supports

We shall consider the following setting. We suppose we are given a formal embedding
Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
, with
Y
and
\mathfrak{P}
proper, and an open subvariety
U\subseteq Y
with complement
Z=Y-U
, so that
U\hookrightarrow Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
is a proper frame. Let
V\subseteq Z
be a closed subvariety, and set
W=Z-V
, as in Figure 1. We want to attach a meaning to cohomology of
U
with compact support “towards
V
” or “towards
W
”.

Remark 0.8.3.1
.

Our treatment is strongly motivated by
[
DI87
, §4.2]
, where such a theory is developed for de Rham cohomology in characteristic 0, assuming that
Z
and
W
are smooth normal-crossing divisors, which is the main case of interest. See also
[
Fal89
, §III]
for étale cohomology,
[
Mie09
, §2]
for Hyodo–Kato cohomology, and
[
BD18
]
for Hodge cohomology of varieties over
\mathbf{C}
.

Proposition 0.8.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an abelian sheaf on
{\,]}Y{[\,}
, and let
V^{\prime}
be any closed subvariety of
Y
such that
Z=V\cup V^{\prime}
.

(a)

We have canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\ \underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}\ \underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\ j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}\ j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}.

(b)

There is a natural map

j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\ \underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\to\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\ j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F},

which is an isomorphism away from
]V\cap V^{\prime}[
.

Proof.

(a) As in Prop 5.1.11 of
[
LS07
]
, since
{\,]}V{[\,}
and
]V^{\prime}[
admissibly cover
{\,]}Z{[\,}
we have
j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\circ j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}=j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}
. So, using
Proposition

0.8.2.1
, we have

j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\circ\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}=j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\circ j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\circ\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}=j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}\circ\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}.

Similarly, we have
\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\circ\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}=\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}
and hence

\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\circ j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}=\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\circ\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\circ j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}=\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}\circ j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}.

(b) It suffices to show that the composite map
j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\,\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\to j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\to h_{*}h^{-1}(j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-V^{\prime}{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F})=h_{*}j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}V^{\prime}-(V\cap V^{\prime}){[\,}}(h^{-1}\mathcal{F})
is zero. However, this map factors through
h_{*}h^{-1}(\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\mathcal{F})
which is the zero sheaf.

The pair
\{Y-V,Y-V^{\prime}\}
is an open covering of
Y-(V\cap V^{\prime})
as a
k
-variety, so their tubes admissibly cover
]Y-(V\cap V^{\prime})[
. It is clear that the above map is an isomorphism after restriction to either
]Y-V[
or
]Y-V^{\prime}[
, so we obtain an isomorphism of sheaves over
]Y-(V\cap V^{\prime})[
.
∎

Definition 0.8.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an abelian sheaf on
{\,]}Y{[\,}
.

•

We define
cohomology with compact support towards
V
(recall
V
is closed in
Z
) by

R\Gamma_{cV}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{F})=R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}\ R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\ \mathcal{F}\right).

•

We define
cohomology with compact support towards
W
(recall
W
is open in
Z
) by

R\Gamma_{cW}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{F})=R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}\ j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\ \mathcal{F}\right).

Note that this notation is
a priori
ambiguous, since if both
V
and
W
are closed in
Y
, we have two candidate definitions of
R\Gamma_{cV}(-)
; but in fact the two candidate definitions agree, since if we start from the first definition we have

\displaystyle R\Gamma_{cV}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{F})\coloneqq

\displaystyle R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}\ R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\ \mathcal{F}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-W{[\,}}\ R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\ \mathcal{F}\right)\quad\text{ (by part (a) of the proposition)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\ j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-W{[\,}}\ \mathcal{F}\right)\quad\text{ (by part (b) of the proposition)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}\ j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-W{[\,}}\ \mathcal{F}\right)\quad\text{ (by part (a) of the proposition)}

which is the second definition of
R\Gamma_{cV}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{F})
. (In particular, this applies when one of
V
and
W
is empty, and we conclude that cohomology with compact support towards
\varnothing
, or towards all of
Z
, has the expected meaning.)

Proposition 0.8.3.4
.

We have exact triangles

R\Gamma_{cW}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{F})\to R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},\ j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\right)\to R\Gamma\left({\,]}Z{[\,},\ j_{{\,]}W{[\,}}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{F}|_{{\,]}Z{[\,}})\right)\to[+1]

and

R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},\ \underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Z{[\,}}^{\dagger}\ R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\right)\to R\Gamma\left({\,]}Y{[\,},\ R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\right)\to R\Gamma_{cV}\left({\,]}U{[\,},\,\mathcal{F}\right)\to[+1].

Proof.

By definition we have an exact triangle of complexes of sheaves on
{\,]}Y{[\,}

R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-Z{[\,}}\ j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\to j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\to Rh_{*}h^{-1}\left(j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\right)\to[+1],

where
h:Z\hookrightarrow Y
is the inclusion map. However, since
(Y-V)\cap Z=W
, we have
h^{-1}\left(j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Y-V{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\right)=j^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Z-V{[\,}}(h^{-1}\mathcal{F})
, by Corollary 5.1.15 of
[
LS07
]
. Applying the (triangulated) functor
R\Gamma({\,]}Y{[\,},-)
gives the first triangle. The second is obtained similarly.
∎

Let us note some “naturality” properties of the construction. Firstly, if we fix
Y
and
Z
, and let
J\supseteq J^{\prime}
be two subvarieties of
Z
, then we have natural maps

R\Gamma_{c-J}(U,\mathcal{F})\to R\Gamma_{c-J^{\prime}}(U,\mathcal{F})
if:

•

J
and
J^{\prime}
are both open,

•

J
and
J^{\prime}
are both closed,

•

J
is closed and
J^{\prime}
is open (using
Proposition

0.8.3.2
(b) with
V=J
and
V^{\prime}=Z-J^{\prime}
).

(We do not consider the case when
J
is open and
J^{\prime}
is closed, since we will not need it here.) Secondly, for coherent sheaves we have cup-products

R\Gamma_{cV}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{F})\otimes R\Gamma_{cW}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{G})\to R\Gamma_{cZ}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}),

and these are compatible with the exact triangles of
Proposition

0.8.3.4
. Finally, we have the following compatibility with respect to morphisms of frames:

Proposition 0.8.3.5
.

Let
u:\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{P}
be a morphism of formal schemes over
\mathcal{O}
, and define
Y^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime}
as the preimages of
Y,V,W
etc. Then pullback along
u
induces canonical maps

u^{*}:R\Gamma_{cV}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{F})\to R\Gamma_{cV^{\prime}}({\,]}U^{\prime}{[\,},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

and

u^{*}:R\Gamma_{cW}({\,]}U{[\,},\mathcal{F})\to R\Gamma_{cW^{\prime}}({\,]}U^{\prime}{[\,},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),

compatible with the exact triangles of
Proposition

0.8.3.4
.

Proof.

This is immediate from the compatibility of
j^{\dagger}
and
R\underline{\Gamma}
with pullback.
∎

Remark 0.8.3.6
.

If we start with a variety
Y
and two closed subvarieties
A,B
, and put
Z=A\cup B
, then we are interpreting
R\Gamma({\,]}Y{[\,},j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-B{[\,}}\ R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-A{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F})
as cohomology with compact support towards the closed subvariety
A
of
Z
, and the subtly different group
R\Gamma({\,]}Y{[\,},R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-A{[\,}}\ j^{{\dagger}}_{{\,]}Y-B{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F})
(with the order of the functors interchanged) as cohomology with compact support towards the open subvariety
A-(A\cap B)
of
Z
. These agree if
A\cap B=\varnothing
, but they are genuinely different otherwise (as the special case
A=B
shows). We shall show in
Section

0.8.6
below that they give the same result for the cohomology of the de Rham complex when
A
and
B
intersect transversely.

0.8.4.
Interpretation via dagger spaces

We recall from
[
GK00
]
the category of
dagger spaces
over
K
. Note that if
\mathfrak{P}
is a proper (admissible)
\mathcal{O}
-scheme, and
X
is a locally closed subvariety of
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
, then there is a natural structure of a dagger space on the tube
{\,]}X{[\,}
; we denote this dagger space by
{\,]}X{[\,}^{\dagger}
, and similarly
[X]^{\dagger}_{\lambda}
for the tubes of radius
\lambda<1
.

Non-compact support

Essentially by definition, if
X\hookrightarrow Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
is a proper smooth frame, and
V
any strict neighbourhood of
{\,]}X{[\,}
in
{\,]}Y{[\,}
, then any coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
V
defines a coherent sheaf on
{\,]}X{[\,}^{\dagger}
, and we have

R\Gamma({\,]}X{[\,}^{\dagger},\mathcal{F})=R\Gamma\left(V,j_{{\,]}X{[\,}}^{\dagger}\,\mathcal{F}\right)

(and similarly for hypercohomology of complexes of coherent sheaves).

Compact support

There is also a concept of
compactly-supported cohomology
for coherent sheaves on dagger spaces: see
[
GK00
, §4.3]
. We will need the following computation:

Proposition 0.8.4.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a proper admissible formal
\mathcal{O}
-scheme, and
W
a locally closed subvariety of
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
. Write
W=X\cap Z
with
X
open and
Z
closed. Then we have

R\Gamma_{c}({\,]}W{[\,}^{\dagger},\mathcal{F})=R\Gamma\left(\mathfrak{P}_{K},\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Z{[\,}}\ R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}X{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}\right).

Proof.

We have
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{{\,]}Z{[\,}}\ \underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}X{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}=\varinjlim_{\lambda}\Gamma_{[Z]_{\lambda}}\ \Gamma_{{\,]}X{[\,}}\,\mathcal{F}=\varinjlim_{\lambda}\Gamma_{[W]_{\lambda}}\,\mathcal{F}
, since
[W]_{\lambda}=[Z]_{\lambda}\cap{\,]}X{[\,}
. Applying this to an injective resolution of
\mathcal{F}
gives the result, since
R\Gamma_{c}({\,]}W{[\,},\mathcal{F})=\varinjlim_{\lambda}R\Gamma\left(\mathfrak{P}_{K},R\underline{\Gamma}_{[W]_{\lambda}}\,\mathcal{F}\right)
.
∎

These results allow the triangles of
Proposition

0.8.3.4
to be written in the following more convenient form. Let
(U,V,W)
be as above, and denote the dagger space tubes of these by
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}
. Then there are exact triangles

R\Gamma_{c}\left(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F}\right)\to R\Gamma_{c}\left(\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F}\right)\to R\Gamma_{cV}\left(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{F}\right)\to[+1]

and

R\Gamma_{cW}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{F})\to R\Gamma\left(\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F}\right)\to R\Gamma\left(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F}\right)\to[+1].

Duality

There is a form of Serre duality for
affinoid
dagger spaces
\mathcal{X}
: by choosing an embedding
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}
with
\mathcal{P}
a smooth affinoid, we can define a
dualising complex

\underline{\omega}_{\mathcal{X}}
, which is a perfect complex of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-modules, and for any coherent
\mathcal{F}
, we have a perfect duality of Hausdorff topological vector spaces

H^{i}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})\times\mathbf{H}^{-i}_{c}\left(\mathcal{X},\underline{\operatorname{RHom}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}(\mathcal{F},\underline{\omega}_{\mathcal{X}})\right)\to K,

with both sides being 0 if
i\neq 0
.

Remark 0.8.4.2
.

This is stated in
[
GK00
]
for smooth affinoids, in which case we have of course
\underline{\omega}_{\mathcal{X}}=\Omega^{d}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{K}}[d]
where
d=\dim\mathcal{X}
, but the proof is valid in the generality above. See
[
GK98
, §7.1]
for more details.

Remark 0.8.4.3
.

This Serre duality does
not
seem to extend straightforwardly to non-affinoid spaces. If
\mathcal{X}
is smooth and quasi-compact, and
\{\mathcal{X}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a finite affinoid covering, then we can form Cech complexes representing
R\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
and
R\Gamma_{c}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F}^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{X}})
with respect to this covering. These are complexes of complete locally-convex
K
-vector spaces which are term-wise dual to one another, so we obtain natural pairings between the cohomology groups. However, it is not clear if the differentials in these complexes are strict; so one does not know if these pairings are perfect dualities of topological vector spaces (or even if the induced topologies on the cohomology groups are Hausdorff).

0.8.5.
Finiteness and Poincaré duality

Non-compact support

Theorem 0.8.5.1

(Grosse-Klönne)
.

If
\mathcal{X}
is a dagger space of the form
\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}
, where
\mathcal{U}
is smooth and quasicompact, and
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is a quasicompact open subset, then the cohomology groups
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X})\coloneqq H^{i}(\mathcal{X},\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{X}/K})
are finite-dimensional over
K
for all
i
.

Proof.

This is (a special case of) the main theorem of
[
GK02
]
.
∎

Note 0.8.5.2
.

This implies finite-dimensionality of rigid cohomology, since for a proper smooth frame
X\hookrightarrow Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
, the dagger space
\mathcal{X}=\ {\,]}X{[\,}^{\dagger}
satisfies the hypotheses of
Theorem

0.8.5.1
, and we have
H^{i}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X)=H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X})
.

Compact support

There is also a compactly-supported analogue of this result, and a Poincaré duality theorem; these are straighforward consequence of results of Grosse-Klönne, but curiously do not seem to be explicitly written down in the literature:

Theorem 0.8.5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a smooth dagger space of the form
\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}
with
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}
quasicompact, as in
Theorem

0.8.5.1
, of pure dimension
d
. Then
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{X})
is also finite-dimensional for all
i
, and we have perfect pairings of finite-dimensional vector spaces

H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X})\times H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{X})\to K\quad\text{for $0\leqslant i\leqslant 2d$}.

Proof.

The case of affinoid
\mathcal{X}
is treated in Theorem 4.9 and remark 4.10 of
[
GK00
]
. The case of
\mathcal{X}
quasicompact follows readily from this, using the Cech spectral sequence associated to a finite covering of
\mathcal{X}
by affinoids (since we know that the Cech complex consists of finite-dimensional vector spaces, there are no topological issues to worry about).

We now consider the general case. We can write
\mathcal{X}
as a countable increasing union
\{\mathcal{X}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of quasicompacta. Then we have

H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X})=\varprojlim_{n}H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}),\qquad H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{X})=\varinjlim_{n}H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{X}_{n}).

Since the terms in the two limits are dual to each other, and we know that
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X})
is finite-dimensional, it follows that
H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{X})
is also finite-dimensional and that Poincaré duality holds for
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Partial compact support

Corollary 0.8.5.4
.

If we are given varieties
U,V,W\subseteq\mathfrak{P}_{0}
as in
Section

0.8.3
, with
U
open in
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
smooth, then the cohomology groups of the complexes

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},cV}(\mathcal{U})\coloneqq R\Gamma_{cV}(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{\bullet})\quad\text{and}\quad R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(\mathcal{U})\coloneqq R\Gamma_{cW}(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{\bullet})

are finite-dimensional for all
i
, and there are perfect pairings

H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},cV}(\mathcal{U})\times H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(\mathcal{U})\to K.

Proof.

Rewriting the exact triangles of
Proposition

0.8.3.4
in terms of dagger spaces using
Proposition

0.8.4.1
, as explained above, and taking
\mathcal{F}
to be the rigid-analytic de Rham complex, we have long exact sequences

\dots\to H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(\mathcal{U})\to H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{W})\to H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{W})\to\dots

and

\dots\leftarrow H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},cV}(\mathcal{U})\leftarrow H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{W})\leftarrow H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{W})\leftarrow\dots.

Moreover, there are compatible pairings between the groups in the first row and their neighbours in the second row. By
Theorems

0.8.5.1
and
0.8.5.3
, the middle and right groups on each row are finite-dimensional and the pairings between them are perfect. By induction on
i
we deduce that the groups in the left-hand column are also finite-dimensional and in perfect duality, as required.
∎

A “logarithmic” variant

Sadly the above setting is still not quite general enough, and we shall need to consider yet another possibility. Suppose we have an proper admissible formal
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme
\mathfrak{P}
, a proper closed subvariety
Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}_{0}
, and a decomposition
Y=U\cup V\cup W
as above. We also suppose that
\mathfrak{D}\subseteq\mathfrak{P}
is a simple normal crossing divisor relative to
\operatorname{Spf}\mathcal{O}_{K}
, which intersects transversely with
U
and
W
. We write
\mathcal{P}
for the dagger space generic fibre of
\mathfrak{P}
, and
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}
for the dagger tubes of
U,V,W
respectively.

Notation 0.8.5.5
.

Write
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},cV}(\mathcal{U}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle)
, resp.
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(\mathcal{U}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle)
, for the hypercohomology of
\mathcal{U}
with compact support towards
V
(resp.
W
) of the
logarithmic
de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{P}}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle
. Similarly, we write
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},cV}(\mathcal{U}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle)
for the hypercohomology of the “minus-log” complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{P}}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle\coloneqq\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}^{\bullet}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle(-\mathcal{D})
.

Proposition 0.8.5.6
.

We have perfect pairings of finite-dimensional
K
-vector spaces

H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},cV}(\mathcal{U},\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle)\times H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(\mathcal{U},\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle)\to K.

and

H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},cV}(\mathcal{U},\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle)\times H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(\mathcal{U},\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle)\to K.

Proof.

By the same long exact sequence argument as above, it suffices to prove the proposition inthe special case
\mathcal{W}=\varnothing
, i.e. that

H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{U},\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle)\times H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U},\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle)\to K

and

H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U},\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle)\times H^{2d-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{U},\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle)\to K

are perfect pairings of finite-dimensional spaces. We prove the former; the argument for the latter is identical with the role of compact and non-compact support interchanged.

Let
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
denote the disjoint union of the
n
-fold intersections of components of
\mathcal{D}
, and
\iota^{(n)}:\mathcal{D}^{(n)}\to\mathcal{P}
the natural map. The logarithmic de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{P}}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle
has an increasing filtration, whose
n
-th graded piece is
\iota^{(n)}_{*}\left(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{D}^{(n)}}\right)
. Similarly, the complex
\Omega^{\bullet}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle
has a decreasing filtration, with the same graded pieces; and the logarithmic duality pairing

(0.8.1)

\Omega^{i}_{\mathcal{P}}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle\otimes\Omega^{d-i}_{\mathcal{P}}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle\to\Omega^{d}_{\mathcal{P}}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle=\omega_{\mathcal{P}},

where
\omega_{\mathcal{P}}
is the dualizing sheaf, is compatible with these filtrations, and the pairing it induces on the
n
-th graded piece is the usual (non-logarithmic) duality pairing on each of the
n
-fold intersections.

So we have spectral sequences

E_{1}^{ij}=\mathbf{H}^{j}_{c}\left(\mathcal{U},\iota^{(i)}_{*}\left(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{D}^{(i)}}\right)\right)\Rightarrow H^{i+j}_{\mathrm{dR},c}\left(\mathcal{U}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle\right)

and

{}^{\prime}E_{1}^{-i,j}=\mathbf{H}^{j-2i}\left(\mathcal{U},\iota^{(i)}_{*}\left(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{D}^{(i)}}\right)\right)\Rightarrow H^{-i+j}_{\mathrm{dR}}\left(\mathcal{U}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle\right).

We have
\mathbf{H}^{j}_{c}\left(\mathcal{U},\iota^{(i)}_{*}\left(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{D}^{(i)}}\right)\right)=H^{j}_{\mathrm{dR},c}\left(\mathcal{U}^{(i)}\right)
, where
\mathcal{U}^{(i)}=(\iota^{(i)})^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
, and similarly without compact support. So by Theorem
0.8.5.3
, the spaces
E_{1}^{ij}
and
{}^{\prime}E_{1}^{ij}
are finite-dimensional for all
i,j
(and zero outside a bounded region), and the pairing
E_{1}^{i,j}\times{}^{\prime}E_{1}^{-i,2d-j}\to E_{1}^{0,2d}\cong K
induced by (
0.8.1
) is perfect. Hence the limits of the two spectral sequences are also finite-dimensional and in perfect duality, as required.
∎

0.8.6.
The transversal case

Although we shall not use it in the remainder of the paper, it would be remiss not to point out the following consistency property of the above constructions. For simplicity, we suppose that
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth and proper over
\mathcal{O}
, and
Y=\mathfrak{P}_{0}
. Let
A,B
be two closed subvarieties of
Y
, let
U=Y-A-B
, and let
A^{\circ}=A-(A\cap B)
and
B^{\circ}=B-(A\cap B)
.

Proposition 0.8.6.1
.

If
A
,
B
, and
A\cap B
are smooth, and
\operatorname{codim}_{Y}(A\cap B)=\operatorname{codim}_{Y}(A)+\operatorname{codim}_{Y}(B)
, then there are isomorphisms

H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c-A}({\,]}U{[\,})\cong H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c-A^{\circ}}({\,]}U{[\,})

for all
i
.

Proof.

Consider the following
3\times 3
grid, in which each row and column is an exact triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces?}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},B}(Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},A\cap B}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},c}(Y-A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},c-A^{\circ}}({\,]}U{[\,})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(Y-B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(A^{\circ}),}

where the term marked ‘?’ is
R\Gamma\left(\mathfrak{P}_{K},R\underline{\Gamma}_{{\,]}Y-A{[\,}}\ \underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{{\,]}B{[\,}}\,\Omega^{\bullet}\right)
. Our smoothness assumptions imply that there is a Gysin isomorphism
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},B}(Y)=R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(B)[-2c]
where
c=\operatorname{codim}_{Y}(B)
, and similarly that
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},A\cap B}(A)=R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(A\cap B)[-2c]
. Moreover, the map
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},B}(Y)\to R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},A\cap B}(A)
is identified, via the Gysin isomorphisms, with the obvious restriction map
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(B)\to R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(A\cap B)
(shifted by
-2c
). Note that this compatibility of Gysin morphisms is far from being merely formal, but rather is a basic case of the “excess intersection formula” of Déglise, see
[
Dég08
, Proposition 4.10]
. So the group ‘
?
’ has to be isomorphic to the mapping fibre of this map, which is simply
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},c}(B^{\circ})[-2c]
.

We now consider the “dual diagram’, obtained by first applying the functor
\operatorname{RHom}(-,K[-2d])
to this diagram, and then reflecting in the off-diagonal. One sees that each term in the dual diagram for
(A,B)
is isomorphic to the corresponding term in the original diagram for
(B,A)
. After a little book-keeping, one also sees that these isomorphisms are compatible with the arrows in the two diagrams. Hence we deduce an isomorphism in the remaining corner also, namely

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},c-A^{\circ}}({\,]}U{[\,})\cong\operatorname{RHom}(R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},c-B^{\circ}}({\,]}U{[\,}),K[-2d]).

But we have seen that the dual of
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},c-B^{\circ}}({\,]}U{[\,})[-2d]
is
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},c-A}({\,]}U{[\,})
.
∎

0.9.
Log-rigid syntomic and fp-cohomology

In order to make use of the formalism of
Section

0.7
for actual computations, we shall need to replace Nekovář–Nizioł cohomology (which has excellent functorial properties, but is inexplicit) with a more explicitly computable theory.

0.9.1.
The semistable case

Let
\pi
be a uniformizer of
K
, and write
O_{K}^{\pi}
for the scheme
\Spec O_{K}
with the canonical log structure, given by the chart
1\mapsto\pi
. For
F
the maximal unramified subfield of
K
, write
O_{F}^{0}
for the scheme
\Spec O_{F}
with the ‘hollow’ log structure given by
1\mapsto 0
. Denote by
k
the residue field of
O_{K}
, and write
k^{0}
for the scheme
\Spec k
, again with the log structure given by
1\mapsto 0
.

Log-rigid syntomic cohomology

Let
X
be a strictly semistable log scheme over
O_{K}^{\pi}
, and
D\subseteq X
a closed subscheme with complement
U
, such that
(U,X)
is a
strictly semistable log scheme with boundary
over
O_{K}^{\pi}
in the sense of
[
EY18
, Definition 3.3]
.

We can then consider three complexes associated to
X
and
D
:

•

the
rigid Hyodo-Kato cohomology

R\Gamma^{\HK}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle)
, which is a complex of
F
-vector spaces with an
F
-semilinear Frobenius
\varphi
and an
F
-linear monodromy operator
N
, satisfying
N\varphi=p\varphi N
. (Its definition involves a rather intricate limiting process over collections of liftings of open subsets of
X_{0}
to characteristic zero, since it is not generally possible to find a global lifting of
X_{0}
compatible with Frobenius.)

•

the
log-rigid cohomology

R\Gamma_{\lrig}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle/\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\pi})
, which is a complex of
K
-vector spaces, quasi-isomorpic to the de Rham cohomology of the dagger space
\mathcal{X}=\ ]X_{0}[\,_{X_{K}^{\mathrm{an}}}^{\dagger}
with log poles along
D_{K}
.

•

the
Deligne-de Rham cohomology

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}^{D}(U_{K})
, which is a complex of
K
-vector spaces with a filtration
\Fil^{r}
(the Hodge filtration). If
X_{K}
is proper, it is quasi-isomorphic to
R\Gamma(X_{K},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X_{K}}\langle D_{K}\rangle)
with the filtration defined by truncation.

Note 0.9.1.1
.

These complexes are related by morphisms in the derived category of
K
-vector spaces (cf. Equation 3.11 of op.cit.)

R\Gamma^{\HK}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle)\otimes_{F}K\rTo^{\iota_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rig}}}R\Gamma_{\lrig}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle/\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\pi})\lTo^{\mathrm{sp}}R\Gamma^{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(U_{K}).

The morphism
\iota_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rig}}
is a quasi-isomorphism, and
\mathrm{sp}
is also a quasi-isomorphism if
X
is proper.

For
r\geqslant 0
, Ertl–Yamada
[
EY18
, Definition 3.4]
define a
log-rigid syntomic cohomology
:

Definition 0.9.1.2
.

Define
R\Gamma_{\lrigsyn}\left(X\langle D\rangle,r,\pi\right)
to be the homotopy limit of the diagram

{diagram}

Here
\varphi_{r}\coloneqq p^{-r}\varphi
.

Note 0.9.1.3
.

There is no properness assumption on
X
, so if we start with a strictly semistable log-scheme
U
, we can always simply take
X=U
and
D=\varnothing
in the above construction.

However, it is important to allow more general
X
in order to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 0.9.1.4
.

Suppose
U=X-D
as above, with
X
proper. Then for all
r\geqslant 0
, there exist canonical quasi-isomorphisms

R\Gamma_{\lrigsyn}\left(U,r,\pi\right)\cong R\Gamma_{\lrigsyn}(X\langle D\rangle,r,\pi)\cong R\Gamma_{\NNsyn}(U_{K},r).

Proof.

This is
[
EY18
, Corollary 4.2]
.
∎

Convention
.

Following
op.cit.
, we shall call a semistable
O_{K}
-scheme
U

compactifiable
if it can be written as
X-D
for some
X
and
D
as above. Thus log-rigid syntomic cohomology agrees with Nekovář–Nizioł syntomic cohomology for compactifiable semistable schemes.

Compactly supported log-rigid syntomic and fp-cohomology

Let
(X,D)
be a strictly semistable log-scheme with boundary, as before; we
do
now assume that
X
is proper. In
[
EY19
]
, Ertl and Yamada define

•

rigid Hyodo-Kato cohomology with compact support
,
R\Gamma^{\HK}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle-D_{0}\rangle)
,

•

log-rigid cohomology with compact support
,
R\Gamma_{\lrig}(X_{0}\langle-D_{0}\rangle/\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\pi})
.

Again, the former is
F
-linear and equipped with Frobenius and monodromy operators, and the latter is
K
-linear.

Proposition 0.9.1.5
.

Let
d=\dim(X)
. Then there exist canonical isomorphisms

(0.9.1)

\displaystyle R\Gamma^{\HK}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle-D_{0}\rangle)

\displaystyle\rTo^{\cong}R\Gamma^{\HK}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle)^{*}[-2d]

(0.9.2)

\displaystyle R\Gamma_{\lrig}(X_{0}\langle-D_{0}\rangle/\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\pi})

\displaystyle\rTo^{\cong}R\Gamma_{\lrig}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle/\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\pi})^{*}[-2d].

Proof.

See
[
EY19
, Theorem 4.1]
.
∎

Note 0.9.1.6
.

The morphism (
0.9.1
) is compatible with
\varphi
and
N
, if we define the Frobenius
\Phi
and monodromy on the right-hand side as
p^{d}\cdot(\varphi^{-1})^{\vee}
and
-N^{\vee}
.

Remark 0.9.1.7
.

The Frobenius on
R\Gamma^{\HK}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle)
admits an inverse in the derived category, although it is not necessarily invertible at the level of complexes; explicitly, we can replace the complex computing
R\Gamma^{\HK}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle)
with its “perfection”, as in
[
Bes12
, §4]
.

We also have the complex
R\Gamma^{D}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(U_{K})\coloneqq R\Gamma(X_{K},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X_{K}/K}\langle-D_{K}\rangle)
computing compactly-supported de Rham cohomology of
U_{K}
, with its truncation filtration; and there are maps in the derived category relating these three complexes, as before.
We define log-rigid syntomic cohomology with compact support as follows:

Definition 0.9.1.8
.

We define
R\Gamma_{\lrigsyn}(X\langle-D\rangle,r,\pi)
as the homotopy limit of the diagram analogous to
Definition

0.9.1.2
with the three complexes replaced by their
\langle-D\rangle
versions.

Definition 0.9.1.9
.

Replacing
1-p^{-r}\varphi
by
P(p^{-r}\varphi)
, for some
P\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}[t]
with constant coefficient
1
, we obtain log-rigid fp-cohomology with compact support, which we denote by
R\Gamma_{\lrigfp}(X\langle-D\rangle,r,\pi;P)
and
R\Gamma_{\lrigfp,c}(X\langle-D\rangle,r,\pi;P)
respectively.

The following result is a consequence of the results in
op.cit.
.

Theorem 0.9.1.10
.

For all
r\geqslant 0
, there exists a canonical isomorphism

R\Gamma_{\lrigfp,}(X\langle-D\rangle,r,\pi;P)\cong R\Gamma_{\NNfp,c}(U_{K},r;P).

Moreover, this isomorphism is compatible with pullback.

Pairings

be as above.

.

We have cup products

.

When

0.9.2.
Cohomology theories with coefficients for smooth schemes

Rigid syntomic/fp-cohomology

Let
X
be a smooth
O_{K}
-scheme with generic fibre
X_{K}
and special fibre
X_{0}
; we assume that
X_{K}
is proper. Let
D
be a divisor in
X
.

Denote by
\mathcal{X}
the dagger space tube of
X_{0}
in
X_{K}^{\mathrm{an}}
.

Definition 0.9.2.1
.

An
overconvergent filtered
F
-isocrystal
on
(X,\bar{X}_{K})
consists of the following data:

•

an overconvergent
F
-isocrystal
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}}
on
X_{0}
;

•

an algebraic vector bundle
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}}
on the variety
\bar{X}_{K}
, endowed with a connection with logarithmic singularities along
\bar{X}_{K}-X_{K}
, and with a filtration satisfying Griffiths transversality;

•

an isomorphism of rigid-analytic vector bundles over the dagger space
\mathcal{X}
, compatible with connections,

\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig},X},

where
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig},X}
is the realisation of
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}}
corresponding to the lifting
X
of
X_{0}
.

Definition 0.9.2.2
.

Define

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}})=R\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig},X}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}_{X_{K}}\langle D_{K}\rangle).

Note 0.9.2.3
.

(1)

In the case of trivial coefficients, we recover the complex
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle)
(c.f. Notation
0.8.5.5
).

(2)

We equip
X
with the log structure associated to
X_{0}
. Then we have
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}})=R\Gamma^{\HK}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}})
, and
N=0
.

(3)

There exists a specialisation map

\operatorname{sp}:R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{K}\langle D_{K}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}})\rTo R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}})_{K}.

Definition 0.9.2.4
.

Let
r\in\mathbf{Z}
, and let
P\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}[t]
have constant coefficient
1
. Following Besser
[
Bes12
]
, we define the
rigid fp-cohomology
of
X\langle D\rangle
with coefficients
\mathscr{F}
, twist
r
and polynomial
P
as the homotopy limit of the diagram

(0.9.5)

\begin{diagram}

where the unlabelled arrow is base-extension to
K
. We denote it by
R\Gamma_{\rigfp}(X\langle D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P)
. When
P(t)=1-t
, then we call it
rigid syntomic cohomology
, denoted by
R\Gamma_{\rigsyn}(X\langle D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r)
.

Notation 0.9.2.5
.

We shall write
R\Gamma_{\rigfp}(X\langle D\rangle,r;P)
if
\mathscr{F}
is the trivial isocrystal.

Note 0.9.2.6
.

(1)

We have
R\Gamma_{\rigfp}(X\langle D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P)=R\Gamma_{\rigfp}(X\langle D\rangle,\mathscr{F}(r),0;P)
where
\mathscr{F}(r)
is the
r
-th Tate twist of
\mathscr{F}
.

(2)

If
F=K
, the middle arrow is the identity, and the zigzag diagram collapses to the mapping fibre of the map

\Fil^{r}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{K}\langle D_{K}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}})\rTo^{P(\varphi_{r})\circ\operatorname{sp}\ }R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}}).

(3)

If
X
is equipped with the log structure associated to
X_{0}
, then by Note
0.9.2.3
(2) we have a natural map

\delta:R\Gamma_{\lrigfp}(X\langle D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P)\rTo R\Gamma_{\rigfp}(X\langle D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P).

Lemma 0.9.2.7
.

Let
X
be a strictly semistable proper log scheme over
O_{K}^{\pi}
, and let
D\subset X
be a closed subscheme with complement
U
. Suppose that
(U,X)
is a strictly semistable log scheme, and let
Z
be a smooth open subscheme of
X
. We then have a restriction map

\mathrm{res}_{Z}:R\Gamma_{\lrigfp}(X\langle D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P)\rTo R\Gamma_{\rigfp}(Z\langle D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P).

Proof.

Consequence of Note
0.9.2.6
, together with the restriction map on lrig-fp cohomology.
∎

Rigid fp-cohomology with compact support

We continue under the assumptions of Section
0.9.2

Notation 0.9.2.8
.

Write
\cosp:R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig},c}(X_{0}\langle-D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}})\rightarrow R\Gamma^{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{K},\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}})
for the cospecialisation map.

Definition 0.9.2.9
.

Let
r\geqslant 0
, and let
Q\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}[t]
have constant coefficient
1
. Define the rigid fp-cohomology with compact support of
X
with coefficients
\mathscr{F}
, twist
r
and polynomial
Q
, as the homotopy limit of the zigzag diagram

(0.9.6)

\begin{diagram}

We denote it by
R\Gamma_{\rigfp,c}(X\langle-D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;Q)
.

We have the following analogue of Proposition
0.9.1.11
for rigid fp-cohomology with compact support:

Lemma 0.9.2.10
.

The complex (
0.9.6
) is quasi-isomorphic to the homotopy limit of the following diagram, shifted by
[-2d]
:

\begin{diagram}

Here
\Phi=(\varphi^{\vee})^{-1}
.

Proof.

See
[
Bes12
, §4]
.
∎

Proposition 0.9.2.11
.

Let
X
be a strictly semistable proper log scheme over
O_{K}^{\pi}
, and let
D\subset X
be a closed subscheme with complement
U
. Suppose that
(U,X)
is a strictly semistable log scheme, and let
Z
be a smooth open subscheme of
X
. Then in the derived category, we have an extension-by-0 morphism

R\Gamma_{\rigfp,c}(Z\langle-D\rangle,r;Q)\rTo R\Gamma_{\lrigfp}(X\langle-D\rangle,r;Q).

Proof.

Clear from Proposition
0.9.1.11
and Lemma
0.9.2.10
. Here, the morphism

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(Z_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle)^{*}\rTo R\Gamma^{\HK}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle)^{*}

is given by the composition of
(\iota_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rig}})^{\vee}
with the dual of the natural restriction map

R\Gamma_{\lrig}(X_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle)\rTo R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(Z_{0}\langle D_{0}\rangle).

∎

Remark 0.9.2.12
.

There should clearly be a version of Proposition
0.9.2.11
with coefficients.

Proposition 0.9.2.13
.

For
r,s\geqslant 0
, we have a cup product

R\Gamma^{i}_{\rigfp}(X\langle D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P)\times R\Gamma^{j}_{\rigfp,c}(X\langle-D\rangle,\mathscr{G},s;Q)\rTo R\Gamma_{\rigfp,c}^{i+j}(X,\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{G},r+s;P\star Q).

Proof.

See
[
Bes12
, §2]
.
∎

Lemma 0.9.2.14
.

If
X
is connected of dimension
d
and
Q(\zeta/p)\neq 0
for all
\zeta\in\mu_{[F:\mathbf{Q}_{p}]}
, then there is a canonical isomorphism

\tr_{\mathrm{fp},X}:H^{2d+1}_{\rigfp,c}(X,d+1;Q)\cong K.

It is given explicitly by mapping
(x,y)\in H^{2d}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(X_{K})\oplus H^{2d}_{\mathrm{rig},c}(X_{0})
to
\tr_{\mathrm{dR},X_{K}}(x)-Q(\tfrac{\varphi}{p})^{-1}\tr_{\mathrm{rig},X_{0}}(y)
.

Remark 0.9.2.15
.

The factor
Q(\varphi/p)
is included to make the isomorphism compatible with change of
Q
.

Corollary 0.9.2.16
.

Assume that
(P\star Q)(\zeta/p)\neq 0
for all
\zeta\in\mu_{[F:\mathbf{Q}_{p}]}
. When
i+j=2d+1
,
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{F}^{\vee}
and
r+s=d+1
, then we get a pairing denoted
\langle\quad,\quad\rangle_{\rigfp,X}
. The restriction map and the extentension-by-0 are adjoint with respect to this pairing.

Gros fp-cohomology

In Section
0.12.2
, we will need a variant of rigid fp-cohomology which is less refined, but more convenient for computations. Recall that we have

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X\langle\pm D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}})_{K}=R\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig},X}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{X}/K}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}_{K}\rangle)=R\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{X}/K}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}_{K}\rangle),

where
\mathcal{X}
is the tube of
X_{0}
in
X_{K}^{\mathrm{an}}
.

Definition 0.9.2.17
.

For
r\geqslant 0
, we define the
truncated rigid cohomology
, denoted by
\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathscr{F},r)
, to be the cohomology of the subcomplex
\left(\Fil^{r-\bullet}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}}\right)|_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{X}/K}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}\rangle
, and similarly with compact support.

Note 0.9.2.18
.

We obtain “filtered” specialisation and cospecialisation maps

\displaystyle\operatorname{sp}:\Fil^{r}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{K}\langle\pm D_{K}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}})\to\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathscr{F},r)_{K},

\displaystyle\cosp:\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{X}\langle\mp\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathscr{F},r)\to\Fil^{r}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},c}(X_{K}\langle\mp D_{K}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}}),

compatible with the usual specialisation and cospecialisation maps on the non-filtered complexes.

Remark 0.9.2.19
.

The inclusion of
\left(\Fil^{r-\bullet}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{dR}}\right)|_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{X}/K}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}\rangle
into the full de Rham complex gives maps

\iota:\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathscr{F},r)\to R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle\pm D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{F}),

and similarly with compact support; but the maps induced by
\iota
on cohomology are not necessarily either injective or surjective, and the groups
\widetilde{H}^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}
and
\widetilde{H}^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}
may not even be finite-dimensional over
K
.

Definition 0.9.2.20
.

(a)

Define the
Gros fp-cohomology
of
X\langle\pm D\rangle
with coefficients
\mathscr{F}
, twist
r
and polynomial
P
to be the cohomology of the complex
\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\rigfp}(X\langle\pm D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P)
which is the homotopy limit of the diagram

{diagram}

where the unlabelled arrow is base-extension.

(b)

Similarly, define the
Gros fp-cohomology with compact support
of
X\langle\mp D\rangle
with coefficients
\mathscr{G}
, twist
s
and polynomial
Q
to be the homotopy limit
\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\rigfp,c}(X\langle\mp D\rangle,\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{rig}},s;P)
of the diagram

{diagram}

Note 0.9.2.21
.

As before, if
F=K
then the middle arrow is the identity map and both diagrams can be simplified to mapping fibres: in this case we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\rigfp}(X\langle\pm D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P)

\displaystyle=MF\left[\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}},r)_{K}\rTo^{\ P(\varphi_{r})\circ\iota\ }R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle\pm D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{rig}})_{K}\right],

\displaystyle\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\rigfp,c}(X\langle\mp D\rangle,\mathscr{G},s;Q)

\displaystyle=MF\left[\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(\mathcal{X}\langle\mp\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{rig}},s)_{K}\rTo^{\ Q(\varphi_{s})\circ\iota\ }R\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{0}\langle\mp D_{0}\rangle,\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{rig}})_{K}\right].

Remark 0.9.2.22
.

Comparing the diagrams of
Definition

0.9.2.20
with (
0.9.5
) and (
0.9.6
), we see that the filtered specialisation map (c.f. Note
0.9.2.18
) on the de Rham cohomology gives a map

\gamma^{*}:R\Gamma_{\rigfp}(X,\mathscr{F}\langle\pm D\rangle,r;P)\to\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\rigfp}(X\langle\pm D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P).

Similarly, the filtered cospecialisation induces a map

\gamma_{*}:\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\rigfp,c}(X\langle\mp D\rangle,\mathscr{G},s;Q)\to R\Gamma_{\rigfp,c}(X\langle\mp D\rangle,\mathscr{G},s;Q).

We also have cup-products

\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\rigfp}(X\langle\pm D\rangle,\mathscr{F},r;P)\times\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\rigfp,c}(X\langle\mp D\rangle,\mathscr{G},s;Q)\to\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\rigfp,c}(X,\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{G},r+s;P\star Q),

related to those in the (non-Gros) rigid fp-cohomology (c.f. Proposition
0.9.2.13
) by the adunction formula

\gamma_{*}(\gamma^{*}(x)\cup y)=x\cup\gamma_{*}(y).

In particular,
\gamma^{*}
and
\gamma_{*}
are transposes of each other with respect to the pairing induced by the trace map on the degree
2d+1
cohomology. Moreover, the pairing is compatible with the maps in Note
0.9.2.21
.

Notation 0.9.2.23
.

We denote the pairing by
\langle\quad,\quad\rangle_{\widetilde{\rigfp},X}
.

Partial compact support

We can similarly define Gros fp-cohomology with partial compact support: let
\mathfrak{P}
be a formal
O_{K}
-scheme with special fibre
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
, and let we are given varieties
U,V,W\subseteq\mathfrak{P}_{0}
as in
Section

0.8.3
, with
U
open in
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
smooth. For
s\geqslant 0
, denote by
\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(]U[,\mathscr{G},s)
the hypercohomology of the
s
-th filtration subcomplex of the de Rham complex of
\mathscr{G}
.

Definition 0.9.2.24
.

Let
Q\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}[t]
be a polynomial with constant coefficient
1
. Define the Gros fp-cohomology of
]U[
with compact support towards
W
, coefficients
\mathscr{G}
, twist
s
and polynomial
Q
as the mapping fibre

\widetilde{R\Gamma}^{j}_{\rigfp,\mathcal{W}}(]U[,\mathscr{G},s;Q)=\MF\left[\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(]U[,\mathscr{G},s)\rTo^{\ Q(\varphi_{s})\circ\iota\ }R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(]U[,\mathscr{G})\right],

where
\iota
denotes the natural map

\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(]U[,\mathscr{G},s)\rTo R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR},cW}(]U[,\mathscr{G}).

0.10.
Geometry of Siegel threefolds

0.10.1.
The Klingen-level Siegel threefold

Definition 0.10.1.1
.

Let
Y_{G,\Kl}
be the canonical
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-model of the Siegel
3
-fold of level
K^{p}\times\Kl(p)
, for some (sufficiently small) tame level
K^{p}
. Let
X_{G,\Kl}
be a toroidal compactification of
Y_{\Kl}
(for some suitably chosen cone-decomposition
\Sigma
), and
X_{\Kl}^{\min}
the minimal compactification. Write
D
for the boundary divisor of the toroidal compactification.

We similarly write
Y_{G}
for the canonical
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-model of the Siegel
3
-fold of level
K^{p}\times G(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
, and
X_{G}
for its toroidal compactification.

Remark 0.10.1.2
.

The scheme
Y_{G,\Kl}
has an interpretation as a moduli space of abelian surfaces with some prime-to-
p
level structure and a subgroup
C
of order
p
. The moduli interpretation of
X_{G,\Kl}
parametrises semiabelian schemes with some appropriate degeneration data at the boundary (depending on
\Sigma
).

Theorem 0.10.1.3
.

The scheme
Y_{G,\Kl}
is semistable over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Its special fibre can be written as a disjoint union

Y_{G,\Kl,0}=Y_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}\sqcup Y_{G,\Kl,0}^{e}\sqcup Y_{G,\Kl,0}^{a}

of loci where
C
is étale-locally isomorphic to either
\mu_{p}
, the constant group scheme
\mathbf{Z}/p
, or
\alpha_{p}
respectively.

The space
Y_{G,\Kl,0}^{a}
is 2-dimensional, and coincides with the non-smooth locus of
Y_{G,\Kl,0}
. Both
Y_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}
and
Y_{G,\Kl,0}^{e}
are open and smooth, and the closure of either of these strata is its union with
Y_{G,\Kl,0}^{a}
.

Proof.

See Theorem 3 of
[
Til06
]
. (Note that our notations are slightly different from Tilouine’s: he uses the notation
X_{P}(p)^{m}
for the
closure

\overline{Y_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}}
of the multiplicative locus, rather than the multiplicative locus alone, and similarly for
X_{P}(p)^{e}
.)
∎

The proof of the following result was suggested to us by Kai-Wen Lan:

Proposition 0.10.1.4
.

The pair
\left(X_{G,\Kl},Y_{G,\Kl}\right)
is
strictly semistable with boundary
in the sense of
[
EY18
]
.

Proof.

We need to check that the union of
X_{G,\Kl}-Y_{G,\Kl}
and
X_{G,\Kl,0}
is a strict normal crossing divisor.
By
[
LS18
, Corollary 2.1.7]
, the toroidal compactification is étale locally a fibre product of an affine toroidal embedding and some scheme. This shows that
X_{G,\Kl}
is semistable when
Y_{G,\Kl}
is. It remains to show that
X_{G,\Kl}
is strictly semistable, not just semistable.

Let
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\in I}
denote the irreducible components of
Y_{G,\Kl,0}
. Let
W_{i}
denote the closure of
Z_{i}
in
X_{G,\Kl}
for each
i\in I
. For each
J\subset I
, we write
Z_{J}=\cap_{i\in J}Z_{i}
. Since
Y_{G,\Kl}
is strict semistable, we know that
Z_{J}
is regular, for each
J\subset I
.

It follows from
[
LS18
, Proposition 2.3.12]
that for each
i\in I
,
Z_{i}
is a well-positioned subscheme of
Y_{G,\Kl,0}
, and its closure
W_{i}
in
X_{G,\Kl}
is an irreducible component of the special fibre
X_{G,\Kl,0}
of
X_{G,\Kl}
. Let
W_{J}=\cap_{i\in J}W_{i}
, for each
J\subset I
.

By
[
LS18
, Lemma 2.2.3]
,
Z_{J}
is also well-positioned, for each
J\subset I
. By Corollary 2.1.7 again,
W_{J}
is also the closure of
Z_{J}
in
X_{G,\Kl}
, which is the toroidal compactification of
Z_{J}
and satisfies Theorem 2.3.2(7) in
op.cit.
, in particular. Then the same argument as for Proposition 2.3.13 in
op.cit.
shows that (when
\Sigma
is smooth) all boundary components of
W_{J}
are regular as
Z_{J}
is.

Since these boundary components are exactly the intersections of
W_{J}
with (multiple) irreducible components of the boundary of
X_{G,\Kl}
, it follows that the union of
X_{G,\Kl}-Y_{G,\Kl}
and the special fibre of
Y_{\Kl}
is a strict semistable divisor, as desired (c.f Stacks Project Tag 0BIA).
∎

Notation 0.10.1.5
.

•

For
\star\in\{m,e\}
, write
\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{\star}}
for the closure of
\overline{Y_{G,\Kl,0}^{\star}}
in
X_{\Kl}
.

•

Let
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{a}=\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}}\cap\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{e}}
.

Definition 0.10.1.6
.

We consider the following closed subschemes of the multiplicative component
\overline{X_{\Kl,0}^{m}}
:

•

the locus
X^{a}=X_{G,\Kl,0}^{a}
just mentioned, where
C
is
\alpha_{p}
;

•

the locus
X^{b}
where
C^{\perp}/C
is bi-connected;

•

the boundary
\partial X
(the intersection of
X^{m}
with the toroidal boundary divisor).

Notation 0.10.1.7
.

Following
[
Pil17
, §9–10]
, let us write

X_{G,\Kl}^{=2}\coloneqq X^{m}-X^{a}-X^{b},\qquad X^{=1}_{G,\Kl}\coloneqq X^{b}-\left(X^{a}\cap X^{b}\right),\qquad X^{=0}_{G,\Kl}\coloneqq X^{a}.

The notations
X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant r}
and
X_{G,\Kl}^{\leqslant r}
have the obvious meanings.

Warning.

These subsets do not define a stratification (since
X_{G,\Kl}^{=1}
is not dense in
X_{G,\Kl}^{\leqslant 1}
), so the notation is slightly abusive, but it is convenient.

Notation 0.10.1.8
.

We shall more often denote the open subvariety
X_{G,\Kl}^{=2}
by
X_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}
, since it is the ordinary locus of
X^{m}
(although not of
X_{G,\Kl,0}
). As before, we use calligraphic letters for the tubes of these subvarieties considered as dagger spaces.

Notation 0.10.1.9
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a coherent sheaf on
\mathcal{X}^{\geqslant 1}_{\Kl}
, write
R\Gamma_{c0}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}},\mathcal{E}\right)
for the cohomology of
\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{E}
, compactly supported away from
\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{=1}
.

Note 0.10.1.10
.

By definition,
R\Gamma_{c0}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}},\mathcal{E}\right)
fits into an exact triangle

R\Gamma_{c}\!\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{=1},\mathcal{E}\right)\to R\Gamma_{c}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{E}\right)\to R\Gamma_{c0}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}},\mathcal{E}\right)\to[+1].

0.10.2.
De Rham cohomology with coefficients

Now let us suppose
\mathcal{V}
is (the canonical extension of) the automorphic vector bundle associated to a
G
-representation
V
, so that
\mathcal{V}
is equipped with a connection
\nabla
having logarithmic poles along the toroidal boundary divisor
D
.

Proposition 0.10.2.1
.

For any integer
i
, any
G
-representation
V
, and either choice of signs, there are perfect pairings of finite-dimensional vector spaces

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V}\right)\times H^{6-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}\langle\mp D\rangle,\mathcal{V}\right)\to\mathbf{Q}_{p},

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c1}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle\pm D\rangle,\mathcal{V}\right)\times H^{6-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c0}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle\mp D\rangle,\mathcal{V}\right)\to\mathbf{Q}_{p}.

Moreover, for these pairings the transpose of the restriction map

H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}\rangle,-\right)\to H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c0}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle\pm\mathcal{D}\rangle,-\right)

is the extension-by-0 map
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c1}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle\mp D\rangle,-\right)\to H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}\langle\mp D\rangle,-\right)
.

Proof.

Let
A
denote the universal abelian variety over
Y_{G,\Kl,\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
. For any
m\geqslant 0
, and suitable choices of the toroidal boundary data
\Sigma
, the fibre product
A^{m}
can be extended to a smooth
(2m+3)
-dimensional projective variety
K_{m}
(the
m
-th Kuga–Sato variety) lying over
X_{G,\Kl,\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
, such that the preimage of the boundary
D=X_{G,\Kl,\mathbf{Q}_{p}}-Y_{G,\Kl,\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
is a normal-crossing divisor
\partial K_{m}
. We let
\mathcal{K}_{m}
denote the associated dagger space.

Then
\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}\langle D\rangle
is quasi-isomorphic to a direct summand of
R\pi_{\star}\left(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{K}_{m}}\langle\partial\rangle\right)[2m]
, for some
m
depending on
V
; and
\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}\langle-D\rangle
identifies with the dual direct summand of
R\pi_{\star}\left(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{K}_{m}}\langle-\partial\rangle\right)[2m]
. So the result follows from
Proposition

0.8.5.6
applied to
\mathcal{K}_{m}
.
∎

Corollary 0.10.2.2
.

(a)

The groups
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c1}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V}\right)
and
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c1}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V}\right)
are finite-dimensional for all
i
.

(b)

The groups
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c0}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V}\right)
and
\mathbf{H}^{i}_{c0}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V}\right)
are finite-dimensional for all
i
.

(c)

There are perfect pairings

H^{6-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c0}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle\right)\times H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c1}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\right)\to\mathbf{Q}_{p}

and

H^{6-i}_{\mathrm{dR},c0}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V}\right)\times H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR},c1}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle-\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\right)\to\mathbf{Q}_{p}.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

0.10.2.1
and the long exact sequences relating
c0
and
c1
cohomology established above.
∎

We will need to assume the following conjecture, which will be proven in forthcoming work of Lan and Skinner:

Conjecture 0.10.2.3

(Eigenspace Vanishing Conjecture)
.

Let
\Pi
be an automorphic representation contributing to
H^{3}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{G,\Kl,0},[V])
, for some automorphic vector bundle
V
, and suppose that
\Pi_{p}
is a generic spherical representation. Write
\{\Pi\}
for generalised
\Pi
-eigenspace for the spherical Hecke algebra of
K^{p}
.

(a)

We have

H^{i}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{G,\Kl,0}^{m},\mathcal{V})\{\Pi\}=H^{i}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{G,\Kl,0}^{e},\mathcal{V})\{\Pi\}=0

for all
i\neq 3
.

(b)

For
\spadesuit\in\{m,e\}
,
H^{3}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{G,\Kl,0}^{\spadesuit},\mathcal{V})\{\Pi\}
is
8
-dimensional.

(c)

If
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{?}
is any of the closed subvarieties
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{=0}
or
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{=1}
of
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}
, then
H^{i}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{G,\Kl,0}^{?},[V])\{\Pi\}
should vanish in all degrees.

If this conjecture holds, then we obtain the following consequence:

Proposition 0.10.2.4
.

We have an isomorphism

H^{3}_{\lrig}(X_{G,\Kl,0}\langle D_{0}\rangle/O_{K}^{0},\mathcal{V})\{\Pi\}\cong H^{3}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}\langle D_{0}\rangle,\mathcal{V})\{\Pi\}\oplus H^{3}_{\mathrm{rig}}(X_{G,\Kl,0}^{e}\langle D_{0}\rangle,\mathcal{V})\{\Pi\}.

Proof.

By
[
Til06
, Theorem 3]
,
X_{\Kl,0}
is the union of the irreducible components
\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}}
and
\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{e}}
. We deduce from
[
GK02
, Theorem C]
that for
\spadesuit\in\{m,e\}
, the canonical map

H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{dR}}\left(]\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{\spadesuit}}\langle D_{0}\rangle[,\mathcal{V}\right)\rTo H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{dR}}\left(]X_{G,\Kl,0}^{\spadesuit}\langle D_{0}\rangle[,\mathcal{V}\right)

is an isomorphism. We hence deduce from Conjecture
0.10.2.3
(a) that
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}\left(]\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{\spadesuit}}\langle D_{0}\rangle[,\mathcal{V}\right)=0
unless
i=3
.

Now we have the C̆ech spectral sequence (c.f.
[
GK07
]
)

E_{1}^{pq}=H^{q}_{\mathrm{dR}}(]Y^{p+1}\langle D_{0}\rangle[,\mathcal{V})\Rightarrow H^{p+q}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V}),

where

Y^{p+1}=\begin{cases}\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}}\sqcup\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{e}}&\text{if $p=0$},\\
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{a}&\text{if $p=1$}\end{cases}.

We deduce from Conjecture
0.10.2.3
that

H^{3}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}\langle\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{V})\{\Pi\}\cong H^{3}_{\mathrm{dR}}\left(]\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}}\langle D_{0}\rangle[,\mathcal{V}\right)\{\Pi\}\oplus H^{3}_{\mathrm{dR}}\left(]\overline{X_{G,\Kl,0}^{e}}\langle D_{0}\rangle[,\mathcal{V}\right)\{\Pi\},

from which we deduce the required result.
∎

0.10.3.
The map
\iota

Let
Y_{H}
be the Shimura variety of
H
of level
K_{H}^{p}\times H(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
, where
K_{H}^{p}=K^{p}\cap H(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{p})
, and write
X_{H}
for its toroidal compactification. We assume that the compactifications of
Y_{H}
and
Y_{G}
are compatible under the natural embedding
Y_{H}\rightarrow Y_{G}
(c.f.
[
LPSZ19
, §2.2.4]
).

Definition 0.10.3.1
.

Let
X_{H,\Delta}
be the covering of
X_{H}
parametrising choices of finite flat
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-subgroup-scheme
C\subset(E_{1}\oplus E_{2})[p]
of order
p
which project nontrivially into both
E_{i}
.

Lemma 0.10.3.2
.

There is a finite map
\iota_{\Delta}:X_{H,\Delta}\to X_{\Kl}
lying over the map
X_{H}\to X_{G}
, and the preimage of
X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
is the open subscheme
X_{H,\Delta}^{\mathrm{ord}}
where
C
is multiplicative.

Proof.

See
[
LPSZ19
, §4.1]
.
∎

Remark 0.10.3.3
.

The map
\iota_{\Delta}
induces the map defined in Section
0.7.6
on the generic fibres.

We therefore obtain a finite map of dagger spaces
\iota_{\Delta}:\mathcal{X}_{H,\Delta}^{\mathrm{ord}}\to\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
(whose image is contained in
\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}
).

Proposition 0.10.3.4
.

For any locally free coherent sheaf
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
, the natural pullback map

\iota_{\Delta}^{*}:R\Gamma_{c}(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{E})\to R\Gamma_{c}(\mathcal{X}_{H,\Delta}^{\mathrm{ord}},\iota^{*}\mathcal{E})

factors through
R\Gamma_{c0}(\mathcal{X}_{\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}},\mathcal{E})
.

Proof.

This is an instance of
Proposition

0.8.3.5
.
∎

0.10.4.
The reduction: Step 2

Notation 0.10.4.1
.

Write
\eta_{\lrigfp,-D}
for the image of
\eta_{\NNfp,-D}
in
H^{3}_{\lrigfp,c}(X_{G,\Kl}\langle-D\rangle,\mathcal{V},1+q;P)
.

Proposition 0.10.4.2
.

The pairing (
0.7.8
) is equal to

\left\langle\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\lrigsyn,\underline{\Phi}},\,(\iota_{\Delta}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]})^{*}(\eta_{\lrigfp,q,-D})\right\rangle_{\lrigfp,Y_{H,\Delta}}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\left\langle\iota_{*}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}(\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\syn,\underline{\Phi}}),\,\eta_{\NNfp,q,-D}\right\rangle_{\NNfp,Y_{G,\Kl}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\syn,\underline{\Phi}},\,(\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]})^{*}(\eta_{\NNfp,q,-D})\right\rangle_{\NNfp,Y_{H,\Delta}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\lrigsyn,\underline{\Phi}},\,(\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]})^{*}(\eta_{\lrigfp,q,-D})\right\rangle_{\lrigfp,Y_{\Delta}}.

Here

•

the first equality uses adjunction between pushforward and pullback;

•

the second one follows from Theorems
0.9.1.4
and
0.9.1.10
.

∎

We now apply Proposition
0.9.2.11
to the case where
X=X_{G,\Kl}
,
D
is the toroidal boundary (so
U=Y_{G,\Kl}
) and
Z=X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
. We obtain an extension by zero map

H^{3}_{\rigfp,c}(X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}\langle-D\rangle,\mathcal{V},n,P)\to H^{3}_{\lrigfp}(X_{G,\Kl}\langle-D\rangle,\mathcal{V},n,P).

Proposition 0.10.4.3
.

The extension-by-0 map induces an isomorphism on the
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}
-eigenspace.

Proof.

Follows from Conjecture
0.10.2.3
.
∎

Corollary 0.10.4.4
.

There is a unique class

\eta_{\rigfp,q,-D}^{\geqslant 1}\in H^{3}_{\rigfp,c}(X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}\langle-D\rangle,\mathcal{V},1+q,P)[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}]

which is in the
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}
-eigenspace for the prime-to-
p
Hecke operators and maps to
\eta_{\mathrm{dR},q,-D}
under extension-by-0.

Notation 0.10.4.5
.

Write
\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\rigsyn,\underline{\Phi},\mathrm{ord}}
for the image of
\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\rigsyn,\underline{\Phi}}
in
H^{2}_{\rigfp}(Y_{H,\Delta}^{\mathrm{ord}},\Sym^{t_{1}}\mathscr{H}\boxtimes\Sym^{t_{2}}\mathscr{H},2)
under the restriction map
\mathrm{res}_{Y_{H,\Delta}^{\mathrm{ord}}}
.

2nd reduction

Theorem 0.10.4.6
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\lrigsyn,\underline{\Phi}},\,(\iota_{\Delta}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]})^{*}(\eta_{\lrigfp,q,-D})\right\rangle_{\lrigfp,Y_{\Delta}}=

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\rigsyn,\underline{\Phi},\mathrm{ord}},\,(\iota_{\Delta}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]})^{*}(\eta_{\rigfp,q,-D}^{\geqslant 1})\right\rangle_{\rigfp,Y^{\mathrm{ord}}_{H,\Delta}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\rigsyn,\underline{\Phi},\mathrm{ord}},\,(\iota_{\Delta}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]})^{*}(\eta_{\rigfp,q,-D}^{\geqslant 1}|_{Y^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\Kl}})\right\rangle_{\rigfp,Y^{\mathrm{ord}}_{H,\Delta}}.

Proof.

The first equality follows from Corollary
0.9.2.16
. The second equality is immediate from Proposition
0.10.3.4
.
∎

Step 3: Reduction to a pairing in coherent cohomology

0.11.
Coherent cohomology and automorphic forms for
G

0.11.1.
Coefficient sheaves

As in
[
LPSZ19
, §5.2]
, the pair
(r_{1},r_{2})
determines algebraic representations
L_{i}
of the Siegel Levi
M_{\Sieg}
, for
0\leqslant i\leqslant 3
, all with central character
\diag(x,\dots,x)\mapsto x^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
; and hence vector bundles
\mathcal{L}_{i}=[L_{i}]_{\mathrm{can}}
on
X_{K,\mathbf{Q}}
for any sufficiently small level
K
(the canonical extensions of the corresponding vector bundles over
Y_{K,\mathbf{Q}}
).

Notation 0.11.1.1
.

For convenience, we re-number these vector bundles by setting
N^{i}=L_{3-i}
, and
\mathcal{N}^{i}=\mathcal{L}_{3-i}
the corresponding vector bundles (so that
\mathcal{N}^{i}
is
\Omega^{i}\langle D\rangle
if
r_{1}=r_{2}=0
).

Note 0.11.1.2
.

The cohomology of these bundles, and their subcanonical analogues
[N^{i}]_{\mathrm{sub}}=\mathcal{N}^{i}(-D)
, is canonically independent of the toroidal boundary data, and hence the direct limits

\varinjlim_{K}H^{\star}(X_{K,\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{N}^{i})\quad\text{and}\quad\varinjlim_{K}H^{\star}(X_{K,\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{N}^{i}(-D))

are (left)
G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-representations. Our normalisations are such that an element
\diag(x,\dots,x)\in Z_{G}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
with
x\in\mathbf{Q}_{>0}
acts on these as multiplication by
x^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
. We know that for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant 3
, the
\GSp_{4}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-representation
\varinjlim_{K}H^{3-i}(X_{K,\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{N}^{i})\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
and its cuspidal counterpart both contain a unique direct summand isomorphic to
\Pi_{f}^{\prime}
; and if
j\neq 3-i
, then the
\Pi_{f}^{\prime}
-generalised eigenspaces for the spherical Hecke operators in
H^{j}(X_{K,\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{N}^{i})
and
H^{j}(X_{K,\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{N}^{i}(-D))
are zero.

0.11.2.
Classical Klingen-level Hecke operators

Taking the level at
p
to be the Klingen parahoric
\Kl(p)
, we obtain an action of the local Hecke algebra
\mathbf{Z}[G(\mathbf{Q}_{p})\sslash\Kl(p)]
on the cohomology of the sheaves
\mathcal{N}^{i}
.

Definition 0.11.2.1
.

We define the following operators:

\displaystyle U_{\Kl,0}

\displaystyle=p^{-(r_{1}+r_{2})}\cdot\left[\Kl(p)\diag(p,p,p,p)\Kl(p)\right]

\displaystyle U_{\Kl,1}

\displaystyle=\left[\Kl(p)\diag(p,p,1,1)\Kl(p)\right]

\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{\Kl,1}

\displaystyle=\left[\Kl(p)\diag(1,1,p,p)\Kl(p)\right]

\displaystyle U_{\Kl,2}

\displaystyle=p^{-r_{2}}\cdot\left[\Kl(p)\diag(p^{2},p,p,1)\Kl(p)\right]

\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{\Kl,2}

\displaystyle=p^{-r_{2}}\cdot\left[\Kl(p)\diag(1,p,p,p^{2})\Kl(p)\right].

Remark 0.11.2.2
.

The powers of
p
are chosen so that these operators are
minimally integrally normalised
– that is, they are minimal such that the relevant cohomological correspondences extend to the integral model of
X_{\Kl}
over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. In particular, all their eigenvalues are
p
-adically integral, which recovers the estimates on the valuations of the Hecke parameters of
\Pi
given above. Of course, the eigenvalues of
U_{\Kl,0}
are roots of unity, and we shall generally use the more familiar alternative notation
\langle p\rangle
for
U_{\Kl,0}
.

Note 0.11.2.3
.

The operators
\{\langle p\rangle,U_{\Kl,1},U_{\Kl,2}\}
generate a commutative subalgebra of the Hecke algebra, and
\{\langle p\rangle,U^{\prime}_{\Kl,1},U^{\prime}_{\Kl,2}\}
generate another commutative subalgebra. Moreover, Serre duality interchanges these two subalgebras: more precisely, the transpose with respect to Serre duality of
U_{\Kl,1}
is
\langle p\rangle^{-1}U^{\prime}_{\Kl,1}
, and the transpose of
U_{\Kl,2}
is
\langle p\rangle^{-2}U^{\prime}_{\Kl,2}
.

0.11.3.
Restriction to the rank
\geqslant 1
locus

Recall that
X_{\Kl}
parametrises pairs
(A,C)
, where
A
is a semi-abelian surface with some prime-to-
p
level structure and degeneration data at the cusps, and
C
is a cyclic subgroup of order
p
. The
Fargues degree

\deg C
is thus a function

\deg:X_{\Kl}(\mathbf{C}_{p})\rTo\left[0,1\right],

with degree 1 corresponding to the locus where
C
is multiplicative.

The images of
(A,C)
under the correspondence
U_{\Kl,r}^{\prime}
, for
r=1,2
, correspond to pairs
(A^{\prime},C^{\prime})
, where
\phi:A\to A^{\prime}
is an isogeny (of some specific type depending on
r
) whose kernel contains
C
, and
C^{\prime}
a cyclic subgroup of
A^{\prime}[p]
such that
\phi^{\vee}(C^{\prime})=C
. This implies that
\deg C^{\prime}\leqslant\deg C
; so
U_{\Kl,r}^{\prime}
restricts to a correspondence
X_{G,\Kl}(\mathbf{C}_{p})_{[0,1[}\to X_{G,\Kl}(\mathbf{C}_{p})_{[0,1[}
. This implies that there is a well-defined action of
U_{\Kl,r}^{\prime}
on the compactly-supported cohomology
R\Gamma_{c}(\mathcal{X}_{\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{N}^{i})
for any
i
, compatible with the extension-by-zero map to
R\Gamma(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl},\mathcal{N}^{i})
.

Proposition 0.11.3.1
.

All slopes of
U_{\Kl,2}^{\prime}
on
R\Gamma_{c}(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{N}^{i}(-D))
are
\geqslant(r_{1}-r_{2}+1)
if
i=2
, and
\geqslant(r_{1}+r_{2}+3)
if
i=3
.

Proof.

See
[
Pil20
]
.
∎

0.11.4.
The ordinary locus and the operator
Z^{\prime}

Inside
\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
we have the ordinary locus
\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}
, parametrising
(A,C)
where
A
is ordinary and
C
multiplicative. The correspondences
U_{\Kl,1}^{\prime}
and
U_{\Kl,2}^{\prime}
described above both act on
R\Gamma_{c0}(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}},\mathcal{N}^{i})
, since ordinarity is an isogeny invariant. However, over the ordinary locus there is an additional structure: we have a decomposition

U_{\Kl,1}^{\prime}=Z^{\prime}+\Phi

as a sum of two simpler correspondences:

•

The correspondence
\Phi
is actually a morphism: it is the map
(A,C)\mapsto(A,C)\mapsto(A/\hat{A}[p],C^{\prime}\bmod\hat{A}[p])
where
\hat{A}
is the formal group of
A
, and
C^{\prime}
is the unique subgroup of
\hat{A}[p^{2}]
such that
pC^{\prime}=C
. This is a lifting of the Frobenius map on the special fibre.

•

The correspondence
Z^{\prime}
parametrises isogenies
(A,C)\mapsto(A/J,C^{\prime})
, where
J\cap\hat{A}[p]=C
, and
C^{\prime}
is the unique multiplicative subgroup of
A^{\prime}
whose image under the dual isogeny is
C
.

These are related to classical correspondences at Iwahori level (since we can also see
\mathcal{X}_{\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}}
as a dagger subvariety of the Iwahori-level Shimura variety, via the canonical-subgroup map): in the Iwahori-level Hecke algebra,
Z^{\prime}
corresponds to
\diag(1,p,1,p)
, and
\Phi
to
(1,1,p,p)
.

Remark 0.11.4.1
.

For the sheaf
\mathcal{N}^{1}
, this is the minimal integral normalisation of
Z^{\prime}
(but this is no longer the case on
\mathcal{N}^{i}
for
i\neq 1
). We have not attempted to give an optimal normalisation for the operator
\Phi
, since this will not play such a major role in our theory.

Note 0.11.4.2
.

If
U_{\Iw,2}^{\prime}
denotes the restriction of
U_{\Kl,2}^{\prime}
, then this operator commutes with both
Z^{\prime}
and
\Phi
, and we have the identity

(0.11.1)

Z^{\prime}\circ\Phi=p^{(r_{2}+1)}U_{\operatorname{Iw},2}^{\prime}.

Convention
.

Since the operators
U^{\prime}_{\operatorname{Kl},2}
and
U^{\prime}_{\operatorname{Iw},2}
are compatible under pullback, there seems to be no harm in dropping the subscript and using the notation
U^{\prime}_{2}
for both.

0.11.5.
Duality and vanishing for coherent cohomology

Proposition 0.11.5.1
.

Both
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{\mathrm{ord}}
and
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{=1}
are smooth, and their images in the minimal compactification
X_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{min}}
are affine.

Proof.

The smoothness of
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{\mathrm{ord}}
is immediate from that of
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{m}
. It is easily seen that the space
X_{G,\Kl,0}^{=1}
maps isomorphically to the
p
-rank 1 locus in prime-to-
p
level (cf. proof of Lemma 10.5.2.2 in
[
Pil17
]
), and the smoothness of this image is established in the course of the proof of Lemma 6.4.2 of
op.cit.
. For the second statement, see the proof of Theorem 11.2.1 of
[
Pil17
]
.
∎

Notation 0.11.5.2
.

Let
\pi:X_{G,\Kl}\to X_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{min}}
be the projection map. For the rest of this section, let
\mathcal{E}=[W]
be the canonical extension to
X_{G,\Kl}
of an automorphic vector bundle attached to a
P_{\Sieg}
-representation
W
, and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=[W^{\vee}\otimes L(3,3;0)]
, so that the Serre dual of
\mathcal{E}
is
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(-D)
and vice versa.

Proposition 0.11.5.3
.

Let
U\subset X_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{min}}
be affinoid, and let
\tilde{U}=\pi^{-1}(U)\subseteq X_{G,\Kl}
. Then

(a)

We have
H^{i}(\tilde{U},\mathcal{E}(-D))=0
for
i\neq 0
.

(b)

We have
H^{i}_{c}(\tilde{U},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})=0
for
i\neq 3
.

(c)

There is a perfect pairing of Hausdorff locally convex spaces

H^{0}(\tilde{U},\mathcal{E}(-D))\times H^{3}_{c}(\tilde{U},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\to\mathbf{Q}_{p}.

Proof.

Note that if
\tilde{U}
is affinoid, then this is an instance of the Serre duality results for affinoid dagger spaces proved in
[
GK98
, §7.2]
. So we shall aim to reduce to this case, using the fact that
R^{i}\pi_{\star}\left(\mathcal{E}(-D)\right)=0
for all
i>0
by
[
Lan17
, Theorem 8.6]
.

For part (a), Lan’s vanishing result shows that
H^{i}(\tilde{U},\mathcal{E}(-D))=H^{i}\big(U,\pi_{\star}\left(\mathcal{E}(-D)\right)\big)
. Since
U
is affinoid and
\pi_{\star}\left(\mathcal{E}(-D)\right)
is coherent, this vanishes for all
i>0
as required.

For parts (b) and (c), we argue as follows. Since
\tilde{U}
is smooth and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
locally free, we have

\underline{\operatorname{RHom}}_{\mathcal{O}(\tilde{U})}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\underline{\omega}_{\tilde{U}})=\underline{\Hom}_{\mathcal{O}(\tilde{U})}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\omega_{\tilde{U}}[3])=\mathcal{E}(-D)[3].

So we have

\displaystyle H^{i}_{c}(\tilde{U},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}^{i}_{c}(U,R\pi_{\star}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}^{i-3}_{c}(U,\underline{\operatorname{RHom}}(R\pi_{\star}(\mathcal{E}(-D)),\underline{\omega}_{U}))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}^{i-3}_{c}(U,\underline{\operatorname{RHom}}(\pi_{\star}(\mathcal{E}(-D)),\underline{\omega}_{U}))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathrm{cts}}\left(H^{3-i}(U,\pi_{\star}(\mathcal{E}(-D))),\mathbf{Q}_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathrm{cts}}\left(H^{3-i}(\tilde{U},\mathcal{E}(-D)),\mathbf{Q}_{p}\right).

This clearly implies (c), and (b) follows from this together with (a).
∎

Corollary 0.11.5.4
.

For
\mathcal{E}
as above, we have
H^{i}(X_{\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{E}(-D))=0
for
i\notin\{0,1\}
, and
H^{i}_{c}(X_{\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{E})=0
for
i\notin\{2,3\}
.

Proof.

There are two affinoids
U_{1},U_{2}
in
X_{G,\Kl}^{\min}
such that
\pi^{-1}(U_{1})
and
\pi^{-1}(U_{2})
cover
X_{\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
. By the previous proposition, we see that
H^{\bullet}(X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{E}(-D))
is computed by a C̆ech complex concentrated in degrees 0 and 1. Similarly, the compactly-supported cohomology is supported by a “homological” C̆ech complex concentrated in degrees 2 and 3.
∎

Corollary 0.11.5.5
.

For
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
as above, we have
H^{i}_{c0}(X_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})=0
unless
i\in\{2,3\}
.

Proof.

By definition, we have an exact triangle

R\Gamma_{c}(X_{G,\Kl}^{=1},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\to R\Gamma_{c}(X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\to R\Gamma_{c0}(X_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\to[+1].

We claim that
H^{*}_{c}(X_{G,\Kl}^{=1},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
is concentrated in degree 3. The image of
X_{G,\Kl}^{=1}
in the minimal compactification is the locus where the Hasse invariant has positive valuation. It is thus naturally covered by an increasing sequence of affinoids
U_{i}
(given by requiring the valuation of a lift of Hasse to be
\geqslant r_{i}
, for some sequence of positive rationals
r_{i}\to 0
), and
R\Gamma_{c}(X_{G,\Kl}^{=1},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})=\varinjlim_{r}R\Gamma_{c}(\pi^{-1}(U_{i}),\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
, which vanishes outside degree 3 by the proposition above. It now follows from the mapping triangle that
R\Gamma_{c0}
is supported in degrees
\{2,3\}
.
∎

Remark 0.11.5.6
.

It seems highly likely that
R\Gamma_{c0}(X_{G,\Kl}^{\mathrm{ord}},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
vanishes in degree 3 as well, but this is not easy to check. It is equivalent to showing that
H^{3}_{c}(X_{G,\Kl}^{=1},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\to H^{3}_{c}(X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
is surjective. If we knew that Serre duality held for
X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
this would be obvious, since the dual map
H^{0}(X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{E}(-D))\to H^{0}(X_{G,\Kl}^{=1},\mathcal{E}(-D))
is clearly injective; but we do not know this, since neither
X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}
nor its image in
X_{G,\Kl}^{\min}
is affinoid.

0.11.6.
Coherent
H^{2}
eigenclasses from
\Pi

The input we need from higher Coleman theory is the following. We fix an automorphic representation
\Pi
which is cohomological with coefficients in
V(r_{1},r_{2};r_{1}+r_{2})
, and unramified and Klingen-ordinary at
p
, as before; and we choose a vector
\eta^{\mathrm{alg}}_{-D}\in H^{2}\left(X_{G,\Kl},\mathcal{N}^{1}(-D)\right)[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}]
which is stable under
\Kl(p)
and lies in the ordinary eigenspace for
U^{\prime}_{2}
.

Remark 0.11.6.1
.

If
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
are the Hecke parameters of
\Pi^{\prime}_{p}
, ordered such that
v_{p}(\alpha)\leqslant\dots\leqslant v_{p}(\delta)
and normalised such that
v_{p}(\alpha)\geqslant 0,v_{p}(\alpha\beta)\geqslant r_{2}+1
, then the ordinarity condition is that
v_{p}(\alpha\beta)
should be exactly
r_{2}+1
, and the ordinary
U^{\prime}_{2}
eigenvalue is the
p
-adic unit
\lambda=\frac{\alpha\beta}{p^{r_{2}+1}}
.

Note 0.11.6.2
.

The operator
U_{\Kl,1}^{\prime}
acts on
\eta
as multiplication by
\alpha+\beta
(which may or may not be a
p
-adic unit).

Proposition 0.11.6.3
.

There exists a unique class
\eta^{\geqslant 1}_{\coh,-D}\in H^{2}_{c}\left(\mathcal{X}_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1},\mathcal{N}^{1}(-D)\right)
with the following two properties:

(1)

U^{\prime}_{\Kl,2}
acts on
\eta^{\geqslant 1}_{\coh,-D}
