0.16.
Evaluation of the pairing

0.16.1.
Reduction to coherent cohomology

We will now evaluate the pairing (
0.15.1
) .

By the herb–chopper diagram (or rather: by the analogous diagram for
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{H,\Delta}
) and the compatibility of the pairings under change of polynomial, (
0.15.1
) is equal to

(0.16.1)

\left\langle(\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta})^{*}\left(\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\rigfp,q}\right),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{Eis}}^{t_{1}+2,\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\crit}_{\rigfp,\cusp,\mathrm{ord}}\sqcup\widetilde{\mathrm{Eis}}^{t_{2}+2,\Phi_{2}^{(p)},\crit}_{\rigfp,\cusp,\mathrm{ord}}\right\rangle_{\widetilde{\rigfp},\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{H,\Delta}}.

Lemma 0.16.1.1
.

The pairing (
0.16.1
) is equal to

(0.16.2)

\left\langle\left(\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta}\right)^{*}(\breve{\zeta},\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q}),\,\left(\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1},\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi_{1}^{(p)},\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}_{2}\right)\right\rangle_{\coh-\mathrm{fp},\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{H,\Delta}},

where
(\breve{\zeta},\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})
is as defined in Proposition
0.12.4.5
.

Proof.

Immediate from Proposition
0.12.4.5
and Lemmas
0.13.2.1
and
0.15.6.1
.
∎

To evaluate this pairing, we use Besser’s formalism for computing the cup product, as explained in Section
0.13.2
. Let

a(x,y)=p^{t_{1}+t_{2}+2}\langle p\rangle^{-1}_{H}y

and

b(x,y)=\frac{P_{q}\left(p^{t_{1}+t_{2}+2}\langle p\rangle^{-1}_{H}xy\right)-p^{t_{1}+t_{2}+2}\langle p\rangle^{-1}_{H}\,yP_{q}(x)}{1-p^{t_{1}+t_{2}+2}\langle p\rangle^{-1}_{H}\,y},

so we have

P_{q}\star Q(xy)=a(x,y)P_{q}(x)+b(x,y)\left(1-p^{t_{1}+t_{2}+2}\langle p\rangle^{-1}_{H}\,y\right).

Then (
0.16.2
) is equal to

\ a(\varphi_{H,1}^{*}\otimes 1,1\otimes\varphi_{H}^{*})\,\left[(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\zeta)\cup\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2}\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{2}\right]+b(\varphi_{H,1}^{*}\otimes 1,1\otimes\varphi_{H}^{*})\,\left[(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh})\cup\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}\right].

Proposition 0.16.1.2
.

We have

\text{\eqref{reduction1}}=b(\varphi_{H,1}^{*}\otimes 1,1\otimes\varphi_{H}^{*})\,\left[(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}\right].

Proof.

We need to show that

(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\zeta)\cup\varphi_{H}^{*}\alpha^{(t_{1},t_{2},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})}_{2}=0.

Now
U_{p}\boxtimes U_{p}\circ\varphi_{H}^{*}=\langle p\rangle_{H}
, so

(0.16.3)

(U_{p}\boxtimes U_{p})\left[(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\zeta)\cup\varphi_{H}^{*}\,\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{2}\right]=(U_{p}\boxtimes U_{p})(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\zeta)\cup\langle p\rangle_{H}\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{2}.

But
(U_{p}\boxtimes U_{p})(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\zeta)=0
by Lemma
0.12.4.4
and Proposition
Corollary

0.12.5.3
, and hence (
0.16.3
) is zero. Now by Note
0.13.1.5
, the operator
U_{p}\boxtimes U_{p}
acts as multiplication by
p^{-2}
on
H^{3,2}_{c}(\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}^{\mathrm{ord}}\langle-\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}\rangle,\mathbf{Q}_{p},3;P\star Q)
and hence is invertible. This finishes the proof.
∎

Write
P(x)=1+c_{1}x+c_{2}x^{2}
; by definition, we have
c_{2}=(\alpha\beta)^{-1}
and
c_{1}=-\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\alpha\beta}
. Then

b(x,y)=1-c_{2}\,p^{t_{1}+t_{2}+2}\langle p\rangle_{H}^{-1}\,x^{2}y.

We now identify
\varphi_{H,1}^{*}
with
p^{-1}\varphi_{H}^{*}
.

Corollary 0.16.1.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\text{\eqref{reduction1}}=\quad

\displaystyle(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\alpha^{(t_{1},t_{2},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})}_{1}-c_{2}p^{t_{1}+t_{2}}\langle p\rangle_{H}^{-1}\cdot\varphi_{H}^{*}\left[(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}\varphi_{H}^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}\right]

(0.16.4)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{0}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}\right)

(0.16.5)

\displaystyle-c_{2}\,p^{t_{1}+t_{2}}\langle p\rangle_{H}^{-1}\,\varphi_{H}^{*}\left[(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}\varphi_{H}^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{0}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}\right)\right]

(0.16.6)

\displaystyle+p^{t_{1}}\,(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\,\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{1}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{0}\right)

(0.16.7)

\displaystyle-c_{2}\,p^{2t_{1}+t_{2}+1}\langle p\rangle_{H}^{-1}\,\varphi_{H}^{*}\left[(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}\varphi_{H}^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\,\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{1}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{0}\right)\right].

We will see that this expression simplifies.

Lemma 0.16.1.4
.

We have

\displaystyle\varphi_{H}^{*}\left[(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}\varphi_{H}^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{0}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\varphi_{H}^{*}\left[(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}\varphi_{G}^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{1}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=0

Proof.

Apply
(U_{p}\boxtimes U_{p})^{2}
and use Note
0.13.1.5
and the fact that

by Lemma
0.15.5.3
.
∎

We hence deduce the following formula for the pairing:

Proposition 0.16.1.5
.

We have

\displaystyle\text{\eqref{reduction1}}=

\displaystyle(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{0}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+p^{t_{1}+t_{2}}\,(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\,\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{1}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{0}\right)

We now apply Corollary
0.14.2.4
:

Note 0.16.1.6
.

For
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant t_{1}+t_{2}
. A basis of
\Gr^{\ell}V_{H}
is given by

\{v^{t_{1}-i_{1}}w^{i_{1}}\boxtimes v^{t_{2}-i_{2}}w^{i_{2}}:0\leqslant i_{n}\leqslant t_{n},\ i_{1}+i_{2}=t_{1}+t_{2}-\ell\}.

Lemma 0.16.1.7
.

The image of
\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{0}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}
under projection to
\Gr^{r_{1}-q}\mathcal{V}_{H}
is given by

(-1)^{r_{2}-q}\frac{t_{1}!}{(r_{1}-r_{2}-r)!}\times\theta^{(r_{1}-r_{2}-r)}E^{-t_{1}}_{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}}}\cdot\,v^{r_{1}-r_{2}-r}w^{r_{2}-q}\boxtimes F^{t_{2}+2}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{2}\Phi_{\crit}}\cdot(v^{t_{2}}\otimes\xi\otimes e_{1}).

Proof.

The basis vectors with non-trivial coefficients of
\epsilon^{t_{1}+2,\Phi_{1}}_{0}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2}+2,\Phi_{2}}_{1}
are of the form

v^{t_{1}-i_{1}}w^{i_{1}}\boxtimes w^{t_{2}}\qquad 0\leqslant i\leqslant t_{1}.

By Note
0.16.1.6
, this will project non-trivially to
\Gr^{r_{1}-q}\mathcal{V}_{H}
if and only if

i_{1}=t_{1}-(r_{1}-q)=r.

∎

We analogously prove the following result:

Lemma 0.16.1.8
.

The image of
\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\,\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{1}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{0}
in
\Gr^{r_{1}-q}\mathcal{V}_{H}
is given by

(-1)^{r_{2}-q}\frac{t_{2}!}{r!}\times\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\left(F^{t_{1}+2}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{1}\Phi_{\crit}}\cdot\,(v^{t_{1}}\otimes\xi\otimes e_{1})\right)\boxtimes\left(\theta^{r}E^{-t_{2}}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{2}\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}}}\cdot v^{r}w^{r_{2}-q}\right).

Proposition 0.16.1.9
.

We have

\displaystyle(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}

\displaystyle(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{0}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r_{2}-q}\,t_{1}!}{(r_{1}-r_{2}-q)!}\times\frac{(-2)^{q}}{\tbinom{t_{1}}{r_{2}-q}}\times\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q},\,\iota^{p-adic}_{\star}\left(\theta^{(r_{1}-r_{2}-r)}E^{-t_{1}}_{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}}}\sqcup F^{t_{2}+2}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{2}\Phi_{\crit}}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r_{2}-q}(-2)^{q}\,(r_{2}-q)!\times\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q},\,\iota^{p-adic}_{\star}\left(\theta^{(r_{1}-r_{2}-r)}E^{-t_{1}}_{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}}}\sqcup F^{t_{2}+2}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{2}\Phi_{\crit}}\right)\right\rangle,

and

\displaystyle(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\,\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{1}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{(-1)^{r_{2}-q}\,t_{2}!}{r!}\times\frac{(-2)^{q}}{\tbinom{t_{2}}{r_{2}-q}}\times\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q},\,\iota^{p-adic}_{\star}\left(\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\left(F^{t_{1}+2}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{1}\Phi_{\crit}}\right)\boxtimes\theta^{r}E^{-t_{2}}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{2}\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}}}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=(-1)^{r_{2}-q}(-2)^{q}(r_{2}-q)!\times\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q},\,\iota^{p-adic}_{\star}\left(\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\left(F^{t_{1}+2}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{1}\Phi_{\crit}}\right)\boxtimes\theta^{r}E^{-t_{2}}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{2}\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}}}\right)\right\rangle.

0.16.2.
Families of Eisenstein series

We now interpret the cup-products of
Proposition

0.16.1.9
in terms of the 2-parameter
p
-adic family of Eisenstein series studied in
[
LPSZ19
]
, and a 1-parameter “critical” variant.

Proposition 0.16.2.1
.

If
\Phi^{(p)}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{p},(\chi^{(p)})^{-1})
, then the
p
-adic Eisenstein series
E^{-k}_{\Phi^{(p)}\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}}}
is the specialisation at
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})=(0,-1-k)
of a
2
-parameter family of Eisenstein series
\mathcal{E}^{\Phi^{(p)}}(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})
. This family has
q
-expansion

\sum_{u,v\in(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)})^{2},\,uv>0}\mathrm{sgn}(u)u^{\kappa_{1}}v^{\kappa_{2}}(\Phi^{(p)})^{\prime}(u,v)q^{uv},

and its specialisation at
(a+\mu,b+\nu)
, for integers
a,b\geqslant 0
and finite-order characters
\mu,\nu
of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, is the
p
-adic modular form associated to the algebraic nearly-holomorphic modular form

(g,\tau)\mapsto\nu(\det g)\cdot E^{(a+b+1,\Phi_{\mu,\nu})}\left(g,\tau;\chi^{(p)}\mu^{-1}\nu,\frac{b-a+1}{2}\right).

Here,
\Phi_{\mathrm{dep},\mu,\nu}
is defined as in
[
LPSZ19
, §7.3]
.

Proof.

See
[
LPSZ19
, Theorem 7.6]
.
∎

We can also put critical-slope Eisenstein series into
1
-parameter
p
-adic families:

Proposition 0.16.2.2
.

Let
\ell\geqslant 0
. Then there exists a
1
-parameter family of Eisenstein series
\mathcal{E}^{\Phi^{(p)}}(\underline{\ell},\kappa)
with
q
-expansion

\sum_{u\in\mathbf{Z}_{p},\,v\in(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)})^{2},\,uv>0}\mathrm{sgn}(u)u^{\ell}v^{\kappa}\grave{\Phi}^{(p)}(u,v)q^{uv}.

(Here, we underline the parameter which does
not
vary in a
p
-adic family.)
Its specialisation at
a+\nu
, for an integer
a\geqslant 0
and a finite-order character
\nu
of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, is the
p
-adic modular form associated to the algebraic nearly-holomorphic modular form

(g,\tau)\mapsto\nu(\det g)\cdot E^{(\ell+b+1,\Phi^{p}\Phi_{\crit,\nu})}\left(g,\tau;\chi^{(p)}\nu,\frac{b-\ell+1}{2}\right),

where
\Phi_{\crit,\nu}^{\prime}(x,y)=\mathrm{ch}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})(x,y)\cdot\nu(y)
.

We can now restate
Proposition

0.16.1.9
in the following form:

Proposition 0.16.2.3
.

Let us define

(0.16.8)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh},\,\iota_{\star}^{p-adic}\left(\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}}(r^{\prime},-1-q^{\prime})\boxtimes\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}(\underline{q^{\prime}+r+1},0)\right)\right\rangle,

(0.16.9)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh},\,\iota_{\star}^{p-adic}\left(\langle p\rangle^{-1}\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}}(\underline{q^{\prime}+r^{\prime}+1},0)\boxtimes\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}(r,-1-q^{\prime})\right)\right\rangle,

where
q^{\prime}=r_{2}-q
and
r^{\prime}=r_{1}-r_{2}-r
(so
q^{\prime},r^{\prime}\geqslant 0
). Then the cup-product (
0.15.1
) is equal to

\frac{(-1)^{r_{2}-q}(-2)^{q}\,(r_{2}-q)!}{\left(1-\tfrac{\gamma}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\delta}{p^{1+q}}\right)}\times\left(\mathscr{L}_{1}+p^{r_{1}+r_{2}-2q}\mathscr{L}_{2}\right).

Proof.

In the above notation, the two terms appearing in
0.16.1.9
are

\displaystyle(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\epsilon^{t_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{1}}_{0}\sqcup\epsilon^{t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1}\right)=(-1)^{r_{2}-q}(-2)^{q}\,(r_{2}-q)!\times\mathscr{L}_{1},

\displaystyle(\iota^{(t_{1},t_{2})}_{\Delta})^{*}(\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\cup\left(\langle p\rangle_{\GL_{2}}^{-1}\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\left(F^{t_{1}+2}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{1}\Phi_{\crit}}\right)\sqcup\theta^{r_{2}-q}E^{-t_{2}}_{\Phi^{(p)}_{2}\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}}}\right)=(-1)^{r_{2}-q}(-2)^{q}(r_{2}-q)!\times\mathscr{L}_{2}.

The normalisation of the trace map on finite-polynomial cohomology gives rise to a factor
P_{q}\left(p^{t_{1}+t_{2}+1}\chi_{\Pi}(p)\right)
, and using the relation
\alpha\delta=\beta\gamma=p^{r_{1}+r_{2}+3}\chi_{\Pi}(p)
, we deduce that

P_{q}\left(p^{t_{1}+t_{2}+1}\chi_{\Pi}(p)\right)=\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p^{1+q}}\right).\qed

We will see shortly that
\mathscr{L}_{2}
is in fact zero, and that
\mathscr{L}_{1}
coincides with a non-critical
p
-adic
L
-value. We first make a preliminary reduction.

Proposition 0.16.2.4
.

We have

\mathscr{L}_{1}=-\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh},\iota^{p-adic}_{\star}\left[\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}}(r_{1}-q+1,r)\boxtimes\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}(\underline{0},-1-q^{\prime}-r)\right]\right\rangle.

and similarly

\mathscr{L}_{2}=(-1)^{r_{1}-r_{2}+1}\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh},\,\iota_{\star}^{p-adic}\left(\langle p\rangle^{-1}\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}}(\underline{0},-1-q^{\prime}-r^{\prime})\boxtimes\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}(r_{1}-q+1,r^{\prime})\right)\right\rangle

Proof.

Both of these statements follow from the general fact that

(0.16.10)

\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh},\iota^{p-adic}_{\star}\left[\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\theta(\mathcal{G})+\theta(\mathcal{F})\boxtimes\mathcal{G}\right]\right\rangle=0

for any nearly-overconvergent
p
-adic modular forms
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
whose weights sum to
r_{1}-r_{2}
. (This, in turn, follows from the fact that
\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\theta(\mathcal{G})+\theta(\mathcal{F})\boxtimes\mathcal{G}
is the projection to a graded piece of the Hodge filtration of an overconvergent vector-valued form in the image of
\nabla
, which must pair to 0 with
\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh}
, since
\nabla(\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh})=0
).
∎

0.16.3.
Evaluation of
\mathscr{L}_{1}

We shall now evaluate
\mathscr{L}_{1}
. We shall do this by interpreting this value as the specialisation at the trivial character of a measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, whose values at certain other characters (corresponding to
critical

L
-values) can be compared with the
p
-adic
L
-function of
[
LPSZ19
]
.

Definition 0.16.3.1
.

Define an element of
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
by

\mathscr{L}_{1}(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})\coloneqq\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh},\iota^{p-adic}_{\star}\left[\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}}(r_{1}-r_{2}-\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})\boxtimes\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}(\underline{0},\mathbf{j}_{1}-\mathbf{j}_{2})\right]\right\rangle.

Proposition 0.16.3.2
.

We have

\mathscr{L}_{1}(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})=\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh},\iota^{p-adic}_{\star}\left[\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}}(r_{1}-r_{2}-\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})\boxtimes\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}(0,\mathbf{j}_{1}-\mathbf{j}_{2})\right]\right\rangle

(without the underline).

Note that the Eisenstein series in the second formula is exactly the
\mathcal{E}(\underline{\Phi}^{p})
appearing in Proposition
0.6.9.5
(compare the choices of parameters above with the formulae of
[
LPSZ19
, §7.4]
).

Proof.

It suffices to prove that these two measures agree after specialising at
(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})=(a_{1}+\rho_{1},a_{2}+\rho_{2})
with
\rho_{i}
finite-order characters and
r_{1}-r_{2}\geqslant a_{1}\geqslant a_{2}\geqslant 0
. In this range, both sides of the claimed formula reduce to cup-products in classical coherent cohomology; and as in
[
LPSZ19
]
, they can be written as Euler products of local integrals at each place, with the factors at all primes except possibly
p
being identical. The computation of §
0.20.3
shows that the factors at
p
are also equal (despite the slightly different choice of test data). Thus the two measures are equal.
∎

Substituting
(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})=(-1-r_{2}+q,r)
, and applying Proposition
0.6.9.5
, we conclude that

\mathscr{L}_{1}=-\frac{\widetilde{Z}^{p}(w^{p},\underline{\Phi}^{p})}{\vol(V)}\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi,-1-r_{2}+q,r).

0.16.4.
Vanishing of
\mathscr{L}_{2}

In order to show that
\mathscr{L}_{2}
is identically zero, we will use a similar deformation argument. Let us write

\mathscr{L}_{2}(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})=\left\langle\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh},\,\iota_{\star}^{p-adic}\left(\langle p\rangle^{-1}\varphi_{\GL_{2}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{1}^{(p)}}(\underline{0},\mathbf{j}_{1}-\mathbf{j}_{2})\boxtimes\mathcal{E}^{\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}(r_{1}-r_{2}-\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})\right)\right\rangle.

Again, if we let
\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2}=(a_{1}+\rho_{1},a_{2}+\rho_{2})
with
r_{1}-r_{2}\geqslant a_{1}\geqslant a_{2}\geqslant 0
and
\rho_{i}
of finite order, we obtain a cup-product in classical coherent cohomology; and the value
\mathscr{L}_{2}
above corresponds (up to a sign) to specialising at
(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})=(-1-r_{2}+q,r^{\prime})
. However, for all of the specialisations corresponding to critical values, the term at
p
in the resulting product is 0, again by the computations in §
0.20.3
. So the measure
\mathscr{L}_{2}(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})
is identically 0, and hence so is its special value
\mathscr{L}_{2}
.

0.16.5.
Conclusion of the proof

Proof (of Theorem
0.6.8.5
).

The computation in this chapter shows that

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1},\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi_{1}^{(p)},\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}_{2}\right),\,\left(\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta}\right)^{*}(\breve{\zeta},\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\right\rangle_{\coh-\mathrm{fp},\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{H,\Delta}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{(-1)^{r_{2}-q+1}(-2)^{q}(r_{2}-q)!}{\left(1-\tfrac{\gamma}{p^{q+1}}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\delta}{p^{q+1}}\right)}\times\langle\eta,\iota_{\Delta,\star}(\mathcal{E}(\underline{\Phi}^{p}))\rangle_{X_{G,\Kl}^{\geqslant 1}}

in the notation of
Proposition

0.6.9.6
.

However,

\left\langle\left(\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\Phi^{(p)}_{2}}_{1},\alpha^{t_{1},t_{2},\Phi_{1}^{(p)},\Phi_{2}^{(p)}}_{2},\,\left(\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta}\right)^{*}(\breve{\zeta},\breve{\eta}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\coh,q})\right)\right\rangle_{\coh-\mathrm{fp},\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{H,\Delta}}

\quad=\left\langle\widetilde{\mathrm{Eis}}^{t_{1}+2,\Phi^{(p)}_{1},\crit}_{\rigfp,\cusp,\mathrm{ord}}\sqcup\widetilde{\mathrm{Eis}}^{t_{2}+2,\Phi_{2}^{(p)},\crit}_{\rigfp,\cusp,\mathrm{ord}},\,(\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta})^{*}\left(\eta^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\rigfp,q}\right)\right\rangle_{\widetilde{\rigfp},\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{H,\Delta}}

by (
0.16.1
)

=\left\langle\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\rigsyn,\underline{\Phi},\mathrm{ord}},\,(\iota_{\Delta}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]})^{*}(\eta_{\rigfp,q,-D}^{\geqslant 1}|_{Y^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\Kl}})\right\rangle_{\rigfp,Y^{\mathrm{ord}}_{H,\Delta}}

by Cor.
0.12.3.6

=\left\langle\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\lrigsyn,\underline{\Phi}},\,(\iota_{\Delta}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]})^{*}(\eta_{\lrigfp,q,-D})\right\rangle_{\lrigfp,Y_{\Delta}}

by Thm.
0.10.4.6

=\left\langle\iota_{*}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}(\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\syn,\underline{\Phi}}),\,\eta_{\NNfp,q,-D}\right\rangle_{\NNfp,Y_{G,\Kl}}

by Prop.
0.10.4.2

=\left\langle\left(\log\circ\pr_{\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime\vee}}\circ\iota^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\Delta,*}\right)\left(\mathrm{Eis}^{[t_{1},t_{2}]}_{\text{{\'{e}t}},\underline{\Phi}}\right),\,\eta_{\mathrm{dR}}\right\rangle_{Y_{G,\Kl}}

by (
0.7.8
).

So we have proved the equality of the two sides of (
0.6.2
); and Proposition
0.6.9.6
shows that this assertion is equivalent to
Theorem

0.6.8.5
.
∎

Step 5: Deformation to critical values

0.17.
Hida families

We will now change our focus slightly: rather than working with a single, fixed automorphic representation
\Pi
, we shall consider
p
-adic families of these objects. In order to avoid fiddly issues involving choices of test vectors at ramified primes, we shall suppose for simplicity that
\Pi
has level 1 from here onwards (i.e. that
\Pi_{\ell}
is unramified for all finite primes
\ell
). Note that this implies that
r_{1}-r_{2}
is even, and that the central character
\chi_{\Pi}
is trivial.

0.17.1.
Families of Galois representations

Notation 0.17.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
denote the
p
-adic weight space
\Hom(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},\mathbf{G}_{m,L}^{\mathrm{rig}})
(the analytification of the formal scheme
\operatorname{Spf}\mathcal{O}_{L}[[\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}]]
). For
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
we write
\mathcal{W}^{\epsilon}
for the union of components classifying characters with
\kappa(-1)=\epsilon
, so
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}^{+1}\sqcup\mathcal{W}^{-1}
.

Definition 0.17.1.2
.

Let
U
be an affinoid disc in
\mathcal{W}
containing 0. By a
Siegel-type Hida family

\underline{\Pi}
over
U
of tame level
1
(passing through weight
(r_{1},r_{2})
), we shall mean the following data:

•

for each
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, a cuspidal automorphic representation
\Pi(n)
of
\GSp_{4}
which is globally generic, cohomological at
\infty
with coefficients in
V(r_{1}+n,r_{2}+n)
, and has level
1
;

•

for each such
n
, an embedding of the coefficient field of
\Pi(n)
into
L
, with respect to which
\Pi(n)
is Siegel-ordinary at
p
;

•

a collection of rigid-analytic functions
\mathbf{t}_{i,\ell}\in\mathcal{O}(U)
, for
i=1,2
and
\ell\neq p
, such that for each
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, the values of
\mathbf{t}_{1,\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}_{2,\ell}
at
n
are the eigenvalues of the spherical Hecke operators
\diag(\ell,\ell,1,1)
and
p^{-(r_{2}+n)}\diag(\ell^{2},\ell,\ell,1)
on the arithmetic twist
\Pi^{\prime}(n)
;

•

rigid-analytic functions
\mathbf{u}_{i,p}\in\mathcal{O}(U)
for
i=1,2
, with
\mathbf{u}_{1,p}
taking
p
-adic unit values, such that for all
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, we can write the Hecke parameters of
\Pi_{p}^{\prime}(n)
as
(\alpha_{n},\beta_{n},\gamma_{n},\delta_{n})
with

\mathbf{u}_{1,p}(n)=\alpha_{n},\qquad\mathbf{u}_{2,p}(n)=\frac{\beta_{n}+\gamma_{n}}{p^{(r_{2}+1+n)}}.

The following theorem is fundamental:

Theorem 0.17.1.3

(Tilouine–Urban)
.

For any
\Pi
which satisfies the conditions of §
0.6.2
and is unramified and Siegel-ordinary at
p
, there exists a disc
U\subset\mathcal{W}
around 0, and an ordinary family of eigensystems
\underline{\Pi}
over
U
, such that
\Pi(0)=\Pi
.

Remark 0.17.1.4
.

Note that Klingen-ordinarity is not needed for this theorem, nor for the constructions below, until Corollary
0.17.3.11
. However, Siegel-ordinarity is fundamental here (whereas it plays no role in the main body of the paper).

The computations of
op.cit.
also give rise to a natural
\mathcal{O}(U)
-module with an action of Hecke operators, which interpolates the
\Pi^{\prime}(n)
-eigenspace in Betti cohomology of level
G(\widehat{\mathbf{Z}})
(with coefficients varying with
n
). One can equally work with étale cohomology, to obtain the following:

Theorem 0.17.1.5
.

In the situation of
Theorem

0.17.1.3
, after possibly shrinking
U
, there exists a free rank 4
\mathcal{O}(U)
-module
W_{\underline{\Pi}}
, whose fibre at
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
is canonically isomorphic to the Galois representation
W_{\Pi(n)}
.

Note 0.17.1.6
.

More precisely, the fibre at
n
of
W_{\underline{\Pi}}
is canonically identified with the subspace of étale cohomology of level
G(\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(p)})\times\Sieg(p)
on which the prime-to-
p
Hecke operators act via the eigensystem of
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}(n)
and
U_{1,\Sieg}
acts as
\alpha_{n}=\mathbf{u}_{1,p}(n)
. This, in turn, is canonically identified with the
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}(n)
-eigenspace at prime-to-
p
level via the map

\myPr_{\alpha_{n}}^{\star}:H^{3}_{\mathrm{et}}\big(G(\widehat{\mathbf{Z}})\big)[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}]\xhookrightarrow{\ \ \ }H^{3}_{\mathrm{et}}\big(G(\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(p)})\times\Sieg(p)\big)[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}]{\mathrel{\to}^{p_{\alpha_{n}}}}H^{3}_{\mathrm{et}}\big(G(\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(p)})\times\Sieg(p)\big)[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime},U_{1,\Sieg}=\alpha_{n}]

where
p_{\alpha_{n}}
denotes the Hecke operator
(1-\frac{\beta_{n}}{U_{1,\Sieg}})(1-\frac{\gamma_{n}}{U_{1,\Sieg}})(1-\frac{\delta_{n}}{U_{1,\Sieg}})
.
(Here we have written
H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(K)
as a shorthand for
H^{3}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(Y_{G}(K)_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}},\mathcal{V})
where
\mathcal{V}
is the appropriate étale coefficient sheaf).

0.17.2.
Two-variable Euler system classes

We now construct families of Euler system classes taking values in
W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}
.

Notation 0.17.2.1
.

Write

\mathcal{E}_{\Sieg}(\Pi,q,r)\coloneqq\left(1-\tfrac{p^{q}}{\alpha}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\beta}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\gamma}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\delta}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{p^{(r_{2}+r+1)}}{\alpha}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\delta}{p^{(r_{2}+r+2)}}\right),

and similarly
\mathcal{E}_{\Sieg}(\Pi(n),q,r)
for
n\geqslant 0
(with
r_{2}
in the last two factors replaced by
r_{2}+n
).

Theorem 0.17.2.2
.

Let
0\leqslant r\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
be a given integer, and let
c_{1},c_{2}>1
be integers coprime to
6pN
. Then there exists a class

{}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw}\in H^{1}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{p^{\infty}}),W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star})

with the following property: for each
(n,q)
with
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
and
0\leqslant q\leqslant r_{2}+n
, we have

\mom_{n,q}\left({}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw}\right)=C_{n,q}\cdot z^{[\Pi(n),q,r]}_{\mathrm{can}},

where
C_{n,q}
denotes the quantity

\left(c_{1}^{2}-c^{-(t_{1}+n)}\right)\left(c_{2}^{2}-c_{2}^{-(t_{2}+n)}\right)\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\Sieg}(\Pi(n),q,r)}{(-2)^{q}}.

Proof.

It follows from the results of
[
LSZ17
]
that there exists a cohomology class interpolating the projections of Lemma–Flach elements
\mathcal{LE}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(\underline{\Phi}\otimes\xi)
to the
U_{1,\Sieg}^{\prime}
-ordinary part of cohomology at level
K_{G}^{p}\times\Sieg(p)
, for any prime-to-
p
level
K_{G}^{p}
, up to modifying by factors depending on
(c_{1},c_{2})
in order to kill the denominators. Here
\underline{\Phi}
and
\xi
are products of arbitrary test data away from
p
with certain specific test data at
p
determined by the construction.

If we choose the prime-to-
p
parts of
\xi
and
\underline{\Phi}
to be the spherical test data, then
\mathcal{LE}_{\text{{\'{e}t}}}(\underline{\Phi}\otimes\xi)
is invariant under the group
G(\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(p)})\times\Sieg(p)
, and its moments are given by

(\text{$c$-factor})\cdot(-2)^{-q}\left(1-\tfrac{p^{q}}{\alpha_{n}}\right)z^{[\Pi(n),q,r]}\left(w_{\sph}^{(p)}\times w_{p,\Sieg},\underline{\Phi}_{\sph}^{(p)}\times\underline{\Phi}_{p,\Sieg}\right),

where
w_{p,\Sieg}
is the image of the spherical Whittaker vector of
\Pi_{p}(n)
under
\myPr_{\alpha_{n}}^{\star}
, and
\underline{\Phi}_{p,\Sieg}=\mathrm{ch}((p\mathbf{Z}_{p}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2})
. The cohomology class in the above formula is the product of
z^{[\Pi(n),q,r]}_{\mathrm{can}}
and a local zeta-integral
\widetilde{Z}_{p}\left(w_{p,\Sieg},\underline{\Phi}_{p,\Sieg}\right)
, which is evaluated in
Proposition

0.20.2.2
below; after rescaling the test data to remove a harmless factor of
\frac{1}{(p+1)^{2}}
, we obtain the formula stated.
∎

0.17.3.
Two-variable motivic p-adic
L
-functions

We recall the following description of the Galois representation
W_{\underline{\Pi}}
. Let
\kappa_{U}:\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\to\mathcal{O}(U)^{\times}
be the canonical character over
U
(specialising to
x\mapsto x^{n}
at each
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}
).

Theorem 0.17.3.1

(Urban)
.

After possibly shrinking
U
, the module
W_{\underline{\Pi}}
has a 3-step increasing filtration stable under
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
, with graded pieces of ranks
(1,2,1)
: we can write

0=\mathscr{F}_{0}W_{\underline{\Pi}}\subset\mathscr{F}_{1}W_{\underline{\Pi}}\subset\mathscr{F}_{3}W_{\underline{\Pi}}\subset\mathscr{F}_{4}W_{\underline{\Pi}}=W_{\underline{\Pi}}

in which
\mathscr{F}_{n}
is free of rank
n
as an
\mathcal{O}(U)
-module and is a direct summand of
W_{\underline{\Pi}}
, and the subquotients

\mathscr{F}_{1}W_{\underline{\Pi}},\quad\frac{\mathscr{F}_{3}W_{\underline{\Pi}}}{\mathscr{F}_{1}W_{\underline{\Pi}}}\otimes\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{\kappa_{U}},\quad\frac{W_{\underline{\Pi}}}{\mathscr{F}_{3}W_{\underline{\Pi}}}\otimes\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{2\kappa_{U}}

are all crystalline as
\mathcal{O}(U)
-linear representations.

More precisely, the graded pieces have the following description:

•

\mathscr{F}_{1}
is unramified, with geometric Frobenius acting as multiplication by
\mathbf{u}_{1,p}\in\mathcal{O}(U)^{\times}
.

•

(\mathscr{F}_{3}/\mathscr{F}_{1})(\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{(\kappa_{U}+r_{2}+1})
has constant Hodge–Tate weights
(0,-r_{1}+r_{2}-1)
, and the trace of Frobenius on
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}\left((\mathscr{F}_{3}/\mathscr{F}_{1})(\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{(\kappa_{U}+r_{2}+1)})\right)
is
\mathbf{u}_{2,p}
.

•

(W_{\underline{\Pi}}/\mathscr{F}_{3})(\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{(2\kappa_{U}+r_{1}+r_{2}+3)})
is unramified with geometric Frobenius acting as
\chi(p)\mathbf{u}_{1,p}^{-1}
.

Proof.

The fact that such filtrations exist “pointwise”, on the fibre at
n
for each
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, is due to Urban
[
Urb05
]
. Since we know that the Galois representations interpolate over
U
, the existence of an
\mathcal{O}(U)
-linear filtration follows from the finite generation of local Galois cohomology groups for
\mathcal{O}(U)
-linear representations.
∎

Dually, we obtain a filtration on
W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}
by setting
\mathscr{F}^{i}
to be the orthogonal complement of
\mathscr{F}_{i}
.

Proposition 0.17.3.2
.

After possibly shrinking
U
, the projection of the Iwasawa cohomology class
{}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw}
to
W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}/\mathscr{F}^{1}W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}
is zero.

Proof.

This follows from the corresponding vanishing result in the fibre at a given
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, which is
[
LPSZ19
, Proposition 11.2.2]
.
∎

We can thus regard
\loc_{p}\left({}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw}\right)
as an element of the module

H^{1}_{\Iw}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\infty}}),{\mathscr{F}^{1}W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}}/{\mathscr{F}^{3}W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}}\right)\cong H^{1}_{\Iw}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\infty}}),\frac{\mathscr{F}^{1}W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}}{\mathscr{F}^{3}W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}}\otimes\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{-(\kappa_{U}+r_{2}+1)}\right)

where the isomorphism comes from the canonical twisting map (the twist is convenient because we land in a representation with constant Hodge–Tate weights, and also matches up better with our normalisation for analytic
p
-adic
L
-functions).
Perrin-Riou’s regulator
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{PR}}
gives a canonical map from this module to
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\mathop{\hat{\otimes}}\mathcal{D}^{\star}=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{W})\mathop{\hat{\otimes}}\mathcal{D}^{\star}
, where

\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}\left((\mathscr{F}_{3}W_{\underline{\Pi}}/\mathscr{F}_{1}W_{\underline{\Pi}})\otimes\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{(\kappa_{U}+r_{2}+1)}\right).

Let us now assume that the Hecke parameters of
\Pi=\Pi(0)
satisfy
\beta\neq\gamma
. After possibly shrinking
U
even further, we can arrange that
\beta_{n}\neq\gamma_{n}
for every
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, and that there is a rank 1 direct summand
\mathcal{D}_{\beta}
of
\mathcal{D}
, stable under
\varphi
, whose specialisation at any
n
is canonically identified with the
\varphi=\beta_{n}
eigenspace of
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi(n)}/\mathscr{F}_{1})
.

Definition 0.17.3.3
.

Let
\underline{\nu}
be a basis of the free rank 1
\mathcal{O}(U)
-module
\mathcal{D}_{\beta}
. We shall set

{}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\mathcal{L}^{\mot,r}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi})\coloneqq\left\langle\underline{\nu}_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{PR}}\left({}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw}\right)\right\rangle\in\mathcal{O}(U\times\mathcal{W}),

which we consider as a “two-variable motivic
p
-adic
L
-function”.

The dependence on
(c_{1},c_{2})
is mild: the element of
\operatorname{Frac}\mathcal{O}(U\times\mathcal{W})
given by

\mathcal{L}^{\mot,r}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi})\coloneqq\frac{{}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\mathcal{L}^{\mot,r}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi})}{\left(c_{1}^{2}-c_{1}^{(\mathbf{j}+1-r^{\prime})}\middle)\middle(c_{2}^{2}-c_{2}^{(\mathbf{j}+1-r)}\right)}

is independent of
c_{1},c_{2}
, where
\mathbf{j}
is the canonical character
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\to\mathcal{O}(W)^{\times}
(which we think of as a “coordinate” on
\mathcal{W}
) and
r^{\prime}=r_{1}-r_{2}-r
. This can be seen as a meromorphic function on
U\times\mathcal{W}
, with poles along the lines
\mathbf{j}=r+1
and
\mathbf{j}=r^{\prime}+1
.

Proposition 0.17.3.4
.

For
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, there exists a unique vector
\nu_{\beta}(n)\in\Fil^{1}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi(n)})
whose image in
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi(n)}/\mathscr{F}_{1})
coincides with the specialisation of
\underline{\nu}_{\beta}
at
n
. This vector is annihilated by
(1-\tfrac{\varphi}{\alpha_{n}})(1-\tfrac{\varphi}{\beta_{n}})
.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{F}_{1}W_{\Pi(n)}
has Hodge–Tate weight 0, the subspace
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathscr{F}_{1}W_{\Pi(n)})
of
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi(n)})
(which is simply the
\varphi=\alpha_{n}
eigenspace) has zero intersection with
\Fil^{1}
. Since
\Fil^{1}
is 3-dimensional, we conclude that it maps isomorphically to
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi(n)}/\mathscr{F}_{1})
; so the image of
\underline{\nu}_{\beta}
in
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W_{\Pi(n)}/\mathscr{F}_{1})
has a unique lifting to
\Fil^{1}
. On the other hand, since the specialisation of
\underline{\nu}_{\beta}
is annihilated by
(1-\tfrac{\varphi}{\beta_{n}})
, and
\mathscr{F}^{1}
is annihilated by
(1-\tfrac{\varphi}{\alpha_{n}})
, we see that this lifting must be annihilated by the given quadratic polynomial.
∎

Notation 0.17.3.5
.

We let
\Sigma_{\mathrm{crit}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{geom}}
denote the subsets of
U\times\mathcal{W}
given by

\Sigma_{\mathrm{crit}}=\{(n,j):n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0},j\in\mathbf{Z},0\leqslant j\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}\}

and

\Sigma_{\mathrm{geom}}=\{(n,j):n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0},j\in\mathbf{Z},-1-r_{2}\leqslant j\leqslant-1\}.

Proposition 0.17.3.6
.

For any
(n,j)\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{geom}}
, the value of
\mathcal{L}^{\mot,r}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi})
at
(n,j)
is given by

\mathcal{L}^{\mot,r}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},n,j)=\frac{(-1)^{r_{2}+n-q}}{(-2)^{q}(r_{2}+n-q)!}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{E}(\Pi(n),q)\mathcal{E}(\Pi(n),1+r_{2}+r)}{\left(1-\tfrac{p^{r_{2}+r+n+1}}{\beta_{n}}\middle)\middle(1-\tfrac{\gamma_{n}}{p^{r_{2}+r+n+2}}\right)}\cdot\left\langle\nu_{\beta}(n),\log_{\mathrm{BK}}\left(z^{[\Pi(n),q,r]}_{\mathrm{can}}\right)\right\rangle,

where
q=j+1+r_{2}+n
.

Proof.

This follows from the interpolation formulae relating the Perrin-Riou regulator to the Bloch–Kato logarithm. These formulae include a twist by
(1-p^{j}\varphi)(1-p^{-1-j}\varphi^{-1})^{-1}
, with
\varphi
acting as
p^{r_{2}+1+n}\beta_{n}^{-1}
; so we have

\mathcal{L}^{\mot,r}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},n,j)=\frac{(-1)^{r_{2}+n-q}}{(r_{2}+n-q)!}\cdot\frac{\left(1-\tfrac{p^{q}}{\beta_{n}}\right)}{\left(1-\frac{\beta_{n}}{p^{1+q}}\right)}\cdot\left\langle\nu_{\beta}(n),\log_{\mathrm{BK}}\left(\mom_{n,q}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw}(\Phi)\right)\right\rangle.

Combining this with
Theorem

0.17.2.2
gives the result.
∎

Note 0.17.3.7
.

The parity constraint of
Note

0.6.6.1
implies
\mathcal{L}^{\mot,r}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi})
is supported on
U\times\mathcal{W}^{(-1)^{r+1}}
.

Proposition 0.17.3.8
.

There exists an element
\mathcal{E}_{r}(\underline{\Pi})\in\mathcal{O}(U)
whose value at
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
is

\left(1-\tfrac{p^{r_{2}+r+n+1}}{\beta_{n}}\middle)\middle(1-\tfrac{\gamma_{n}}{p^{r_{2}+r+n+2}}\right).

Proof.

Clear from the fact that
p^{-n}\beta_{n}
and
p^{-n}\gamma_{n}
are analytic functions on
U
.
∎

Notation 0.17.3.9
.

We define
\mathcal{L}^{\mot,[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi})\coloneqq\mathcal{E}_{r}(\underline{\Pi})\cdot\mathcal{L}^{\mot,r}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi})\in\mathcal{O}(U\times\mathcal{W}^{(-1)^{r+1}})
.

Remark 0.17.3.10
.

This is an cowardly definition. We should really have defined a 3-parameter or even 4-parameter family of zeta elements with both
q
and
r
varying, and shown directly that it recovered the above element after specialisation, with the Euler factor
\mathcal{E}_{r}
arising naturally from a comparison between elements at Siegel and Iwahori level. See forthcoming work of Rockwood for a more satisfying treatment of this point.

We now reimpose the assumption that
\Pi
be Klingen-ordinary, and we suppose that
\beta
is the unique Hecke parameter of minimal possible valuation
r_{2}+1
. We can now compare the motivic
p
-adic
L
-function with the analytic
p
-adic
L
-function.

We can now re-state
Theorem

0.6.8.5
in the following form:

Corollary 0.17.3.11
.

For all
(n,j)\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{geom}}
, we have

\mathcal{L}^{\mot,[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},n,j)=\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\nu}(n)}(\Pi(n),j,r).

0.17.4.
Conjectures on Eichler–Shimura isomorphisms

Conjecture 0.17.4.1
.

Let
\underline{\Pi}
be a Siegel-type Hida family over
\mathcal{O}(U)
through
(r_{1},r_{2})
, which is also Borel-ordinary. Then:

(A)

There exists a rank 1 free
\mathcal{O}(U)
-module
H^{1}(\underline{\Pi})
, whose fibre at
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
is canonically identified with the direct summand of

H^{1}\left(Y_{G}(K_{1}(M,N)\cap\Iw(p)),\mathcal{N}_{n}^{2}(-D)\right)[\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}(n)]
which is ordinary for the Hecke operators

U_{2,\Iw}\coloneqq\left[\Iw(p)\diag(p^{2},p,p,1)\Iw(p)\right]\quad\text{and}\quad Z\coloneqq\left[\Iw(p)\diag(p,1,p,1)\Iw(p)\right].

(B)

There exist a pushforward map sending families of
p
-adic modular forms for
H
to elements of
H^{1}(\underline{\Pi})
, compatible via specialisation with the pushforward maps on classical modular forms.

(C)

There is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}(U)
-modules
\mathcal{D}_{\beta}^{\star}\cong H^{1}(\underline{\Pi})
, interpolating the comparison isomorphisms of
p
-adic Hodge theory.

A proof of part (A) of this conjecture has already been announced by Pilloni, and will appear in forthcoming work. Part (B), which is an analogue for Siegel-type families of the pushforwards constructed for Klingen-type families in
[
LPSZ19
]
, should also be accessible.

These two parts of the conjecture would suffice to define a 3-variable analytic
p
-adic
L
-function
\mathcal{L}_{\underline{\mu}}(\underline{\Pi})
on
U\times\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
, where
\underline{\mu}
is any basis of
H^{1}(\underline{\Pi})^{\star}
, whose restriction to
\{n\}\times\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
coincides with
\mathcal{L}_{\underline{\mu}(n)}(\Pi(n))
for each
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.

If part (C) holds, then we can arrange that
\underline{\mu}
is the image of
\underline{\nu}
. Then Corollary
0.17.3.11
would assert the equality of two analytic functions on
U\times\mathcal{W}
at every point
(n,q)
in a Zariski-dense set; hence these functions would agree everywhere. Specialising to
n=0
, we would then obtain the strongest possible form of an explicit reciprocity law, namely the following:

Conjecture 0.17.4.2
.

We have the following equality of rigid-analytic functions of
\mathbf{j}\in\mathcal{W}^{(-1)^{r+1}}
:

\mathcal{L}^{\mot,[r]}_{p,\nu}(\Pi,\mathbf{j})=\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi,\mathbf{j},r).

0.17.5.
Comparison with a
\GL_{4}
p-adic
L
-function

In order to work around our ignorance of Conjecture
0.17.4.1
, we shall make use of the functorial transfer to
\GL_{4}
. This allows one to make use of a somewhat different toolset (based on Betti rather than coherent cohomology).

Notation 0.17.5.1
.

We write
\Theta
for the functorial transfer of
\Pi\otimes\|\cdot\|^{-(r_{1}-r_{2}-1)/2}
to
\GL_{4}(\mathbf{A})
, so that
\Theta
is a isobaric automorphic representation of
\GL_{4}
satisfying

L(\Theta,s)=L(\Pi,\tfrac{1-r_{1}+r_{2}}{2}+s).

The choice of twist implies that the critical values of
L(\Theta,s)
are at the integers
0\leqslant s\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
, matching our normalisation for
p
-adic
L
-functions. Note that since
\Pi
is assumed to be non-CAP and non-endoscopic, the representation
\Theta
is in fact cuspidal. The compatibility of local and global transfers at
\infty
implies that
\Theta
is cohomological (with infinity-type determined by
(r_{1},r_{2})
); and the compatibility at finite places implies that
\Theta
has level 1, and is ordinary at
p
.

Definition 0.17.5.2
.

For each sign
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
, we write
H^{5}_{B,c}(\Theta)_{F}^{\epsilon}
for the eigenspace inside the compactly-supported Betti cohomology of the infinite-level symmetric space for
\GL_{4}
(with coefficients in the local system of
E
-vector spaces determined by
(r_{1},r_{2})
) which is
\Theta_{\mathrm{f}}
-isotypical for the
\GL_{4}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
action, and on which complex conjugation acts as
\epsilon
.

It follows from the Eichler–Shimura–Matsushima isomorphism, together with strong multiplicity one for
\GL_{4}
, that each of the two spaces
H^{5}_{B,c}(\Theta)_{E}^{\epsilon}
is isomorphic to a single copy of
\Theta_{\mathrm{f}}
. In particular, for each choice of
\epsilon
, the
\GL_{4}(\widehat{\mathbf{Z}})
-invariants of
H^{5}_{B,c}(\Theta)^{\epsilon}_{E}
are one-dimensional. We denote this space of invariants by
W^{\epsilon}(\Theta)_{E}
, and its base-extension to
L
by
W^{\epsilon}(\Theta)_{L}
.

Definition 0.17.5.3
.

We let
\tau=(\tau^{+},\tau^{-})
be a pair of
L
-bases of the spaces
W^{\epsilon}(\Theta)_{L}
, for each choice of sign.

Having chosen
\tau
, the construction of
[
DJR18
]
shows that for each sign
\epsilon
we can find constants
\Omega_{p}(\Theta,\tau^{\epsilon})\in L^{\times}/E^{\times}
, and
\Omega_{\infty}(\Theta,\tau^{\epsilon})\in\mathbf{C}^{\times}/E^{\times}
, such that the following proposition holds:

Proposition 0.17.5.4
.

There exists a measure
\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta)\in\Lambda_{L}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
such that for all
0\leqslant a\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
we have

\frac{\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta,a+\rho)}{\Omega_{p}(\Theta,\tau^{\epsilon})}=R_{p}(\Theta,\rho,a)\cdot\frac{\Lambda(\Theta\otimes\rho,a)}{\Omega_{\infty}(\Theta,\tau^{\epsilon})}

where
\epsilon=(-1)^{a}\rho(-1)
, and
R_{p}(\Theta,\rho,a)
is a product of Euler factors and Gauss sums at
p
.

Remark 0.17.5.5
.

As with the
\GSp_{4}

p
-adic
L
-function defined above, the quantity
\Omega_{p}(\Theta,\tau^{\epsilon})^{-1}\otimes\Omega_{\infty}(\Theta,\tau^{\epsilon})\in L\otimes_{E}\mathbf{C}
is uniquely determined by
\tau
, although the individual factors are only determined modulo
E^{\times}
, so the measure
\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta)
depends only on
\tau
.

By comparing the interpolating properties of the
p
-adic
L
-functions, we obtain the following:

Corollary 0.17.5.6
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi)
is not identically 0 (which is automatic if
r_{1}-r_{2}>0
). Then there is an isomorphism of
L
-vector spaces

t_{\Theta}:W^{+}(\Theta)_{L}\otimes W^{-}(\Theta)_{L}\cong\operatorname{Gr}^{1}\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(W_{\Pi})

with the following property: if
\nu
is the image of
\tau^{+}\otimes\tau^{-}
, then we have

\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu}(\Pi)(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})=\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta)(\mathbf{j}_{1})\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta)(\mathbf{j}_{2})

for all
(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{2})\in\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
with
\mathbf{j}_{1}+\mathbf{j}_{2}
odd.

Note that this isomorphism matches up the
E
-structure
W^{+}(\Theta)_{E}\otimes W^{-}(\Theta)_{E}
with the
E
-rational structure on the right-hand side determined by de Rham cohomology, although we shall not use this fact.

0.17.6.
Variation in families for
\GL_{4}

This discussion applies identically with
\Pi
replaced by any of the other specialisations
\Pi(n)
of Siegel-type family through
\Pi
discussed above, and we have the following statement:

Proposition 0.17.6.1
.

After possibly shrinking
U
, we can find free rank 1
\mathcal{O}(U)
modules
W(\underline{\Theta})^{\epsilon}
for each sign
\epsilon
, whose specialisation at
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
is canonically identified with
W(\Theta(n))_{L}^{\epsilon}
.

The following proposition is considerably deeper, but will be established in forthcoming work:

Theorem 0.17.6.2
.

Let
\underline{\tau}=(\underline{\tau}^{+},\underline{\tau}^{-})
be
\mathcal{O}(U)
-bases of the modules
W(\underline{\Theta})^{\epsilon}
. Then there exists a bounded rigid-analytic function

\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta}):U\times\mathcal{W}\to L

with the following property: for every
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, the restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta})
to
\{n\}\times\mathcal{W}
is
\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau(n)}(\Theta(n))
, where
\tau(n)
is the specialisation of
\underline{\tau}
at
n
.

The proof of this theorem will appear in forthcoming work of the present authors with Barrera, Dimitrov and Williams (or some subset of the above).

0.17.7.
The reciprocity law

We now carry out a rather delicate comparison argument. We choose a
\underline{\tau}
, giving us a 2-variable analytic
p
-adic
L
-function; and we choose a
\underline{\nu}
and a value of
r
, giving a 2-variable motivic one. For technical reasons we shall suppose that
r_{1}-r_{2}>0
, and take
r\in\{0,\dots,r_{1}-r_{2}\}
such that

r\neq\frac{r_{1}-r_{2}}{2}.

Notation 0.17.7.1
.

Define
\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta})\in\mathcal{O}(U\times\mathcal{W})
by

\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j})=\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},r)\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j})&\mathbf{j}\in\mathcal{W}^{(-1)^{r+1}},\\
0&\mathbf{j}\in\mathcal{W}^{(-1)^{r}}.\end{cases}

So Corollary
0.17.3.11
tells us that for all
(n,j)\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{geom}}
, we have

(0.17.1)

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{mot},[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},n,j)=B(n)\cdot\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},n,j),

where
B(n)\in L^{\times}
is the constant such that

B(n)t_{\Theta(n)}\left(\underline{\tau}(n)^{+}\otimes\underline{\tau}(n)^{-}\right)=\underline{\nu}(n).

Lemma 0.17.7.2
.

The function on
U\times\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
defined by

C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{j}^{\prime})\coloneqq\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j})\cdot\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{mot},[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j}^{\prime})-\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j}^{\prime})\cdot\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{mot},[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j}).

is identically zero.

Proof.

From Corollary
0.17.3.11
, we know that
C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{j}^{\prime})
vanishes at all triples
(n,j,j^{\prime})
such that both
(n,j)
and
(n,j^{\prime})
are in
\Sigma_{\mathrm{geom}}
. Such triples are clearly Zariski-dense, so the result follows.
∎

Proposition 0.17.7.3
.

There is a non-zero meromorphic function
D\in\operatorname{Frac}\mathcal{O}(U)
(independent of the
\mathcal{W}
variable) such that we have

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{mot},[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j})=D(\mathbf{u})\cdot\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j}).

Moreover,
D
has no pole at any
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.

Proof.

Let
s\in\{0,\dots,r_{1}-r_{2}\}
with
s\neq\tfrac{r_{1}-r_{2}}{2}
, and let
\rho
be a finite-order character of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, such that
(-1)^{s}\rho(-1)\neq(-1)^{r}
. (If
r_{1}-r_{2}
is
\geqslant 4
then we can assume
\rho
is trivial.) We shall substitute
\mathbf{j}^{\prime}=s+\rho
into the identity
C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{j}^{\prime})=0
. Unravelling the notations, we find that

\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},s+\rho)=\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},r)\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},s+\rho).

Both factors on the right-hand side are non-vanishing at
\mathbf{u}=n
for any
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, since they correspond to non-central critical values of the complex
L
-function, which are non-zero by the convergence of the Euler product. So this function is a non-zero-divisor in
\mathcal{O}(U)
; and dividing the identity
C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j},s+\rho)=0
by this function, we obtain

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{mot},[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j})=D(\mathbf{u})\cdot\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j}),\qquad D(\mathbf{u})\coloneqq\frac{\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{mot},[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},\mathbf{u},s+\rho)}{\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},r)\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},s+\rho)}.\qed

Proposition 0.17.7.4
.

For all but finitely many integers
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, the following holds: there exists an integer
j
with
j=r+1\bmod 2
such that
(n,j)\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{geom}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau(n)}(\Theta(n),j)\neq 0
.

Proof.

Assume the contrary. Then there exists an infinite sequence of integers
n_{k}\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
such that the function
\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta})
vanishes at
(n_{k},j)
for all
j
such that
j=r+1\bmod 2
and
(n,j)\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{geom}}
. In particular, if we fix a
j\leqslant-1
congruent to
r+1\bmod 2
, then
\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta})
vanishes at
(n_{k},j)
for all sufficiently large
k
, and since the sequence
(n_{k})
is Zariski-dense in
U
, it follows that
\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},u,j)
vanishes for all
u\in U
. Since this holds for all
j\leqslant-1
of the appropriate parity, we conclude that
\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta})
has to be identically 0 on
U\times\mathcal{W}^{(-1)^{1+r}}
. This is a contradiction, since its values at
(n,j+\rho)
with
0\leqslant j\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
and
\rho
a finite-order character are critical values of the complex
L
-function multiplied by explicit non-zero factors, and if
j\neq\tfrac{r_{1}-r_{2}}{2}
these values are not central or near-central, so they are non-zero by the convergence of the Euler product.
1
1

1

Note that since
\Pi
has tame level 1,
r_{1}-r_{2}
must be even, and since we have assumed it is not zero, it is
\geqslant 2
. If we allow general tame levels, then this argument becomes more delicate in the case
r_{1}-r_{2}=1
: we need to invoke the non-vanishing of
\GL_{4}

L
-functions along the abcissa of convergence (the “prime number theorem” for
\GL_{4}

L
-functions) due to Jacquet and Shalika.

∎

Corollary 0.17.7.5
.

For any
n\in U\cap\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, one of the following two possibilities occurs:

•

\mathcal{L}^{\mot,[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}(n)}(\Pi(n))
is a non-zero scalar multiple of the analytic
p
-adic
L
-function
\mathcal{L}_{\underline{\nu}(n)}(\Pi(n),-,r)
.

•

\mathcal{L}^{\mot,[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}(n)}(\Pi(n))
is identically 0.

Moreover, for all but finitely many
n
, the first possibility occurs and the scalar multiple is the constant
B(n)
of
Eq.

0.17.1
, so we have

\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu(n)}^{\mot,[r]}(\Pi(n),\mathbf{j})=\mathcal{L}_{p,\nu(n)}(\Pi(n),\mathbf{j},r)

as an identity of rigid-analytic functions of
\mathbf{j}\in\mathcal{W}^{(-1)^{r+1}}
.

Proof.

Since the function
D
of
Proposition

0.17.7.3
is finite at any positive integer
n
, it must either be zero there, or an element of
L^{\times}
, and the result of the proposition gives the two cases stated. However, if
n
satisfies the condition of
Proposition

0.17.7.4
, then
Eq.

0.17.1
shows that
D(n)
must equal the constant
B(n)
, and in particular is non-zero; and by that proposition we know that this case occurs for all but finitely many
n
.
∎

Remark 0.17.7.6
.

There are two “bad” cases which could possibly occur for some
n
: either
D(n)=0
, in which case the motivic
p
-adic
L
-function of
\Pi(n)
vanishes identically; or
D(n)\neq 0
but
B(n)\neq D(n)
, in which case the motivic
p
-adic
L
-function is still a non-zero multiple of the analytic one, but the “wrong” multiple. The first case is disastrous for applications, while the second is only a minor irritant. However, since both cases occur for only finitely many
n
, we can shrink
U
to assume that neither case occurs
except possibly for
n=0
.

We have so far been quite agnostic about the value of
r
; we assumed only that it was non-central. We now consider varying
r
. Note that the meromorphic function
D(\mathbf{u})
must be independent of
r
, since the constants
B(n)

are
independent of
r
. So we may conclude that the function

\frac{\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\nu}}^{\mot,[r]}(\underline{\Pi})(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j})}{\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},r)}

is also independent of
r
, being equal to
D(\mathbf{u})\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{j})
.

0.17.8.
Proof of Theorem B

We note the following theorem:

Theorem 0.17.8.1
.

There exists a collection of classes

{}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw,M}\in H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star})

for every
M\geqslant 1
coprime to
pc_{1}c_{2}
, satisfying the Euler system norm compatibility relations as
M
varies, with the
M=1
case being the class
{}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw}
above.

Proof.

This follows from the results of
[
LSZ17
]
in the same way as the
M=1
case covered in
Theorem

0.17.2.2
.
∎

Notation 0.17.8.2
.

We let
c_{M}
be the image of
{}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw,M}
under the Soulé twist map

H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star})\to H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}(-1-r_{2}-\kappa_{U})).

The following result follows easily from the integrality of the original Lemma–Flach classes:

Lemma 0.17.8.3
.

If
\mathcal{O}^{+}(U)
is the subring of functions of supremum norm
\leqslant 1
in
\mathcal{O}(U)
, then there exists a
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
-stable
\mathcal{O}^{+}(U)
-lattice
\mathcal{T}\subseteq W_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\star}(-1-r_{2}-\kappa_{U})
independent of
M
such that all these classes take values in
H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),\mathcal{T})
.

If
D(0)\neq 0
, then it is a small step from here to Theorem B. The chief difficulty is that we cannot rule out the possibility of
D(0)
vanishing, so we shall perform a delicate argument with “leading terms”.

Notation 0.17.8.4
.

Let
u
denote a generator of the principal ideal of
\mathcal{O}^{+}(U)
corresponding to the point
0\in U
.

Definition 0.17.8.5
.

For
M\geqslant 0
, let
h(M)
be the largest integer
n
such that

c_{M}\in u^{n}\cdot H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),\mathcal{T}),

and let
h=\inf_{M}h(M)
, where the infimum is over
M\geqslant 1
coprime to
pc_{1}c_{2}
.

The Euler system norm-compatibilities imply that
h(M)\leqslant h(1)
for all
M
, and
h(1)
is finite, since
{}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw,M}
is not zero. From
Proposition

0.17.7.3
, we have

h\leqslant h(1)\leqslant v_{u}\left(D\right)

where
v_{u}
denotes the
u
-adic valuation on
\mathcal{O}(U)
.

Proposition 0.17.8.6
.

There exists a collection of classes
c_{M}^{(h)}\in H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),\mathcal{T})
satisfying the Euler-system norm relations, such that we have

c_{M}=u^{h}\cdot c_{M}^{(h)}

for all
M
. Moreover, there is some
M
such that
c_{M}
has non-zero image in
H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),T)
, where
T
denotes the lattice
\mathcal{T}/u\mathcal{T}\subset W_{\Pi}^{\star}
.

Proof.

Let us write temporarily
\mathcal{M}=H^{1}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),\mathcal{T})
for some
M
. We note that
\mathcal{M}/u^{h}\mathcal{M}
injects into
H^{1}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),\mathcal{T}/u^{h}\mathcal{T})
, which is the Iwasawa cohomology of a finite-rank free
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-linear representation and is therefore
p
-torsion-free. Thus the fact that
{}_{c_{1},c_{2}}z^{[\underline{\Pi},r]}_{\Iw,M}
is divisible by
u^{h}
in
\mathcal{M}[1/p]
implies that it is in fact divisible by
u^{h}
in
\mathcal{M}
. Moreover, it is even
uniquely
divisible by
u^{h}
, since the
u^{h}
-torsion of
\mathcal{M}
is a subquotient of
H^{0}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),\mathcal{T}/u^{h}\mathcal{T})
which is zero by standard properties of Iwasawa cohomology. Hence
c_{M}^{(h)}
is well-defined. Since multiplication by
u^{h}
is injective, and the
c_{M}
for varying
M
satisfy the Euler-system norm relations, so do the
c_{M}^{(h)}
.

This argument also shows that
c_{M}^{(h)}
has non-zero image in
H^{1}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}}),T)
if and only if
h(M)=h
. Since this does occur for some
M
by the definition of
h
, the final claim follows.
∎

Proposition 0.17.8.7
.

Assume that
h<v_{u}(D)
. Then we have

\loc_{p}(c_{M}^{(h)}\bmod u)\in H^{1}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{Mp^{\infty}})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{p},\Fil^{2}T)

for all
M
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for every
M
, the class
c_{M}^{(h)}\bmod u
lies in the kernel of the Perrin-Riou regulator map for
\Fil^{1}T/\Fil^{2}
, since the kernel of this map is zero by
Lemma

0.6.2.5
.

Repeating the construction of the previous sections with the additional tame level
M
, we obtain an “equivariant” motivic
p
-adic
L
-function
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{mot},[r]}_{p,\underline{\nu}}(\underline{\Pi},M)
over
U\times\mathcal{W}
, taking values in the group ring of
(\mathbf{Z}/M\mathbf{Z})^{\times}
. For each character
\chi
of
(\mathbf{Z}/M\mathbf{Z})^{\times}
, the
\chi
-isotypical projection of this object interpolates values of the
L
-function of the twisted representation
\Pi(n)\otimes\chi
in the geometric range
\Sigma_{\mathrm{geom}}
.

On the other hand, the
\GL_{4}
construction extends straightforwardly to an equivariant version of the analytic
p
-adic
L
-function,
\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},M)
. Both of these objects depend on the same choices of periods
\underline{\nu}
,
\underline{\tau}
as the non-equivariant
L
-functions of the previous section.

Hence we can run the argument of Proposition
0.17.7.3
to obtain a relation between the motivic and analytic equivariant
p
-adic
L
-functions; and the function
D(\mathbf{u})
that appears must be the same for all
M
, since it is characterised by agreeing with the numbers
B(n)
of (
0.17.1
) for almost all
n
, and these numbers are independent of
M
.

From this and the definition of
c_{M}^{(h)}
, we have

\langle\nu_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{PR}}(c_{M}^{(h)}\bmod u)\rangle=\left((u^{-h}D)(0)\right)\cdot\mathcal{L}^{[r]}_{p,\underline{\tau}}(\underline{\Theta},M).

So if
(u^{-h}D)(0)=0
, we can conclude that
c_{M}^{(h)}\bmod u
lies in the kernel of the regulator for all
M
as required.
∎

Corollary 0.17.8.8
.

If the “big image” assumption
\operatorname{Hyp}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{p^{\infty}}),-)
of
[
Rub00
]
is satisfied for every Dirichlet-character twist of
T
, then we have
h=v_{u}(D)
.

Proof.

In the book
[
MR04
]
, the authors define a notion of
Euler characteristic
associated to a Galois representation and a collection of local conditions, and show that if the Euler characteristic is 0 (and the big-image condition holds), then no nonzero Kolyvagin systems exist.

In our setting, one computes easily that the Euler characteristic of the Greenberg-type local condition at
p
defined by
\Fil^{2}T
(with the usual unramified local conditions at all other primes) is 0. However, since
c_{M}^{(h)}
is non-zero for some
M
, its projection to some character component
\chi
must also be non-zero, so it gives a non-zero Kolyvagin system for
T(\chi)
, contradicting Mazur and Rubin’s result.
∎

Theorem 0.17.8.9

(
B
)
.

Let
\Pi
be an automorphic representation which satisfies our running hypotheses, and has tame level 1, is Borel-ordinary at
p
, and satisfies the “big image” condition of
[
LSZ17
, Assumption 11.1.2]
. Suppose also that
r_{1}-r_{2}\geqslant 6
.

Then for any choice of basis
\tau=(\tau^{+},\tau^{-})
as above, there exists an Euler system for
W_{\Pi}^{\star}(-1-r_{2})
with the following property: for all
M
, the localisation of the class at
p
lands in
\Fil^{1}
; and the image of the bottom class in this Euler system under the Perrin-Riou regulator is
\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta)
.

Proof.

The above argument shows that for each
r
we can construct an Euler system whose regulator is
(c_{1}^{2}-c_{1}^{\mathbf{j}+1-r^{\prime}})(c_{2}^{2}-c_{2}^{\mathbf{j}+1-r})\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta,r)\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta,\mathbf{j})
on
\mathcal{W}^{(-1)^{r+1}}
, and 0 on
\mathcal{W}^{(-1)^{r}}
.

Over the
-1
component of weight space, we note that the factors

(c_{1}^{2}-c_{1}^{\mathbf{j}+1-r^{\prime}})(c_{2}^{2}-c_{2}^{\mathbf{j}+1-r})\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta,r)

for
r=0
and
r=2
between them generate the unit ideal of
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{W}^{(-1)})
, so we can take a suitable linear combination to obtain an Euler system with the desired regulator
\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta,\mathbf{j})
. Similarly, over the other sign component, we use
r=1
and
r=3
, unless
r_{1}-r_{2}=6
, in which case we can use
r=1
and
r=5
.
∎

0.18.
Applications

Throughout this section, we let
\Pi
be a non-endoscopic, non-CAP automorphic representation of
G(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
of weights
(r_{1}+3,r_{2}+3)
with
r_{2}\geqslant 1
and
r_{1}-r_{2}\geqslant 6
. Assume that
\Pi
has tame level
1
, and that it is Borel ordinary at
p
.

0.18.1.
Selmer groups over
\mathbf{Q}_{\infty}

Let
\mathbf{Q}_{\infty}=\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})
. For simplicity we write
V=W_{\Pi}^{\star}(-1-r_{2})
in this section. (Note that this conflicts with our earlier use of
V
for an algebraic
G
-representation, but that usage will not recur here.)

Definition 0.18.1.1
.

Let
\widetilde{R\Gamma}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{\infty},V)
denote the Nekovář Selmer complex, with the unramified local conditions at
\ell\neq p
, and at
p
the Greenberg-type local condition determined by
\Fil^{2}W_{\Pi}^{\star}
.

This is a perfect complex of
\Lambda_{L}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
-modules. Its cohomology groups are zero for
i\notin\{1,2\}
, and we have

\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{\infty},V)=\ker\Big(H^{1}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{\infty},V)\to H^{1}_{\Iw}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{p,\infty},V/\Fil^{2}\right)\Big).

The degree 2 cohomology is related to classical
p
-torsion Selmer groups via Pontryagin duality:

Proposition 0.18.1.2
.

If
T
denotes a choice of lattice in
V
, and
(-)^{\vee}
denotes Pontryagin dual, then we have a canonical isomorphism of
\Lambda_{L}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
-modules

\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{\infty},V)=\left(\varprojlim_{n}H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{Q}(\mu_{p^{n}}),T^{\vee}(1+j))\right)^{\vee}(j)\otimes L,

for any integer
0\leqslant j\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
.

We can now state our main theorem in Iwasawa-theoretic form:

Theorem 0.18.1.3
.

The module
\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{\infty},V)
is torsion over
\Lambda_{L}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
and its characteristic ideal divides the
p
-adic
L
-function
\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta)
. Moreover, we have
\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{\infty},V)=0
.

Proof.

This is proved in Theorem 11.3.2 of
[
LSZ17
]
with the motivic
p
-adic
L
-function (for some specific choice of
r
) in place of
\mathcal{L}_{p,\tau}(\Theta)
. Applying the same argument with the Euler system emerging from
Theorem

0.17.8.9
we obtain the result stated.
∎

0.18.2.
Selmer groups over
\mathbf{Q}

By a standard descent argument (using the fact that no exceptional-zero phenomena arise because of
Lemma

0.6.2.5
), we deduce the following:

Theorem 0.18.2.1
.

Let
0\leqslant j\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
, and let
\rho
be a finite-order character of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. If
L(\Pi\otimes\rho,\tfrac{1-r_{1}+r_{2}}{2}+j)\neq 0
, then
H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{Q},V(-j-\rho))=0
.

This establishes the analytic rank 0 case of the Bloch–Kato conjecture for all critical values of the
L
-function of
\Pi
.

Note 0.18.2.2
.

The hypothesis
L(\Pi\otimes\rho,\tfrac{1-r_{1}+r_{2}}{2}+j)\neq 0
is automatic if
j\neq\tfrac{r_{1}-r_{2}}{2}
.

Appendices

0.19.
Uniqueness of local periods

The aim of this result is to prove the following somewhat delicate uniqueness result in smooth representation theory. This will be used to understand the dependence of our Euler system classes on the choice of test data at the bad primes, but the statement and its proof are purely local.

0.19.1.
Setup

Let
F
be a nonarchimedean local field and
|\cdot|
the norm on
F
, normalised such that
|\varpi|=\frac{1}{q}
, where
q
is the order of the residue field. Let
\psi
be a non-trivial additive character
F\to\mathbf{C}^{\times}
. We let
\pi
be a generic irreducible smooth representation of
G(F)
, and we let
\mathcal{W}(\pi)
be its Whittaker model with respect to
\psi
. We fix a pair of characters
(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})
of
F^{\times}
, with
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}=\chi_{\varpi}
; we shall assume these characters are
unitary
.

Zeta integrals

In
[
LPSZ19
, §8.2]
, we defined a local zeta-integral
Z(w,\Phi,s_{1},s_{2})
, for
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(F^{2}\times F^{2})
and
w\in\mathcal{W}(\pi)
. In Theorem 8.8
op.cit.
we showed (as a consequence of the computations of
[
RW17
,
RW18
]
) that the minimal common denominator of the values of this zeta-integral is given by
L(\pi\otimes\chi_{2},s_{1}-s_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2})L(\pi,s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})
. Accordingly, the quotient

(0.19.1)

\widetilde{Z}(w,\Phi,s_{1},s_{2})=\lim_{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\to(s_{1},s_{2})}\frac{Z(w,\Phi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}{L(\pi\otimes\chi_{2}^{-1},\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2})L(\pi,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})}

is a well-defined and non-zero map
\mathcal{W}(\pi)\times\mathcal{S}(F^{2}\times F^{2})\to\mathbf{C}
, for every
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbf{C}^{2}
. This map is
H(F)
-equivariant up to a twist by the norm character.

Principal series of
H

We consider the following families of induced representations of
H(F)
, depending on
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbf{C}^{2}
:

\displaystyle I_{s_{1},s_{2}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Ind(|\cdot|^{\tfrac{1}{2}-s_{1}}\chi_{1}^{-1},|\cdot|^{s_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}})\boxtimes\Ind(|\cdot|^{\tfrac{1}{2}-s_{2}}\chi_{2}^{-1},|\cdot|^{s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle I_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Ind(|\cdot|^{s_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}},|\cdot|^{\tfrac{1}{2}-s_{1}}\chi_{1}^{-1})\boxtimes\Ind(|\cdot|^{s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}},|\cdot|^{\tfrac{1}{2}-s_{2}}\chi_{2}^{-1})

where
\Ind
denotes normalised induction. We are interested in the behaviour of these at a point
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbf{C}^{2}
with
\Re(s_{i})\leqslant 0
. This condition implies that
I_{s_{1},s_{2}}
and
I^{\prime}_{s_{1},s_{2}}
are both irreducible unless
L(\chi_{1},2s_{1})L(\chi_{2},2s_{2})=\infty
. In this case
I_{s_{1},s_{2}}
is reducible but indecomposable, and its unique irreducible subrepresentation is generic; while
I^{\prime}_{s_{1},s_{2}}
has the same Jordan–Hölder factors in the opposite order, so its unique irreducible quotient is generic.

Siegel sections

We can define two maps from
\mathcal{S}(F^{2})
to smooth
\mathbf{C}(q^{s})
-valued functions on
\GL_{2}(F)
: the map
\Phi\mapsto f^{\Phi}(g,\chi,s)
of
[
LPSZ19
, §8.1]
; and the map
\Phi\mapsto F^{\Phi}(g,\chi,s)\coloneqq\chi^{-1}(\det g)\cdot f^{\widehat{\Phi}}(g,\chi^{-1},1-s)
, where
\hat{\Phi}(x,y)=\int_{F^{2}}\Phi(u,v)\psi(xv-yu)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v
is the Fourier transform. Both maps are
H(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-equivariant up to a twist by the norm character. Taking tensor products, we obtain maps from
\mathcal{S}(F^{2}\times F^{2})
to functions on
H(F)
, denoted
\Phi\to f^{\Phi}(h,s_{1},s_{2})
and
\Phi\to F^{\Phi}(h,s_{1},s_{2})
. One checks that for any
\Phi
, the function
f^{\Phi}
is a meromorphic section of
I_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{\prime}
, and
F^{\Phi}
is a meromorphic section of
I_{s_{1},s_{2}}
.

If
(s_{1},s_{2})
is not a reducibility point, then
F^{\Phi}
and
f^{\Phi}
are regular at
(s_{1},s_{2})
and define surjective maps from
\mathcal{S}(F^{2}\times F^{2})
to
I_{s_{1},s_{2}}
and
I^{\prime}_{s_{1},s_{2}}
respectively. If
(s_{1},s_{2})
is a reducibility point (still with
\operatorname{Re}(s_{i})\leqslant 0
as above), then
F^{\Phi}
is still regular and surjective onto
I_{s_{1},s_{2}}
, but
f^{\Phi}
can have poles; and one checks that the leading terms of the
f^{\Phi}
span the unique irreducible subrepresentation of
I^{\prime}_{s_{1},s_{2}}
.

We can now state the main theorems of this section:

Theorem 0.19.1.1
.

For any pair
(s_{1},s_{2})
with real parts
\leqslant 0
, the map
(w,\Phi)\mapsto\widetilde{Z}(w,\Phi,s_{1},s_{2})
factors through
\Phi\mapsto F^{\Phi}(-,s_{1},s_{2})\in I_{s_{1},s_{2}}
, and defines a non-zero element of the space

\Hom_{H(F)}\left(\pi\otimes I_{s_{1},s_{2}},\mathbf{C}\right).

Theorem 0.19.1.2
.

Suppose that one of the following conditions holds:

•

We have
L(\chi_{1},2s_{1})L(\chi_{2},2s_{2})\neq\infty
;

•

We have
L(\pi,s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})\neq\infty
;

•

\pi
is of Sally–Tadic type IIIa or IVa.

Then we have
\dim\Hom_{H(F)}\left(\pi\otimes I_{s_{1},s_{2}},\mathbf{C}\right)=1
, and
\widetilde{Z}
is a basis of this space. Moreover, for any non-zero
H
-subrepresentation
V
of
I_{s_{1},s_{2}}
, we have
\dim\Hom_{H(F)}\left(\pi\otimes V,\mathbf{C}\right)=1
spanned by the restriction of
\widetilde{Z}
.

0.19.2.
Proof of
Theorem

0.19.1.1

As in Theorem 8.8 of
[
LPSZ19
]
, renormalising Novodvorsky’s Whittaker integral by the factor
L(\pi\otimes\chi_{2}^{-1},s)^{-1}
and evaluating at
s=s_{1}-s_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}
gives a canonical nonzero intertwining map from the Whittaker model
\mathcal{W}(\pi)
to the Bessel model
\mathcal{B}(\pi)
, with respect to a specific character of the Bessel subgroup depending on the
\chi_{i}
and
s_{i}
.

If
B_{w}\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)
is the image of
w\in\mathcal{W}(\pi)
, and
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}
, we define

z_{w,\alpha}(h)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}^{\times}}B_{w}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
&x\\
&&1\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)h\right)|x|^{\alpha-2+s_{1}+s_{2}}\,d^{\times}x,

which is a meromorphic section of the 1-parameter family of representations
2
2

2

Note that this is a family of
H(F)
-representations over
\mathbf{C}(q^{\alpha})
, not just over
\mathbf{C}(q^{\alpha/2})
, since we can write it in the slightly less symmetric form
\Ind(|\cdot|^{\tfrac{1}{2}-s_{1}},|\cdot|^{s_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}+\alpha}\chi_{1})\boxtimes\Ind(|\cdot|^{\tfrac{1}{2}-s_{2}-\alpha},|\cdot|^{s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\chi_{2})
.

J_{(s_{1}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2},s_{2}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2})}^{\prime}
, where

J_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{\prime}\coloneqq\Ind(|\cdot|^{\tfrac{1}{2}-s_{1}},|\cdot|^{s_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\chi_{1})\boxtimes\Ind(|\cdot|^{\tfrac{1}{2}-s_{2}},|\cdot|^{s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\chi_{2})

is the dual of the representation
I_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{\prime}
of the previous section.

Proposition 0.19.2.1
.

We have

\widetilde{Z}(w,\Phi,s_{1},s_{2})=\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\tfrac{1}{L(\pi,\alpha+s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})}\left\langle z_{w,\alpha},f^{\Phi}(s_{1}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2},s_{2}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2})\right\rangle.

In particular, the limit on the right exists for all
(w,\Phi)
, and is non-zero for some
(w,\Phi)
.

Proof.

This is a restatement of
[
LPSZ19
, Proposition 8.4]
.
∎

Since the sections
f^{\Phi}
may have poles at
\alpha=0
, it is more convenient to work with the
F^{\Phi}
. Let
J_{s_{1},s_{2}}
denote the dual of
I_{s_{1},s_{2}}
, and let

M_{\alpha}:J_{(s_{1}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2},s_{2}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2})}\to J_{(s_{1}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2},s_{2}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2})}^{\prime}

denote the intertwining operator (normalised to be regular and nonzero at
\alpha=0
, although not necessarily an isomorphism there). Then
M_{\alpha}
is invertible for generic
\alpha
, and it follows from Propositions 3.1.5 and 3.2.3 of
[
LSZ17
]
that

\widetilde{Z}(w,\Phi,s_{1},s_{2})=C\cdot\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\tfrac{1}{L(\pi,\alpha+s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})}\left\langle M_{\alpha}^{-1}(z_{w,\alpha}),F^{\Phi}(s_{1}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2},s_{2}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2})\right\rangle

for
C
a nonzero scalar. Since the
F^{\Phi}
are regular at
(s_{1},s_{2})
and their specialisations there surject onto
I_{(s_{1},s_{2})}
, it follows that the limit

\tilde{z}_{w}\coloneqq\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\tfrac{1}{L(\pi,\alpha+s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})}M_{\alpha}^{-1}(z_{w,\alpha})\in J_{(s_{1},s_{2})}

must exist for every
w
, and we have

\widetilde{Z}(w,\Phi,s_{1},s_{2})=\langle\tilde{z}_{w},F^{\Phi}(s_{1},s_{2})\rangle.

This proves Theorem
0.19.1.1
.∎

0.19.3.
Proof of
Theorem

0.19.1.2

Let
J=J_{s_{1},s_{2}}=I_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{\vee}
. By duality, we can regard
\widetilde{Z}
as a non-zero
H(F)
-homomorphism
\pi\to J
. So we need to show that under the hypotheses of the theorem, if
K\subsetneqq J
is any
H(F)
-stable subspace, then the image of
\widetilde{Z}
in
J/K
is non-zero and spans
\Hom_{H(F)}(\pi,J/K)
.

Theorem 0.19.3.1

(Waldspurger, Emory–Takeda)
.

For any irreducible smooth representations
\pi
of
G(F)
and
\tau
of
H(F)
, we have

\dim\Hom_{H(F)}(\pi,\tau)\leqslant 1.

Proof.

This will be proved in forthcoming work of Emory and Takeda (building on the case of trivial central characters, which is due to Waldspurger
[
Wal12
]
).
∎

This proves Theorem
0.19.1.1
if
J
is irreducible, or equivalently, if
L(\chi_{1},2s_{1})L(\chi_{2},2s_{2})
is finite. The remaining cases are more intricate. There is a filtration of
J
by
H
-stable subspaces

J\supseteq J_{0}\supseteq J_{00},

where
J_{00}
is either zero or one-dimensional,
J/J_{0}
is irreducible and generic, and
J_{0}/J_{00}
is a direct sum of representations of the form “(generic)
\boxtimes
(1-dimensional)” or “(1-dimensional)
\boxtimes
(generic)”. By a theorem of Piatetski-Shapiro
[
PS97
, Theorem 4.3]
, we have
\Hom(\pi\otimes J_{00})=0
.

Proposition 0.19.3.2

(Rösner–Weissauer)
.

(i)

The choice of split Bessel model determines a factorisation

L(\pi,s)=L^{\sharp}(\pi,s)\cdot L^{\flat}(\pi,s)

as a product of two Euler factors, where
L^{\sharp}(\pi,\alpha+s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})
is the minimal common denominator of the functions
z_{w,\alpha}(1)
for
w\in\mathcal{W}(\pi)
, and
L^{\flat}(\pi,s)
(the “subregular factor”) lies in the fractional ideal generated by
L(\chi_{1},s_{1}-s_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}+s)L(\chi_{2},s_{2}-s_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}+s)
.

(ii)

If the subregular factor does not have a pole at
s=s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}
, then we have

\Hom_{H(F)}\left(\pi,J_{0}/J_{00}\right)=0.

(iii)

If
\pi
is of Sally-Tadic type IIIa or IVa, then the subregular factor is trivial.

Proof.

Part (i) is the factorisation of the
L
-factor considered in
[
RW17
]
, as the product of a factor arising from the asymptotics of the Bessel model
\mathcal{B}(\pi)
along the torus
\diag(x,x,1,1)
, and a second (the subregular factor) arising from the poles of the Siegel sections
f^{\Phi}
.

The subregular poles of generic representations are classified in
[
RW18
]
, where they are studied via certain auxiliary linear functionals on
\pi
(termed “
(H_{+},\tilde{\rho})
-functionals”) which transform by a scalar under one of the subgroups
(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}*&*\\
0&*\end{smallmatrix}\right),\star)
and
(\star,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}*&*\\
0&*\end{smallmatrix}\right))
of
H(F)
. These correspond, via Frobenius reciprocity, to elements of
\Hom_{H(F)}(\pi,J_{0}/J_{00})
in our notation.

It is shown in §3 of
op.cit.
that the leading term of the zeta integral at a subregular pole gives rise to an
(H_{+},\tilde{\rho})
-functional; and §4 of
op.cit.
shows that in fact all nonzero
(H_{+},\tilde{\rho})
-functionals arise in this way. So if
L^{\flat}(\pi,s)
does not have a pole at
s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}
, then
\Hom_{H(F)}(\pi,J_{0}/J_{00})=0
, which is (ii). The explicit classification of subregular poles also gives part (iii) (see Table 2 of
op.cit.
).
∎

We can now prove
Theorem

0.19.1.2
. If
s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}
is not a pole of
L(\pi,s)
, then it is certainly not a subregular pole. So every non-generic composition factor
\tau
of
J
satisfies
\Hom_{H(F)}(\pi,\tau)=0
. If
K
is a proper
H
-stable subspace of
J
, then we must have
K\subseteq J_{0}
, and it follows from the above that
\Hom(\pi,K)=\Hom(\pi,J_{0}/K)=0
; hence the natural maps

\Hom_{H(F)}(\pi,J)\to\Hom_{H(F)}(\pi,J/K)\to\Hom_{H(F)}(\pi,J/J_{0})

are both injective. However,
\widetilde{Z}
is a non-zero element of the first space, and (since
J/J_{0}
is irreducible)
Theorem

0.19.3.1
shows that the third space has dimension
\leqslant 1
. So we can conclude that for any choice of
K
, the middle space in this sequence is 1-dimensional and is spanned by the image of
\widetilde{Z}
. ∎

0.19.4.
Tempered representations

Proposition 0.19.4.1
.

If
\pi
is a generic and tempered irreducible representation of
G(F)
, then all poles of its
L
-factor have real parts in the set
\{0,-\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{3}{2}\}
; and the case
-\tfrac{3}{2}
occurs if and only if
\pi
is an unramified twist of the Steinberg representation of
G(F)
(hence of type IVa). In particular, if
\pi
is an unramified tempered representation, then all poles of its
L
-factor are purely imaginary.

Proof.

This is easily verified from Tables A.1 and A.8 of
[
RS07
]
, which list the
L
-factors for all non-supercuspidal representations and identify which are generic and/or tempered. (The tables do not include supercuspidal representations, but for such representations the
L
-factor is identically 1 so the claim is immediate.)
∎

Corollary 0.19.4.2
.

If
s_{1}=-\tfrac{t_{1}}{2}
,
s_{2}=-\tfrac{t_{2}}{2}
for integers
t_{1},t_{2}\geqslant 0
, and
\pi
is generic, then the hypotheses of Theorem
0.19.1.2
are automatically satisfied, except possibly if
\pi
is ramified and
(t_{1},t_{2})=(0,0)
.

0.19.5.
Adelic results

We now give a semi-local variant on the above results. Let
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}
be the common finite part of a pair
(\Pi^{H},\Pi^{W})
of automorphic representation of
G(\mathbf{A})
satisfying the running hypotheses of this paper, so in particular
\Pi_{\mathrm{f}}=\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\bigotimes_{\ell}}\Pi_{\ell}
with every
\Pi_{\ell}
being generic and tempered.

We shall apply the results of the previous sections to the
G(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
-representation
\Pi_{\ell}
for every
\ell
, taking
(s_{1},s_{2})=(-\tfrac{t_{1}}{2},-\tfrac{t_{2}}{2})
