in the notation of the main body of the paper. Tensoring together the corresponding local maps, we obtain a bilinear form

\widetilde{Z}=\prod_{\ell}\widetilde{Z}_{\ell}:\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}})\otimes\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2})\to\mathbf{C},

factoring via
\Phi\mapsto F_{\Phi}
to give an
H(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-equivariant map

\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}})\otimes I_{(s_{1},s_{2})}\to\mathbf{C},

where
I_{(s_{1},s_{2})}
is the product of the local representations above for each
\ell
. It follows readily from Theorem
0.19.1.2
that the space of such
H(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})
-equivariant homomorphisms is 1-dimensional, and
\widetilde{Z}
is a basis vector, as long as there is no prime
\ell
such that
L(\Pi_{\ell},s)
has a subregular pole at
s=s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}
.

Finally, we remark that if
(I_{(s_{1},s_{2})})_{(0)}
denotes the image in
I_{(s_{1},s_{2})}
of the functions vanishing along
(0,0)\times\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}
and
\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\times(0,0)
, then
(I_{(s_{1},s_{2})})_{(0)}
is a sum of subrepresentations of the form
I_{\ell_{1},0}\otimes I_{\ell_{2},0}\otimes\bigotimes_{\ell\notin\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}}I_{\ell}
, where
I_{\ell}
is the local representation at
\ell
, and
I_{\ell_{i},0}
are nonzero subrepresentations at some primes
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
. It now follows from the last assertion of Theorem
0.19.1.2
that

\Hom_{H(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{f}})}\left(\mathcal{W}(\Pi_{\mathrm{f}})\otimes(I_{(s_{1},s_{2})})_{(0)},\mathbf{C}\right)

is one-dimensional and spanned by (the restriction of)
\widetilde{Z}
.

Since we showed in sections 7 and 8 of
[
LSZ17
]
that the Lemma–Eisenstein map, restricted to Schwartz functions of character
\underline{\chi}
, factors via
\Phi\mapsto F^{\Phi}
, this proves
Theorem

0.6.6.2
.

0.20.
Explicit formulae at unramified primes

We now evaluate the linear functionals
\widetilde{Z}(w,\Phi,s_{1},s_{2})
of (
0.19.1
) explicitly, for some specific choices of the test data. We shall let the local field
F
be
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, and we shall take for
\pi
the local factor
\Pi_{p}
of a globally generic cuspidal automorphic representation at an unramified prime, as in
Section

0.6.2
.

As in that section,
r_{1}\geqslant r_{2}
are the weights of the algebraic representation for which
\Pi
is cohomological, and we write
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
for the Hecke parameters of
\pi^{\prime}=\pi\otimes|\cdot|^{-(r_{1}+r_{2})}
. The temperedness of
\pi
is thus equivalent to the condition that
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
all have complex absolute value
p^{(r_{1}+r_{2}+3)/2}
.

0.20.1.
Bases of eigenspaces at parahoric levels

Let
w^{\sph}
be the spherical Whittaker function of
\pi
, normalised such that
w^{\sph}(1)=1
(which is always possible). We are interested in describing Hecke operators which will map
w^{\sph}
to normalised generators of the eigenspaces at the various parahoric levels, where “normalised” again means these Whittaker functions take the value 1 at the identity.

We shall write
U_{1,\Sieg}
for the double coset operator
p^{(r_{1}+r_{2})/2}[\Sieg(p)\diag(p,p,1,1)\Sieg(p)]
on
\pi^{\Sieg(p)}
, and
U_{2,\Kl}
for
p^{r_{1}}[\Kl(p)\diag(p^{2},p,p,1)\Kl(p)]
acting on
\pi^{\Kl(p)}
; these correspond to the normalisations used in the main text for double coset operators on
\pi^{\prime}
, so as before the eigenvalues of
U_{1,\Sieg}
are
\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\}
and those of
U_{2,\Kl}
are
\{\tfrac{\alpha\beta}{p^{r_{2}+1}},\dots\}
.

Lemma 0.20.1.1
.

(1)

The normalised generator of the
U_{1,\Sieg}=\alpha
eigenspace of
\pi^{\Sieg(p)}
is given by

w^{\Sieg}_{\alpha}=\left(1-\frac{\beta}{U_{1,\Sieg}}\middle)\middle(1-\frac{\gamma}{U_{1,\Sieg}}\middle)\middle(1-\frac{\delta}{U_{1,\Sieg}}\right)w^{\sph}.

(2)

If
\alpha+\gamma\neq 0
, the normalised generator of the
U_{2,\Kl}=\tfrac{\alpha\beta}{p^{r_{2}+1}}
eigenspace of
\pi^{\Kl(p)}
is given by

w^{\Kl}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{(1+\tfrac{\gamma}{\alpha})}\left(1-\frac{\beta\gamma}{p^{r_{2}+1}U_{2,\Kl}}\middle)\middle(1-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{p^{r_{2}+1}U_{2,\Kl}}\middle)\middle(1-\frac{\beta\delta}{p^{r_{2}+1}U_{2,\Kl}}\right)w^{\sph}.

(3)

The normalised generator of the
(U_{1,\Iw}=\alpha,U_{2,\Iw}=\tfrac{\alpha\beta}{p^{r_{2}+1}})
eigenspace in
\pi^{\Iw(p)}
is given by

w^{\Iw}_{\alpha,\beta}=\left(1-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{p^{r_{2}+1}U_{2,\Iw}}\right)w^{\Sieg}_{\alpha}=\left(1-\frac{\beta}{U_{1,\Iw}}\right)w^{\Kl}_{\alpha\beta}.

In each case, it is obvious that the given vector lies in the relevant eigenspace, and the content of the lemma is that it takes the value 1 at the identity. This follows by tedious computations from the Casselman–Shalika formula giving the values of
w^{0}_{\sph}
on any diagonal element; an explicit form of this formula in the
\GSp_{4}
case can be found as Equation 7.3 in
[
RS07
]
.

Proposition 0.20.1.2
.

If
\alpha+\gamma\neq 0
, then the image of
w^{\Kl}_{\alpha\beta}
under the trace map
v\mapsto\sum_{\gamma\in G(\mathbf{Z}_{p})/\Kl(p)}\gamma\cdot v
is given by

\operatorname{Tr}\left(w^{\Kl}_{\alpha\beta}\right)=p^{3}\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{p\beta}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p\alpha}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p\beta}\right)w^{\sph}.

Proof.

This follows from an extremely tedious explicit computation; rather than the Whittaker model, one fixes an ordering of the Hecke parameters, giving a choice of model of
\pi
as an induction from the Borel subgroup. The Klingen-invariants then have an explicit basis given by coset representatives for
B(\mathbf{Z}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbf{Z}_{p})/\Kl(p)
. A lengthy double-coset computation gives the matrix of
U_{2,\Kl}
in this basis; and the trace map in this basis is explicit, so the result follows from a routine computation.
∎

Remark 0.20.1.3
.

A formula for
\operatorname{Tr}\left(w^{\Kl}_{\alpha\beta}\right)
is stated without proof in
[
GT05
]
. However, their formula differs from ours, having terms of the form
\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{\beta}\right)
rather than
\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{p\beta}\right)
. We believe the formula stated above to be the correct one.

To link up with the zeta-integral computations of
[
LPSZ19
]
we also needed to consider eigenvectors for the “transpose” Hecke operator
U_{2,\Kl}^{\prime}=p^{-r_{2}}\diag(1,p,p,p^{2})
. Given the above computations, it seems natural to consider the vector

(0.20.1)

w^{\Kl\prime}_{\alpha\beta}\coloneqq\frac{1}{(1+\tfrac{\gamma}{\alpha})}\left(1-\frac{\beta\gamma}{p^{r_{2}+1}U^{\prime}_{2,\Kl}}\middle)\middle(1-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{p^{r_{2}+1}U^{\prime}_{2,\Kl}}\middle)\middle(1-\frac{\beta\delta}{p^{r_{2}+1}U^{\prime}_{2,\Kl}}\right)w^{\sph}.

By dualizing the previous computation, we see that
\operatorname{Tr}\left(w^{\Kl,\prime}_{\alpha\beta}\right)=\operatorname{Tr}\left(w^{\Kl}_{\alpha\beta}\right)
.

We briefly summarize how this relates to the computations of
op.cit.
. Let
w=r_{1}+r_{2}+3
, and let
\Lambda
be the unramified character of
T(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
given by
\chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}\rtimes\rho
in the notation of
[
RS07
]
§2.2, where
\rho(p)=p^{-w/2}\alpha
,
\chi_{1}(p)=\gamma/\alpha
,
\chi_{2}(p)=\beta/\alpha
. Then
\Ind_{B(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}^{G(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}(\Lambda)
gives an explicit model of
\pi
; and identifying the Klingen Levi
M_{\Kl}
with
\GL_{2}\times\GL_{1}
as in
[
LPSZ19
, §8.4]
, we can thefore write
\pi=\Ind_{P_{\Kl}}^{G}(\tau\boxtimes\theta)
, where
\theta=\chi_{1}
and
\tau
is the unramified principal series
\rho\chi_{2}\times\rho
of
\GL_{2}
.

In
op.cit.
we considered the diagram of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(\tau)}

Here the horizontal arrows are the canonical intertwining maps from the induced representations to their Whittaker models, and the vertical arrow is given by restriction of functions in the induced representation from
G(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
to
\GL_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})\subset M_{\Kl}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
(note that this is only
\GL_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
-equivariant up to a twist by a power of
|\det|
).

In
op.cit.
we considered a vector
\phi_{1}\in\pi^{\Kl(p)}
, characterized by the property of being supported on
B(\mathbf{Q}_{p})\cdot\Kl(p)
and taking the value
p^{3}
at the identity. This maps to
p^{3}\xi\in\tau
, where
\xi
is the spherical function of
\tau
satisfying
\xi(1)=1
; and via the Casselman–Shalika formula, we have
W_{\xi}(1)=\left(1-\frac{\beta}{p\alpha}\right)
.

Lemma 0.20.1.4
.

The image of
\phi_{1}
in
\mathcal{W}(\pi)
is
(1-\tfrac{\beta}{p\alpha})w^{\Kl\prime}_{\alpha\beta}
.

Proof.

Since both vectors are
U_{2,\Kl}^{\prime}
-eigenvectors with the same eigenvalue, it suffices to check that they both have the same trace down to spherical level. By construction
\phi_{1}
has trace
p^{3}\phi_{\mathrm{sph}}
where
\phi_{\mathrm{sph}}
is the normalised spherical function of
\phi
; and, by the Casselman–Shalika formula for
\GSp_{4}
, the image of
p^{3}\phi_{\mathrm{sph}}
in
\mathcal{W}(\pi)
is
p^{3}\left(1-\frac{\beta}{p\alpha}\right)\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{p\beta}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p\alpha}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p\beta}\right)w_{\mathrm{sph}}
. This agrees with the formula we have computed above for
\operatorname{Tr}\left(w^{\Kl,\prime}_{\alpha\beta}\right)
.
∎

Remark 0.20.1.5
.

So the “correct” basis of the ordinary eigenspace for
U_{2,\Kl}^{\prime}
at level
\Kl(p)
is clearly
w^{\Kl\prime}_{\alpha\beta}
, which has the effect of renormalising the
\GL_{2}
Whittaker function
W_{\xi}(1)
to take the value 1 at the identity.

0.20.2.
Particular values of the zeta integral

Let us now choose characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
such that
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}=\chi_{\Pi}
; we shall suppose (for now) that the
\chi_{i}
are unramified. We shall also choose integers
(q,r)
with
0\leqslant q\leqslant r_{2}
and
0\leqslant r\leqslant r_{1}-r_{2}
, and consider the zeta integral at
(s_{1},s_{2})=(-\tfrac{t_{1}}{2},-\tfrac{t_{2}}{2})
, where
(t_{1},t_{2})=(r_{1}-q-r,r_{2}-q+r)
.

Note 0.20.2.1
.

We then have
L(\Pi,s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})=L(\Pi,\tfrac{-(r_{1}+r_{2}+3)}{2}+q+1)=\frac{1}{\left(1-\tfrac{\alpha}{p^{q+1}}\right)\dots\left(1-\tfrac{\delta}{p^{q+1}}\right)}
, and similarly
L(\Pi,s_{1}-s_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2})=L(\Pi,\tfrac{-(r_{1}+r_{2}+3)}{2}+r+r_{2}+2)=\frac{1}{\left(1-\tfrac{\alpha}{p^{r+r_{2}+2}}\right)\dots\left(1-\tfrac{\delta}{p^{r+r_{2}+2}}\right)}
.

Spherical test data

We let
\Phi^{\mathrm{sph}}=\mathrm{ch}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{2})
, and we let
w^{\mathrm{sph}}
be the normalised spherical Whittaker function as above. Then, as we have already noted, we have

\widetilde{Z}(w^{\mathrm{sph}},\Phi^{\sph})=1.

Siegel parahoric test data

Recall that
\Sieg(p)
denotes the Siegel parahoric modulo
p
. We choose a Hecke parameter
\alpha
and consider the vector
w^{\Sieg}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi)^{\Sieg(p)}
of
Lemma

0.20.1.1
; and we let
\Phi^{\Sieg}=\mathrm{ch}\left((p\mathbf{Z}_{p}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2}\right)
.

Proposition 0.20.2.2
.

We have

\widetilde{Z}(w^{\mathrm{\Sieg}}_{\alpha},\Phi^{\Sieg})=\frac{1}{(p+1)^{2}}\left(1-\frac{\beta}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p^{r_{2}+2+r}\chi_{2}(p)}\right)\left(1-\frac{\chi_{2}(p)p^{r_{2}+1+r}}{\alpha}\right).

Proof.

We use the Bessel-model description of
\widetilde{Z}(w,\Phi,s_{1},s_{2})
given in
Proposition

0.19.2.1
. Note that the choice of Bessel model used depends on the value of
r
(but is independent of
q
). The Schwartz function
\Phi^{\Sieg}
is chosen so that
f^{\Phi^{\Sieg}}
is supported on the coset
B_{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})\cdot\Iw_{H}
, where
\Iw_{H}=\Sieg(p)\cap H(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
is the upper-triangular Iwahori subgroup of
H
, and its value at the identity is 1. So for any
\Sieg(p)
-invariant (or just
\Iw_{H}
-invariant)
w
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}(w,\Phi^{\Sieg})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\xi\to 0}\frac{1}{L(\pi,s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}+\xi)}\int_{B_{H}\backslash H}z_{w,\xi}(h)f^{\Phi}(h,s_{1}+\tfrac{\xi}{2},s_{2}+\tfrac{\xi}{2})\ \mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{w,0}(1)}{(p+1)^{2}L(\pi,s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})}.

(Note that the denominator is finite, since
\pi
is tempered and
\Re(s_{i})\leqslant 0
.) Since
w
is by assumption
\Sieg(p)
-invariant, we have
z_{w,0}(1)=F_{w}(p^{-1-q})
, where
F_{w}(X)
is the rational function

F_{w}(X)=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}}p^{n(r_{1}+r_{2}+6)/2}B_{w}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}\\
&p^{n}\\
&&1\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)X^{n}.

It is easy to see that for any
\Sieg(p)
-invariant
w
we have

F_{w}(X)=F_{w}(0)+XF_{U\cdot w}(X),

so in particular
F_{w_{\alpha}^{\Sieg}}(X)
is a constant multiple of
1/(1-\alpha X)
. We can determine the constant by comparing with the spherical Whittaker vector
w^{\sph}
: by Proposition 3.5.6(b) of
[
LSZ17
]
, we have

F_{w^{\sph}}(X)=\frac{\left(1-\chi_{1}(p)p^{r_{1}+1-r}X\right)\left(1-\chi_{2}(p)p^{r_{2}+1+r}X\right)}{(1-\alpha X)(1-\beta X)(1-\gamma X)(1-\delta X)},

and an explicit computation shows that we have

F_{w^{\Sieg}_{\alpha}}(X)=\frac{\left(1-\tfrac{\chi_{1}(p)p^{r_{1}+1-r}}{\alpha}\right)\left(1-\frac{\chi_{2}(p)p^{r_{2}+1+r}}{\alpha}\right)}{(1-\alpha X)}.

Substituting this into the formula
\widetilde{Z}(w_{\alpha}^{\Sieg},\Phi^{\Sieg})=\frac{1}{(p+1)^{2}}L(\Pi_{p},s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{-1}\cdot F_{w^{\Sieg}_{\alpha}}\left(p^{-1-q}\right)
gives the result.
∎

Klingen test data: the general formula

We now consider the case of test vectors of Klingen parahoric level. This computation is largely worked out in
[
LPSZ19
]
§8.4, but without making explicit the normalisation of the vector
w\in\mathcal{W}(\Pi)
used, so we shall tease out this detail.

We shall assume that our Hecke parameters are ordered as
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
with the common ratio
\gamma/\alpha=\delta/\beta
not equal
3
3

3

This will be automatically satisfied if
\Pi
is Klingen-ordinary at
p
, since this ratio then has
p
-adic valuation
r_{2}+2>0
.
to
-1
, so that the vector
w^{\Kl\prime}_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi)^{\Kl(p)}
of (
0.20.1
) is defined. As Proposition 5.6 of
[
LPSZ19
]
we can extend this to a compatible collection of vectors
w^{\Kl\prime}_{n}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi)^{\Kl(p^{n})}
for
n\geqslant 1
, all of them eigenvectors for the operators
U_{2,\Kl}^{\prime}
with eigenvalue
\alpha\beta/p^{r_{2}+1}
, and satisfying

w^{\Kl,\prime}_{n}=\frac{1}{p^{3}}\sum_{\Kl(p^{n})/\Kl(p^{n+1})}\gamma\cdot w^{\Kl\prime}_{n+1}.

We define the following Euler factor:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\pi,m)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(1-\tfrac{p^{m}}{\alpha}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{p^{m}}{\beta}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\gamma}{p^{m+1}}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{\delta}{p^{m+1}}\right).

We write
w^{\mathrm{sph}}_{\tau}
for the spherical Whittaker function of the
\GL_{2}
representation
\tau
, normalised so that
w^{\mathrm{sph}}_{\tau}(1)=1
; the values of this function along the maximal torus are given by

w^{\mathrm{sph}}_{\tau}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=p^{-n(r_{1}+r_{2}+4)/2}\left(\alpha^{n}+\alpha^{n-1}\beta+\dots+\beta^{n}\right).

Proposition 0.20.2.3
.

Let
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
be Schwartz functions on
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{2}
, with
\Phi_{1}
satisfying
\Phi_{1}^{\prime}(0,0)=0
, where
\Phi_{1}^{\prime}
is the partial Fourier transform in the second variable only. Then for all
m\gg 0
we have

\widetilde{Z}(\gamma\cdot w^{\Kl,\prime}_{m},\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2})=\\
\mathcal{E}(\pi,q)\mathcal{E}(\pi\times\chi_{2}^{-1},r_{2}+1+r)\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}}w^{\sph}_{\tau}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right))W^{\Phi_{1}}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\tfrac{-(r_{1}-q-r)}{2})W^{\Phi_{2}}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\tfrac{-(r_{2}-q+r)}{2})\tfrac{\theta(x)}{|x|}\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}x.

Remark 0.20.2.4
.

Here we are assuming that the integrand has no pole at the relevant value of
(s_{1},s_{2})
, which can only happen if
r=(r_{1}-r_{2}+1)/2
and
\mathcal{E}(\pi\times\chi_{2}^{-1},r_{2}+1+r)
vanishes.

Proof.

This is a special case of Proposition 8.14 of
[
LPSZ19
]
. Since we are assuming
\pi
and the
\chi_{i}
to be unramified, the epsilon-factor term in
op.cit.
is 1; and the ratio of
L
-factors gives the two
\mathcal{E}(\pi,-)
terms. Moreover, as shown above, our renormalisation of the Klingen test vectors is precisely the one which scales the (non-normalised)
\GL_{2}
Whittaker function
W_{\xi}
of
op.cit.
to its normalised equivalent
w_{\tau}^{\mathrm{sph}}
.
∎

Particular cases

We define the following Schwartz functions on
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{2}
:

•

\Phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{dep}}=\mathrm{ch}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
,

•

\Phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{crit}}=\mathrm{ch}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
,

These will correspond to holomorphic Eisenstein series that are respectively
p
-depleted, ordinary, or critical-slope (hence the notation). We let
\Phi_{?}
denote the preimage of
\Phi^{\prime}_{?}
under the inverse Fourier transform (in the second variable only); these are a little messy to write down explicitly.

Then we have the following formulae, assuming
n\geqslant 0
and
\chi
unramified:

•

W^{\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}}}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),s\right)=
1 if
n=0
, and zero otherwise.

•

W^{\Phi_{\mathrm{crit}}}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),s\right)=p^{-ns}
,

Accordingly, for
m\gg 0
the integral of Proposition
0.20.2.3
is given by

\frac{\widetilde{Z}(\gamma\cdot w^{\Kl,\prime}_{m},\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2})}{\mathcal{E}(\pi,q)\mathcal{E}(\pi\times\chi_{2}^{-1},r_{2}+1+r)}=\begin{cases}1,\quad\text{if $(\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})=(\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}},\Phi_{\mathrm{crit}})$, $(\Phi_{\crit},\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}})$ or $(\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}},\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}})$;}\\[5.69054pt]
\left[\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{p^{1+q}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p^{1+q}}\right)\right]^{-1},\quad\text{if $(\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})=(\Phi_{\crit},\Phi_{\crit}).$}\end{cases}

Remark 0.20.2.5
.

The case of
(\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}},\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}})
appears already in the computation of the interpolating property of the
p
-adic
L
-function in
[
LPSZ19
]
. The case of
(\Phi_{\mathrm{dep}},\Phi_{\mathrm{crit}})
will appear in our formula for the syntomic regulator; and the case of
(\Phi_{\crit},\Phi_{\crit})
plays a somewhat different role – it will be used to relate the étale class we want to study at prime-to-
p
level to an auxiliary étale class at
\Kl(p)
level which is easier to study.

0.20.3.
Twisted zeta integrals

For the purposes of
Sections

0.16.3
and
0.16.4
we will need to consider a “twisted” form of the above integrals. We let
\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\rho
be smooth characters of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
with
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}=\rho^{2}\chi_{\Pi}
, and consider the slightly more general integral

Z(w,\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2},s_{1},s_{2};\underline{\chi},\rho)\coloneqq\int_{(Z_{G}N_{H}\backslash H)(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}w(h)f^{\Phi_{1}}(h_{1};\chi_{1},s_{1})f^{\Phi_{2}}(h_{2};\chi_{2},s_{2})\rho(\det h)\ \mathrm{d}h.

This is, of course, an instance of the
\GSp_{4}
zeta-integral of
[
LPSZ19
, Definition 8.3]
with
\pi
replaced by
\pi\times\rho
; but we want to focus on the case where
\pi
is unramified (as above) but
\rho
and the
\chi_{i}
are not, so it is helpful to also consider it as an instance of the
\GSp_{4}\times\GL_{2}
zeta-integral of
op.cit.
for
\pi\times\sigma
, where
\sigma
is taken to be the representation

\sigma\coloneqq I(|\cdot|^{s_{2}-1/2}\rho,|\cdot|^{1/2-s_{2}}\rho\chi_{2}^{-1})

(subject to an appropriate definition of the Whittaker model of
\sigma
in the reducible case, as in the footnote to Proposition 8.14 of
op.cit.
). The first interpretation shows that the fractional ideal generated by the values of the renormalised zeta-integral

\widetilde{Z}(\dots)=\frac{Z(\dots)}{L(\pi\times\rho,s_{1}+s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})L(\pi\times\rho\chi_{2}^{-1},s_{1}-s_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2})}

is the unit ideal of
\mathbf{C}[\ell^{\pm s_{1}},\ell^{\pm s_{2}}]
. The second interpretation shows that if
\Phi_{1}^{\prime}(0,0)=0
, then for
m\gg 0
we have the special-value formula

\widetilde{Z}(\gamma\cdot w^{\Kl,\prime}_{m},\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2},s_{1},s_{2};\underline{\chi},\rho)=\frac{1}{L(\tau\times\sigma\times\theta,s_{1})L(\tau^{\vee}\times\sigma^{\vee},1-s_{1})\epsilon(\tau\times\sigma,s_{1})}\\
\times\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}}w^{\sph}_{\tau}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right))W^{\Phi_{1}}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right);\chi_{1},s_{1})W^{\Phi_{2}}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right);\chi_{2},s_{2})\tfrac{\theta(x)\rho(x)}{|x|}\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}x.

In this more general setting, the test functions we shall use are of the form

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{dep},\mu,\nu}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\cdot\mu(x)\nu(y),

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{crit},\nu}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\cdot\nu(y),

\displaystyle\langle p\rangle^{-1}\varphi\cdot\Phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{crit},\nu}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}(p\mathbf{Z}_{p}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\cdot\nu(y).

for finite-order characters
\mu,\nu
, with
\chi|_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}=\mu^{-1}\nu
(taking
\mu
to be trivial in the case of
\Phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{crit},\nu}
, so this condition becomes simply
\chi|_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}=\nu
). Note that
\Phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{dep},\mu,\nu}(x,y)
is the same function considered in
[
LPSZ19
, Definition 7.5]
.

We have

W^{\Phi_{\mathrm{dep},\mu,\nu}}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right);\chi,s)=\begin{cases}\mu(-x)\nu(-1)&\text{if $x\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and

W^{\Phi_{\mathrm{crit},\nu}}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right);\chi,s)=\begin{cases}|x|^{s}\nu(-1)&\text{if $x\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Thus, for test data
\Phi_{\mathrm{dep},\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{dep},\mu_{2},\nu_{2}}
, assuming
\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}=1
and
\rho=\nu_{1}\nu_{2}
, the torus Whittaker integral is simply the integral of the constant function 1 over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, so it is 1. Similarly, for test data of the form
\Phi_{\mathrm{dep},\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}\times\Phi_{\crit,\nu_{2}}
we again obtain that the integral is 1. This equality of zeta integrals for “
\mathrm{dep}\times\mathrm{dep}
” and “
\mathrm{dep}\times\crit
” data is the input needed in §
0.16.3
.

On the other hand, the Whittaker function of
\langle p\rangle^{-1}\varphi\Phi_{\mathrm{crit},\nu}
is zero at
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
unless
x\in p\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, so if we consider test data of the form
\langle p\rangle^{-1}\varphi\cdot\Phi_{\mathrm{\crit},\nu_{1}}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{dep},\mu_{2},\nu_{2}}
, then we are integrating the product of a function supported on
p\mathbf{Z}_{p}
and another supported on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Hence the zeta integral is 0, as required for §
0.16.4
.
